# Kurz language design

Status: brainstorm record, 2026-10-01 to 2026-10-30. Everything under "Decided" was chosen by the owner during the session. Anything marked *assumed* was proposed and not objected to, but never explicitly confirmed. A marker that carries the date of a review names a reading proposed after that review; it stands as assumed until the owner confirms or refuses it by number in a round. A marker *(proposed: ...)* inside a decided statement names a reading that the owner's answer did not reach; it is no decision, and a design round asks about it by the id of the reference rule that carries it. On 2026-10-02 the owner accepted, as one batch, the details the language reference had filled in to write its cases: the six that the question named are recorded as decided, the rest as *assumed*. Keyword spellings in code samples are illustrative unless listed in the syntax section.

## 1. Identity and goals

- Name: **Kurz**. Source files: `.kz`.
- Syntax in the spirit of C#.
- Main selling point: do as much as possible with as little code as possible.
- General purpose. Primary target: highly scalable, horizontally scalable backend systems.
- Games are a target, but the language gets no game-specific features.
- Should run on microcontrollers (Arduino, ESP32 class) without being designed for them. Consequence: the runtime is pay-per-use. A program that uses no actors links no scheduler.
- Compiled to native code, as fast as C, optimized for each target platform.
- A mechanism of Kurz is designed by first taking how Rust does it, and putting the developer experience and the distributed idea on top of that (the owner, 2026-10-20, round 16, in the owner's words: "i want to do it like rust/c for maximum performance, always when implementing smth for kurz check mainly how rust does it then apply our heavy developer experience and distributed idea on top of it"; against a mechanism chosen for the developer experience first; the cost is that a feature of the developer experience is paid for in the forms Rust pays it in and not by work on the hot path: the line of a crash and a trace cost the walk and the reads at the crash, as Rust pays for a backtrace when it is asked for (checked 2026-10-20: the Rust standard library's documentation of `std::backtrace`, which captures a backtrace at the point it is asked for), and the executable's size in every build, its unwind tables, its symbol names and the file and the line each raising site carries, while a release build keeps the line tables only under an option of the build, without which a stack overflow and a primitive's raise name no line there, and on Windows the tables and the names are a second file to ship beside the executable, without which a crash names no line (sections 2 and 6)) *(assumed: that a departure from Rust's way is a question to the owner, as the reading of the owner's "always"; that C counts where Rust follows C, for the performance of both, as the reading of the owner's "rust/c" and "for maximum performance"; that the owner's "distributed idea" is the model of section 6 (decided there, with the assumptions its markers name) and section 7 (decided there); and the store of the line before every call of round 15, which section 6 (decided there) drops for the line tables, as an instance of the mechanism the principle is set against, which the record names and the owner did not; each proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*.
- The developer experience and the meaning of a program come from C#, made better where C# is weak; the performance and the memory safety come from Rust; and the distributed model comes from Elixir (the owner, 2026-10-29, in the owner's words: "generally the language will be: developer experience and semantics from c# but even better, performance memory safte and all that from rust, and elixir distributed stuff"). *(assumed: that it sharpens the line above, so that a mechanism starts from Rust's and what a program means starts from C#'s, the reading of the owner's "semantics from c#" beside round 16's "check mainly how rust does it", proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*
- Memory safety is proven by the compiler, not found by best-effort analysis. *(assumed)*
- The size of the language is counted, not capped: a gate prints the count of the core words, of the reference's rules and of its error ids on every change, and each question of a design round says what a new rule adds (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Printed counts", against no measure, at the cost that growth is seen only by reading, and against a budget of rules, at the cost that a good rule can be refused for the count; the cost is a small tool and one line per question). The reference's lint prints the count of its rules and of its error ids on every run; no tool prints the count of the core words, and the questions of a design round carry no line on what a new rule adds: that part is not built.

## 2. Toolchain

- The compiler emits LLVM IR directly. Going through C source is not wanted. LLVM as a build-time dependency is acceptable.
- The emitted IR is compiled and linked by the `clang` of an LLVM release that the machine or the image that builds has: the compiler accepts one major release of LLVM, the one it pins, and tries `clang-<major>` for that major, where the distribution installs such names, then `clang`, where the installer gives that name alone, as the LLVM installer on Windows does (checked 2026-10-15 in the `bin` directory that installer fills: `clang.exe`, and no `clang-<major>.exe`), both on the path, and on Windows then `clang.exe` in that directory, `%ProgramFiles%\LLVM\bin`, for a machine on which the installer did not add that directory to the path (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 20, "Path + installer dir", as Rust's compiler finds the linker of its MSVC targets through the installation of Visual Studio and not on the path alone (checked 2026-10-21: rustc's choice of a linker, `get_linker` in `rustc_codegen_ssa`, which asks the `find-msvc-tools` crate for `link.exe`, which takes the tools on the path of a developer command prompt and otherwise looks for the newest installation of Visual Studio), against the path alone, at the cost that a user on Windows who did not let the installer add LLVM to the path is told that clang is not there, and against the path and a variable that names the clang, at the cost of one more name to set wrong, which the gate tests; the cost is a third place to look, and that of two installs the one on the path wins, while a refusal of it names the place it was found in) *(assumed: that the first of the places that holds a clang, three on Windows and two elsewhere, gives the one taken and that a refusal of it ends the search, the reading of that answer's cost, under which the install on the path wins, said back to the owner on 2026-10-21 and not objected to)*, asks the one it found for its version and refuses it when its major is not the pinned one, on every machine (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "One pinned release", Rust's way as the question named it, rustc shipping one LLVM, against the last two majors, at the cost that the emitter handles their differences in IR text and CI tests both, and against a minimum and up, at the cost that a newer release runs untested while nobody has tried it; the cost is that moving to a newer LLVM is a release of the compiler); Apple's clang, whose version is Apple's own and names no LLVM release, is refused whatever number its answer names (the owner, 2026-10-23, "Refuse it outright", as Apple numbers the clang it ships apart from LLVM's releases (checked 2026-10-23: the table of toolchain versions in the English Wikipedia's article on Xcode, under which Apple clang 17.0.0 is built from LLVM 19.1.4 and 19.1.5, and Apple clang 21.0.0 from LLVM 21.1.6), against a table from Apple's versions to the LLVM releases they are built from, which Apple publishes no list of, at the cost of a table that ages, and against reading Apple's number as a release, under which a refusal names a number that is no LLVM release and an Apple clang of the pinned number passes whatever LLVM it is built from; the cost is that a Mac with Apple's clang alone, one built from the pinned major among them, builds nothing, and LLVM's clang has to stand ahead of it on the path); the compiler's gate must print the version the compiler found and fail when the compiler refused it or found none, a requirement this record puts on the gate; the gate learns that version from the command, running `kurz --version` and printing its output, the compiler's version, the major of LLVM the compiler pins and the clang it found with that clang's version, and failing where the command ends with an exit code other than zero, which it does where it refused that clang or found none (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 20, "kurz --version", as `rustc -vV` prints, among its lines, the version of the LLVM it was built with, which rustc ships, so that it has no other to refuse (checked 2026-10-21: rustc's driver, which prints its version under `-V` and, under `-vV`, also the version of its backend after its own lines, and the LLVM backend's `print_version`, which prints `LLVM version:` with the major, the minor and the patch, read and not run), against a test of the compiler's suite that prints it, at the cost that a user cannot ask for it and that the gate parses the log of a test; the cost is one more option of the command, whose output lines the gate reads), so that on a machine with no clang that the compiler takes, `kurz --version` ends with a failure, which a caller that asks it for the version alone, to learn whether `kurz` is there, reads as a failure of the command, a cost the question did not name *(assumed: that the command writes those lines on standard output, said back to the owner on 2026-10-21 and not objected to)*; `kurz --version` writes them on one line, `kurz <version> (LLVM <major>, clang <release> at <path>)`, and `kurz --version --verbose` on lines of their own, `kurz <version>`, `LLVM version: <major>`, `clang: <path>` and `clang version: <release>` (the owner, 2026-10-23, "One line, like rustc --version", as `rustc -V` writes one line and `rustc -vV` its lines (checked 2026-10-21, above), against those four lines under `--version` alone, at the cost that a caller that wants the parts reads them out of one line; the cost is a second option of the command); before a failure `--version` writes the line without the clang, `kurz <version> (LLVM <major>)`, and `--version --verbose` its first two lines, and `--verbose` stands before or after `--version`, as rustc takes `-v` and `-V` in either order (the owner, 2026-10-23, "Keep them", the readings of that answer said back to the owner, against no output on standard output before a failure, at the cost that a failed run tells a caller no pinned major; the cost is a line on standard output that a caller which asks for the version alone takes for an answer unless it reads the exit code): the pinned major is the newest stable major release of LLVM at the time of the pin, and this record names no number, so that moving to another changes the number the compiler names alone, and the emitter where the release's IR text differs (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "Newest stable", against the major the distribution of the CI image ships, at the cost of an older LLVM; the cost is that installers fetch that release, since distributions lag), and a machine or an image whose distribution has no clang of the pinned major takes it from the packages LLVM publishes for that major, which an image installs when it is built and not on every run, so that the clang the image has (round 15's answer, below) is that release *(assumed: that the pin is the compiler's, that the compiler refuses by it, and LLVM's own packages as the source of a pinned major the distribution lacks, at the cost of an install from outside the distribution on each machine that builds and in the image, proposed with this record, said back to the owner on 2026-10-21 and kept as an assumption; the costs of the two options set against are the question's statement of them)* (the owner, 2026-10-15, round 15, as to the clang that the machine or the image has, against a package pinned by its version, which the distribution drops in time and which Windows has not, and against a downloaded release pinned by its digest, which every run fetches; the cost is that an image update can change the clang behind the name: a release of another major is a refused build and a red run, one of the pinned major is a change the compiler admits by design, so the major is the pin). A requirement this record puts on this repository's CI is an image the repository builds itself, with the pinned release of LLVM from those packages, pushes to GitHub's container registry and builds again when the pin moves, an image the job `gates` runs in, while the Windows job, which no such image serves, installs that release on every run from a cache, so that an image not built again after the pin moved holds a clang of another major, which the compiler refuses, a red run (above) (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 20, "Own Linux image", as Rust's own repository runs its Linux jobs in images it builds from its own source and its Windows jobs install their tools on every run (checked 2026-10-21: the rustc dev guide's chapter on CI, under which every job but those on macOS and Windows runs in a Docker image of the repository's own, built from `src/ci/docker` and cached on GitHub's container registry, and `src/ci/scripts/install-clang.sh` in its repository, which its CI runs and which on Windows downloads the LLVM installer of a pinned release and unpacks it, with no cache, each time it runs), against an install of that release on every run on both systems, from a cache, which the question named Rust's CI, at the cost of a minute or two at each miss of the cache, a download from outside the image, and a change to this record's "not on every run"; the cost is an image and the workflow that builds it to keep, and a Windows job that takes its clang in another way); for that Windows job the answer narrows round 15's (above), since the job installs the pinned release on every run, as the downloaded release that round 15 turned down is installed, and not the clang a machine or an image has, and it bears two of the costs this question gave the option it turned down, a minute or two at each miss of the cache and a download from outside an image *(assumed: that a workflow that every change of the compiler's file holding the pin starts builds the image again, with the red run of a stale image (above) as the check where it did not, proposed with round 20 and not put to the owner)*; the clang that these answers on the places and on `kurz --version` speak of is the one of the pinned major, and where the compiler looks for the clang of Espressif's fork for the Xtensa models, and whether `kurz --version` and the gate report it, is open (section 14). On Windows that clang targets the MSVC ABI, and a build needs beside it the C library and the import libraries of the Visual C++ Build Tools and the Windows SDK, which the LLVM installer does not ship and which clang finds on a machine that has them (both an assumption, which the marker below names); on Linux the pinned clang, the distribution's or LLVM's own (above), links against the distribution's C library in the same way, its math library `libm` with it, as Rust's standard library links it through its `libc` crate (checked 2026-10-29: the `libc` crate's `src/unix/mod.rs`, which links `m` beside `c` on Linux with the GNU C library), since the calls of an unoptimised build reach it, `fmod` for a `%` of a `double`, at the cost of nothing beyond that library's load, which the GNU C library ships with itself (an assumption, which the marker below names); the cost is that a Windows machine that builds Kurz programs has those two installed beside the LLVM installer, under the licence the Build Tools come with, Microsoft's Visual Studio licence, whose terms bound what they may be used for, a condition on whoever builds there (an assumption as to those terms, which the marker below names) *(assumed: what a Windows build needs beside clang, that the LLVM installer does not ship it and that clang finds it where it is installed, the licence terms the Build Tools come under, the ABI that clang targets there and that each platform's clang links that platform's C library, on Linux with its `libm`, proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*. A build for the Xtensa models, which needs Espressif's fork of LLVM (below), takes a second pin, one release of that fork, for those builds alone, and the compiler refuses another release of the fork for them as it refuses a clang of another major for the rest, as Rust's support for those models is Espressif's fork of the Rust compiler with a fork of LLVM, which Espressif's `espup` installs beside upstream Rust (checked 2026-10-21: the Rust on ESP book's chapter on the toolchain, under which `espup` installs Espressif's Rust fork, an LLVM fork with support for Xtensa and a GCC toolchain that links the binary, and the forked compiler can coexist with the standard one) (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "Second pin for Xtensa", against one pin for every target that follows the fork's major, at the cost that every target waits on the fork's lag and the answer "Newest stable" no longer holds, and against no Xtensa models, keeping the RISC-V ones that upstream LLVM builds, at the cost of the classic ESP32, the S2 and the S3; the cost is a second clang to install for a program that builds for those models, and two releases to keep current).
- The runtime a compiled program links is written in Kurz, one runtime per target, so that every target has the whole language (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "A runtime per target", in the owner's words "A runtime per target but the library is in kurz not in C?? why are you talking about C so much, it will be pure kurz for everything", which sets aside the runtime in C of round 15 (below), against Rust's split, under which a target without a runtime has plain functions and numbers and no actors, strings, lists or `print`, as Rust's `no_std`, at the cost of two flavours of the language, and against no runtime on the targets that run neither Linux nor Windows, at the cost of actors, strings, lists, `print` and the update of a device on all of them; the cost is one runtime per target to write and maintain, the small memory of AVR included); the compiler compiles its Kurz with the program's, as it compiles the standard library's, whose packages it takes as source (section 13), and the same clang compiles the IR of both and links it statically (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "With each program", whole-program as this section decides for all code, against the runtime shipped compiled per target, as Rust ships its standard library as one `rust-std` component per target (checked 2026-10-21: the rustup book's page on components), which a build links and does not compile (an assumption as to what the component holds, which the marker below names), at the cost of a compiled form beside packages that are source only and of whole-program checks that see the runtime through its interface alone; the cost is the runtime's compile in every build and every `kurz exec`; that the same clang compiles the IR of both and links it is an assumption, which the marker below names); its source is the directory `runtime/` at the top of the compiler's repository, beside `compiler/`, which the build of the compiler embeds into the executable `kurz` (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 20, "Top-level runtime/", as Rust keeps the source of its standard library in the directory `library/` at the top of its compiler's repository (checked 2026-10-21: the directory `library/` of Rust's repository, which holds `core`, `alloc` and `std`, and the rustup book's page on components, under which `rust-src` is a local copy of the source code of the standard library), against a directory under `compiler/`, at the cost that it reads as a part of the compiler written in C#, and against files shipped beside the executable, at the cost of an install in two parts, in which a file missing or edited breaks every build; the cost is a rule of the tree gate that admits `.kz` files there, changed with its gate, and that every change of the runtime is a release of the compiler), a rule the tree gate holds, which admits `.kz` files in the directories of `runtime/`; the embedding is a departure from Rust's way, which ships that source apart, as `rust-src` (above), at the cost that the user has no file of the runtime's source, a cost the question did not name; the code that every target shares is in `runtime/common/` and the code of one target in a directory of that target's, `runtime/linux/` and `runtime/windows/`, and the compiler compiles `common/` with the directory of the target it builds for and no other target's (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 20, "Directory per target", against a suffix of a file's name that names its target, at the cost of code written twice and a rule for the names, and against a form of the language that compiles code on a condition, as Rust's `#[cfg]` does, at the cost of the language's first conditional compilation and of code for the other targets that is checked only in a build for them; the cost is that a function that differs by one line between two targets lives in two files, and that every target's directory gives the same functions, which the checker checks), a choice by directory that is a departure from Rust's way, a reading the question did not give, which named Rust's `sys/pal`, chosen per module, as its model, since Rust's standard library keeps the code of each platform in a file or a directory of that platform's and picks it with a condition, the form set aside above (checked 2026-10-21: the modules of `library/std/src/sys` in Rust's repository, `sys/alloc` and `sys/pal` among them, each of which holds a file or a directory per platform and picks the platform's with `cfg_select!`), the functions that every target's directory gives being the ones a function outside the directory calls, each with the same parameters and the same result, while a function that only its own directory calls is that directory's alone (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 20, "Called from outside", against every function of the directory, its helpers among them, at the cost of a helper of one target written as a stub on every other; the cost is that the checker tells the two kinds apart by their callers), which the answer "not supported" (below) narrows to the functions the code of `runtime/common/` and of `std/` calls *(assumed: that reading of the two answers, since a call of the program comes from outside the directory too and reaches a function the answer lets a target's directory lack; a default the product pass of "not supported" took, not put to the owner)*, code in `common/` reaching those functions as the folder `sys`, the directory of the target the compiler builds for with the directory of the build's architecture read into it: `common/` writes `use sys` and calls `sys.Write(...)`, and a function of a target's directory that `common/` calls is `pub` (the owner, 2026-10-23, "Rust's sys", as Rust's standard library reaches the code of its platform through its module `sys`, which picks the platform's module with the condition of the check above (checked 2026-10-23: `library/std/src/io/stdio.rs` of Rust's repository, which writes `use crate::sys::stdio;` and calls `crate::sys::io::is_terminal(self)`), against reading `common/`, the target's directory and its architecture's as one folder whose files see each other without `use`, at the cost of a rule the runtime alone has, against section 8's folder as a namespace, and of a name declared in two of them being `redeclared`; the cost is `use` of a folder in the checker, which section 8 needs for any program of more than one folder, calls qualified by the folder's name, a name that means another folder in each build, and `pub` on every function of a target that `common/` calls), and a build for a target whose runtime lacks a function that a call of the program reaches, as the runtime of Windows lacks the calls on files and sockets (section 12), says `not supported` at that call of the program (the owner, 2026-10-30, "not supported", against a runtime that gives the function and fails when it is called, as Rust's standard library builds `File::open` for `wasm32-unknown-unknown` and answers that the operation is not supported on this platform (checked 2026-10-30 with Rust 1.82), at the cost of a failure that shows only when the program runs; the cost is a program that does not build for that target), so that the check of the same functions holds every target's directory to the functions the code of `runtime/common/` and of `std/` calls, and a function that the program's calls alone reach is one a target's directory may lack, as the functions on files and sockets are, which a program's call reaches in the target's directory and which the code of `std/`, which holds the allocator and the blocks of lists and maps, does not call, while the check fails the directory of a target whose runtime lacks a function the code of `runtime/common/` or of `std/` calls, and what a build for that target does then is open (section 14), and the code of one architecture of a target is in a directory of that architecture's inside the target's, `runtime/linux/x86-64/` and `runtime/linux/arm64/`, which the compiler compiles with `common/` and the files of the target's directory outside the directories of its architectures, the directory of the build's architecture and no other architecture's (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 20, "Dir per arch", by the mechanism of the answer above and a departure from Rust's way as that one is, since Rust picks the code of an architecture with `#[cfg(target_arch = ...)]` (checked 2026-10-21: `crates/core_arch/src/mod.rs` of Rust's `stdarch`, the source of `core::arch`, which gates its modules `x86`, `x86_64` and `aarch64` with `#[cfg(any(target_arch = ...))]`), against a directory per system and architecture, `runtime/linux-x86-64/`, at the cost that what the architectures of one system share is written twice or needs a directory inside anyway, and against the switch of stacks emitted by the compiler as IR for each architecture, at the cost of code of the runtime outside the runtime and of round 17's `naked` function in Kurz set aside; the cost is a third level of directories, and the check of the same functions for each architecture's directory) *(assumed: that WebAssembly, the ESP32 models and AVR get a directory each in the same way, and that the checker reads the directory of every target and of every architecture in each build, so that the code of no target is checked only in a build for it, the reading under which the form set aside above bears that cost and this one does not, at the cost of that reading in each build, and that the check of the same functions reads the files of a target's directory and those of one of its architectures as one set, matched against the set of every other target and architecture, all three proposed with round 20 and not put to the owner)*; and the runtime is built as the program is, in a test build with the overflow checks of section 4 and with its calls kept as calls (below), and in a release build as a release (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 20, "As the program", a departure from Rust's way, which the question named and recommended, under which the standard library ships built without overflow checks, whatever the build of the program that links it, but for its code marked to inherit the program's (checked 2026-10-21: the documentation of `overflow_checks()` in Rust's `core::intrinsics`, under which a program built with overflow checks is commonly linked against the distributed standard library, built without them, whose code marked to inherit them takes the program's, and Cargo's unstable `build-std`, which compiles the standard library with the program's profile), against the runtime built as a release in both builds, at the cost that an overflow in the runtime wraps without a word in the test build too; the cost is that every allocation and every `print` is slower in the test build, and that a test run measures a slower runtime than a release build runs), the fault handler and the crash path of section 6 among its code, where a check that fires inside them in a test build ends the process with the one line section 6 names for it, which carries the check's id and no source line, and no trace (section 6, decided there); it gives a program its printing, the text of its values, the memory forms of its strings and lists with the primitives on them that the emitted IR calls (the blocks of strings, lists and maps, with the functions the emitted code calls on them, stand in the code of `std/`, below, which a build compiles with every program as it compiles the runtime), its exit on an exception, and, on every target but WebAssembly, where every actor runs on the one stack (below), the switch of a thread to an actor's stack and back, which the scheduler of section 3 calls as a primitive (section 3, an assumption there) and which the runtime writes as inline assembly in a `naked` function (section 3, decided there), whose body counts as a `raw` block (section 3, an assumption there, which the owner kept on 2026-10-21): a few instructions per target and architecture that store the registers the calling convention keeps across a call and the stack pointer into the record of the actor the switch leaves, which the scheduler owns, and load the other's from its record, and not onto the stack, so that the call's return address is the only word the switch's call puts on the stack it leaves, pushed before the switch runs, and a fault there is the caller's and lands inside no change of the switch's (an assumption, which the marker below names; the cost is the room for those registers in every actor's record, the ten vector registers Windows keeps across a call among them), so that a switch costs those stores and loads and the call, the store of the actor's stack limit into the runtime's record of the thread, which the scheduler makes before the switch to an actor, and of the empty limit after the switch back, on both platforms (section 6, an assumption there), with the four stores into the thread environment block on Windows (below), the saving and the clearing of the count per thread of section 6 (decided there) on the way to an actor and its restoring on the way back, a load and a store each way and the clearing's store on the way to the actor, and no entry compare, since the switch has no frame of its own to fit and saves nothing on the stack (section 6, where the compare is an assumption), the compiler emitting no prologue and no epilogue for it, as Rust's compiler emits none for a naked function, whose body is one block of assembly (checked 2026-10-21: the Rust Reference's `naked` attribute, stable since Rust 1.88), the switch being such a function in Kurz, marked `naked` (section 3, decided there), and whoever maintains the runtime writes and carries those instructions for each target and architecture that has the switch; the collections, the allocator and the scheduler of section 3 (decided there) are written in Kurz above those primitives *(assumed: the scope the question described, the switch of stacks as a primitive the scheduler calls, its instructions per target and architecture, with its cost, that the same clang compiles the IR of both and links it statically, and what Rust's `rust-std` holds, not put to the owner as questions of their own)*. The runtime's string and list primitives take their memory from the allocator of section 3 (decided there) through one allocation entry, the running actor's heap, which the allocator provides and the runtime calls, so that a string the runtime builds lives on the heap of the actor that asked for it and is freed with that heap (section 3, decided there); ## 3. Memory

- No lifetimes, no borrow annotations, no global garbage collector.
- Immutable by default. Immutable data cannot form reference cycles, so compiler-inserted reference counting is enough, and most counting is optimized away at compile time.
- Mutable state lives inside exactly one actor. Each actor has its own heap. When an actor dies its whole heap is freed at once. A big value that is shared between actors lives outside these heaps (section 6).
- There is **no cycle collector**. A possible reference cycle is a compile error.
  - The rule is judged by types, over the whole program: a class may not reach itself through strong fields when one field on that path is `mut`. Values cannot contain themselves (section 4), so the rule only ever applies to classes.
  - `weak` means "I point at this but do not keep it alive". A weak reference is nullable and becomes null when its target is freed. It is the same idea as `WeakReference<T>` in C#. A weak type always shows its `?`: `weak Node?` (the owner, 2026-10-02).
  - Between different types, `weak` on the back-pointer is enough: `Customer` holds its orders, `Order` points back `weak`. A class that reaches itself through a strong `mut` field is rejected even with a `weak` back-pointer, because `a.children.Add(b)` followed by `b.children.Add(a)` closes a ring through the children alone.
  - Only `mut` fields can close a cycle, so with immutable-by-default the error is rare.
  - A `mut` field of interface type could hold any implementer; the compiler needs the whole program to judge it. A field of function type, `mut` or not, is judged the same way: the lambda it holds can capture any class instance, so the field counts as reaching every class whose instance a lambda of the program captures, and the `mut` field that closes the ring can be the function field or any other field on the path; a button that stores a handler that reads the button is a cycle (the owner, 2026-10-03, against a weak capture inside stored lambdas and against forbidding such a capture; that a field without `mut` counts too follows from a ring the review showed through one, *(assumed)*). The cost is not local: once a class has a field of function type, any lambda anywhere in the program that captures an instance of that class, or of a class that holds one, makes it an error, and the lambda can sit in another file. An event handler escapes the error only in a program in which no lambda captures the button's class: through an interface (which must not hold the button), or by receiving the button as a parameter.
  - Subscribing a handler to a source gives a subscription value, which unsubscribes the handler at its last count, as a Rust drop guard does and as the `IDisposable` that .NET's `IObservable<T>.Subscribe` returns is meant to, so that the holder of the subscription decides how long the source keeps the handler and what the handler reaches (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Subscription value", against an unsubscribe by hand, at the cost of C#'s leak of a listener that a long-lived source holds, and against a weak capture for a stored lambda, which the owner set aside on 2026-10-03 (above), at the cost of a liveness check and a `null` case at every call of the handler; the cost is a type of the library, the hook that section 12 runs at the last count of a handle on a second type, and a field for each subscription). A handler that reads the holder of its subscription closes a ring, from the holder through the subscription, the source and the handler back to the holder, and that ring is the error of the bullet above, as any ring through a field of function type is: a subscription serves a handler that reads no holder, one that reaches its actor with a `send` or reads what the event passes it, in a program in which no lambda captures the holder's class or a class that holds one, as the bullet above says of the button (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Ring stays an error", against the source's edge to its handler left out of the rule, since the subscription's last count unsubscribes, at the cost of a ring that nothing breaks while the holder is held through the source alone, C#'s leak of a listener; the cost is that C#'s `this.sub = service.Changed.Subscribe(() => this.Refresh())` does not build).
- A tree is a recursive `data` value. It is updated in place while nobody else holds it; when somebody holds an older version, an update copies the path from the root to the changed node. It has no parent pointers: the parent is passed down while walking.
- Objects with identity that point at each other (scene graph, UI tree, general graphs) have one flat owner; the edges are `weak` or indices. Each hop through a `weak` edge pays a small liveness check.
- The standard library gives such a graph an arena whose handles carry a generation, so that a handle to a slot reused since it was made is a case the program handles and not a wrong object (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Arena with generations", against `weak` edges and plain indices alone, at the cost of the bookkeeping of indices by hand and of a stale index that stays a silent bug, and against a collector that a class opts into, dropped on 2026-10-01 (below), at the cost of a collector in the runtime and of pauses in the actors that use it; the cost is the library's work and a compare at each use of a handle).
- A grid is a flat array and a chunked world is a map of chunk values; the rule does not touch either.
- Known trap: a write to one large flat array while a snapshot of it is still held copies the whole array once. Large data is chunked, and the compiler can warn where a write provably copies.
- The compiler warns where a write provably copies, with an id for the warning, and a build report lists every site of an allocation or a copy for each function, as a compiler's optimisation remarks list them (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Warning rule + report", against nothing more, at the cost that a developer finds copies with a profiler, and against Rust's way, where a copy of a value that is not trivial is written as `.clone()`, at the cost of noise in every program and a break with C#'s meaning of a value; the cost is a copy analysis in the compiler with some false alarms, and the tool work of the report). The warning stands only where every run of the write copies, and a copy the analysis cannot prove draws no warning and stands in the build report among the sites of a copy (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Only proven copies", against a warning where a run may copy, at the cost of false alarms that developers learn to ignore and of a harder analysis, as Rust's Clippy moved its `redundant_clone` lint to the nursery group, which is off by default, for its false alarms, and against both, the second under an id switched on, at the cost of a second id and a second analysis; the cost is a copy that draws no warning, found in the report or by a profiler, in the place of the false alarms that the cost of "Warning rule + report" names).
- **Lifetimes.** When an instance is freed follows from what holds it: a variable holds its instance until the end of the block that declares the variable, a field as long as its owner lives, a temporary until the end of its statement; the instance is freed when the last of these ends, and a `weak` reference to it is null from then on. *(assumed: proposed on 2026-10-03, against releasing a variable after its last use as Swift's ARC may, which would free an instance that a later line still reads through a `weak` reference)*
- **Raw memory.** Collections, the allocator and the scheduler are written in Kurz, above the primitives of the runtime, which is Kurz too (section 2, decided there), and which take their memory from the allocator through its entry (section 2, an assumption there), so some Kurz code has to touch raw memory, the runtime's among it. That code sits in `raw` blocks (pointers, manual allocation), and so do the machine instructions no other form of the language expresses, the switch of stacks among them (section 2), as inline assembly (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "Inline assembly in raw blocks", as Rust's `asm!` stands inside `unsafe` (checked 2026-10-21: the Rust Reference's chapter on inline assembly, under which invoking it is an `unsafe` capability whose caller takes on not violating the rules of the compiler or of the architecture, and whose operands each name a direction and a register class or a register), against built-ins of the compiler such as `switch_stack(...)` and `syscall(...)` that the compiler expands, at the cost of a new built-in for each need of an instruction; the cost is an assembly syntax and its operand rules in the language); inline assembly is a statement `asm(...)` inside a `raw` block, written as Rust writes `asm!`: `asm("add {r}, 1", r = inout(reg) count)`, where a `{name}` in the template names an operand and is no interpolation, unlike in every other string, where a `{...}` interpolates (section 8), a `"""` block holds a template of many lines, and the operands take Rust's words, `in`, `out`, `inout`, `lateout`, the register classes and `clobber_abi` (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "Rust's form as asm()", against the braces escaped, `\{r}`, at the cost of every operand reference escaped, and against the operands inside the template, `asm("add {inout(reg) count}, 1")`, at the cost of a template that is no string and a departure from Rust; the cost is that braces mean something else inside `asm` alone). The template and its operands take the rest of Rust's rules for them: `{{` and `}}` write a brace, an operand is taken by its name or by its position, a modifier after a colon gives the form of its register, `{r:e}`, every operand appears in the template but one in an explicit register, `in("rax") 60`, which the template names nowhere, and the operands take the rest of Rust's words, `inlateout`, `const`, `sym`, `label` and `options(...)`, words inside an `asm` alone as the others are (below), while a variable an operand writes is `mut`, as every variable a program assigns is (checked 2026-10-21: the Rust Reference's chapter on inline assembly, under which the template takes the syntax of a format string, operands are taken in order, by index or by name, with a modifier after the colon, an operand the template never uses fails the compile, and the operand kinds are those words; checked again 2026-10-22: the same chapter, under which explicit register operands cannot be used by placeholders in the template string, and all other operands must appear in it at least once) *(assumed: Rust's rules of the template and the rest of its words, proposed with this record, said back to the owner on 2026-10-21 and kept as an assumption)*. On WebAssembly and on Xtensa, which the Reference's list does not name, the register classes are those of Rust's experimental support for them, `local` on wasm32 and `reg`, `freg`, `sreg` and `breg` on Xtensa (checked 2026-10-21: the Rust unstable book's `asm_experimental_arch`, which names both architectures with those classes); a breach of the template's rules, an operand the template does not use but one in an explicit register, a name in it no operand has, or a word or a register class that is not one of Rust's, is the compile error `asm-operand`, and an operand that writes a variable without `mut` is the error for an assignment to it, as any assignment is *(assumed: the classes there and the ids, proposed with this record, said back to the owner on 2026-10-21 and kept as an assumption)*. A register class is read against Rust's table for the target the build compiles for (section 2), so that a class the build's target does not have, `xmm_reg` in a build for AArch64, is `asm-operand` at its line, and a case of the conformance corpus names its target in its header, or is read for x86-64 where it names none (the owner, 2026-10-22, round 21, "The build's target", against every class Rust names for any architecture, a class wrong for the target being reported by clang when it compiles the emitted code, at the cost of an error that comes late and names a place in the emitted code and not the program's line, and against no check of the class, at the cost that `asm-operand` would name a check no compiler makes; the cost is a table of classes for each target in the compiler; a cost the question did not name is that every compiler carries x86-64's table of classes to read the corpus's cases of `asm`, whatever target it builds for, while no case names its target), how a header names the target standing open (section 14). A function the compiler emits no prologue and no epilogue for is marked `naked` before its signature, `naked void Switch(...)`, and its body is one `asm`, in a package granted `allow raw` (below), as Rust marks a naked function `#[unsafe(naked)]` with one `naked_asm!` as its body (checked 2026-10-21: the Rust Reference's `naked` attribute) (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "A naked word", against assembly text at the level of a module, Rust's `global_asm!`, at the cost of the symbol and the unwind directives written by hand; the cost is a function form in the language). `naked` stands before a function at the top level of a file alone, `naked extern "C"` among them, since a naked function takes the C calling convention in any case (below), and before a method, a lambda or a function declared inside a block of code it is the error for text no rule gives a meaning, a method's `this` and the layout of its class being the compiler's own, which an `asm` cannot rely on (the owner, 2026-10-22, round 21, "Top-level functions only", against methods too, at the cost that the compiler fixes how a method receives `this`, in the first argument register of the C calling convention, for an `asm` to read, and against the top level alone with an id of its own for a misplaced `naked`, at the cost of one more row in the reference's table of errors; a cost the question did not name is that a naked function reaches the state of an object through its parameters alone). A `naked extern "C"` function takes the grant of each of its words, `allow raw` for `naked` and `allow native` for the convention (section 13), while a `naked` function without `extern "C"` takes `allow raw` alone, the grant going with the word written and not with the convention a naked function takes in any case, so that in a package with neither grant a `naked extern "C"` function is `naked-not-allowed` and `native-not-allowed` at its declaration, one error for each missing grant, two at one line, which no case shows, a case expecting one error (below) *(assumed: the grants of `naked extern "C"` and the two errors, proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*.
