- Considered and dropped on 2026-10-01: a single-owner rule in which a node sits in one place and changes place with `move`, and a collector that a class opts into by keyword. The collector could return later as an opt-in keyword without breaking code.
- Expected speed, taken from a language that uses the same technique; no Kurz measurement exists. Koka's purely functional red-black tree, updated in place this way, ran 42 million inserts within 10% of C++ `std::map` ([Perceus, MSR-TR-2020-42](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2020/11/perceus-tr-v1.pdf)); a later paper measured it 19% faster on one CPU and about equal on another ([Frame Limited Reuse, MSR-TR-2021-30](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2021/11/flreuse-tr.pdf)).

```
data Tree(int Key, Tree? Left, Tree? Right)

mut root = Tree(8, null, null)
root.Insert(3)                 // a `mut` method: walks down and writes in place
root.Key = 9

class Scene { mut Map<int, Node> nodes }      // owns every node
class Node {
    mut List<weak Node?> children
    mut weak Node? parent
}
```

## 4. Types and paradigm

- Static typing with inference.
- Immutable by default; `mut` marks what may change.
- **Values.** `data`, collections, strings and numbers are values (the owner chose this as "probably" on 2026-10-01; the cycle rule of section 3 and the `mut` parameters below were chosen afterwards and build on it):
  - Without `mut`, nothing reachable through a variable changes. With `mut`, it changes in place. The guarantee stops at a class instance: its `mut` fields change through every reference to it, whatever holds the reference (the class bullet below), so what a variable reaches through an instance is not covered. *(assumed: the qualification; it follows from the owner's choice on `mut` fields)*
  - Assigning or passing a value copies it in meaning. In practice the storage is shared until one side writes, and a write happens in place when nobody else holds the value. Where the compiler cannot prove that, a counter check precedes the write. The counter of a list is the count of the list values that hold its storage, which a copy raises and the end of a holder lowers, at the end of its scope, at an assignment over it and where the value that holds it ends, and the storage is freed when the count reaches zero, as Swift's `Array` counts its storage and copies it at a write while another array holds it (the owner, 2026-10-24, "Full counting now", against a count that only grows, under which nothing is freed and a list shared once is copied at its next write after the other holder is gone, and against a copy of the list at every copy, as C++ copies a `std::vector` by value, every item at every assignment and nothing freed; the cost is the count in every list, a change of it at every copy and every end of a holder, and code at each of those places for every variable, field and union case that holds a list, which the option named as "the largest change to the emitter, with drop points for every variable, field and union"). A string is counted as a list's storage is: a string made while the program runs holds its bytes in a block that holds, in front of them, the count of the values that hold the block, the count of its bytes and the room it was given, 16 bytes, laid out as a list of bytes lays out its block, so that `s.Bytes` shares it (below), as Rust's `Arc<str>` holds its counts in front of the text in one allocation (checked 2026-10-29 with Rust 1.82: an `Arc<str>` is the 16 bytes of an address and a length, and `Arc::from` of a `String` copies the text into the allocation of the counts); a copy raises the count, the end of a holder lowers it where a list's count is lowered, and the block is freed when the count reaches zero; a string never changes (below), so no write copies a block *(assumed: the block in front of the bytes, its 16 bytes and the counts and the room among them, proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*. A literal holds no block, the empty literal among them, and is never freed, and a string tells the two apart by the highest bit of the 64-bit word that holds its count of bytes, which the limit of the largest `int` (below) leaves free, so that a copy and the end of a literal read no memory (the owner, 2026-10-29, "Bit in the count", as Swift marks a literal string immortal in the highest bit of its 16-byte value (checked 2026-10-29: `StringObject.swift` in Swift's standard library, "b63: isImmortal: Should the Swift runtime skip ARC", "Large strings can sometimes be immortal, e.g. literals") and as Rust's `Cow<'static, str>` keeps its tag in values the capacity of a `String` never takes (checked 2026-10-29 with Rust 1.82: a `Cow<str>` takes the 24 bytes of a `String`), against a count in front of every literal too, in the executable, whose value means never freed, as Swift's statically allocated objects hold one, at the cost of 16 bytes for each literal and a read of memory at every copy and end of one, and against a test of the address against the constants of the executable, at the cost of two comparisons with symbols that differ for each target and linker; the cost is a mask at every read of the count of bytes and the 16 bytes in front of every string made while the program runs). A function value is the pair of its code and its environment: a lambda that reads variables allocates its environment once, when it is made, with the values it reads, and the environment is counted and freed as a list's storage is, while a lambda that reads none allocates nothing; a call of a function value is indirect, and the compiler may inline it where it sees the lambda (the owner, 2026-10-25, "Pair + heap env", as C#'s delegates and Rust's `Rc<dyn Fn>`, against an environment on the caller's stack for a lambda passed only to parameters that never keep it, which costs a whole-program analysis, and against Rust's `impl Fn`, a copy of a function for each lambda passed to it, which costs code size; the cost is one allocation for each lambda made that reads a variable, and a change of the count at every copy and every end of a holder). The environment holds what its lambda reads, so a ring through a field of function type is judged by the cycle rule of section 3, under which such a field reaches every class whose instance a lambda in the program can capture (the owner, 2026-10-03; section 3 names its cost, which is not local: a lambda in any file that captures such an instance can close the ring).
  - There is one family of collections, and every value can cross between actors.
  - Assigning into a path of a `mut` variable stores what nested `with` over the variable stores, with the variable read after the value: the value is evaluated first, then the path, in the variable as the value left it, so a `mut` argument in the value that changes the variable keeps its change, which nested `with` written before the value would lose: Rust evaluates an assignment's value before its place, and C# writes the field of a struct into the variable's own storage after the right side ran (checked 2026-10-24 on .NET 10: `c.Home.City = Move(ref c)` keeps the `Customer` that `Move` stored) (the owner, 2026-10-24, "Value first", against the path first, as the nested `with` spells it, under which that change is lost without a word, and against a compile error for a value that changes the path's variable, which refuses what Rust and C# accept).
  - A method that changes its own value carries a `mut` marker and can be called only on a `mut` variable.
  - A function changes a caller's value only through a `mut` parameter, as `ref` does in C#, and the caller writes `mut` at the call as well. Without both, a method cannot change the collection it was handed.
  - Within one expression, a read of what a call takes as `mut`, of a value that holds it or of a value it holds, the variable itself, a path of fields or an item of a list, is a compile error where the expression still holds the read's value when that call runs: as an argument of that call, or of a call around it given before the argument the call stands in; as the receiver of a method, or the target of `with` or of an index, around the call; as an operand of an operator around it, written before the operand the call stands in; and in a lambda made before the call that reads the variable. So `xs.Add(F(mut xs))`, `G(xs, F(mut xs))`, `Fill(mut a, a)`, `p with { A = F(mut p) }` for a `p` that holds a list, `xs[F(mut xs)]`, `xs == F(mut xs)`, `G(ys[0], F(mut ys))` for a list of lists `ys` and `G(() => xs.Count, F(mut xs))` are that error, as Rust's borrow check refuses a mutable borrow while a shared one is in use, and two fields of one variable stay two values, so `G(c.Items, F(mut c.Name))` is none (the owner, 2026-10-24: "Compile error" for the method, as Rust refuses `v.push(f(&mut v))`, against its arguments first, as C# calls the method of a struct on the variable after its arguments ran, and against the receiver first; "Compile error, as Add" for the argument, the `with` and the index, against the value read first kept, as C# keeps it; "Rust's line", against every read of the variable in the expression; "All three too" for the operand, the item and the lambda, against those three reading the value from before the call, as in C#). `Add` changes its receiver after its argument ran and counts the item it stores before the list grows, so a read of the receiver in its argument holds nothing while it changes: `rows.Add(rows[0])` for a list of lists `rows` passes, as Rust takes `v.push(v[0].clone())` through its two-phase borrows, while the receiver is held against a call inside the argument, so `xs.Add(F(mut xs))` stays the error (the owner, 2026-10-25, "Allow, as Rust's clone", which replaces "Refused, as Rust" of the same day, an answer given on a comparison that does not hold: Rust refuses `v.push(v[0])` for a `Vec<Vec<i32>>` as a move out of an index, E0507, not by its borrow check; against keeping the call refused, stricter than Rust; the cost at run time is none, since the item `Add` stores is counted before the list grows). A lambda that reads the receiver and is that argument's value, bare, in brackets or as the value of an arm, holds it while `Add` changes it, so `fs.Add(() => fs.Count)` for a list of function values is the error, as Rust refuses `fs.push(Box::new(|| fs.len()))` while the closure borrows `fs` (E0502) (the owner, 2026-10-25, "Refuse, as Rust's borrow", against a lambda that holds a counted copy of the list, under which that `Add` copies the list once; the fix is a copy into a variable on a line before). So is a lambda given to a call or a `with` in that argument whose value can keep it, a value of a type that can hold a function value: `fs.Add(Wrap(() => fs.Count))`, with a `Wrap` that returns its lambda, is the error, while `xs.Add(Count(() => xs.Count))`, whose `Count` gives an `int`, passes, as Rust refuses `fs.push(Box::new(wrap(|| fs.len())))` (E0502) and takes `v.push(count(|| v.len()))` (checked with rustc 1.82 on 2026-10-25) (the owner, 2026-10-25, "Refuse if it can keep it", against every lambda in the argument that reads the receiver, which refuses the second as well, and against a call's result as a value of its own, under which the first passes and its `Add` copies the list once; the cost is that a call that gives another function value than the lambda it is given is refused too, where Rust's lifetimes tell the two apart). Only what the argument's value can keep counts: a value inside it of a type that can hold no function value ends what it keeps, so `xs.Add(Count(Wrap(() => xs.Count)))`, whose `Count` gives an `int`, passes, as Rust takes `v.push(count(wrap(|| v.len())))` (checked with rustc 1.82 on 2026-10-28) (the owner, 2026-10-28, "Pass, as Rust", against counting every value made between the lambda and the argument, as the words of the option of 2026-10-25 read, under which it is the error; nothing at run time, since the environment is let go of before the list grows). `Add` lets go of the environment of such a lambda before the list grows, so that no count the environment holds makes `Add` copy the items (the owner, 2026-10-28, "Pass, as Rust", whose option states it), and of everything else its argument made besides its item as well *(assumed: the item is counted into the list, and with it what the item holds, before the list grows, and the other values the argument made belong to the argument's own region, which ends there; the option named the environment alone)*. A type that can hold a function value is a function type, a type parameter, a list or a union that holds one, and a `data` type when one of its type arguments holds one, whether a field stores it or not, or when a field of it or of a type derived from it holds one (the owner, 2026-10-28, "confirmed", for the type parameter, since a function value of type `(() => int) => T` can give the lambda back as a `T`, and for the `data` type whose derived type holds one, since a value of the base can be one of them; the option of 2026-10-25 names the cost that Kurz's types cannot tell such a value from one that keeps nothing; and "Counts, as Rust", for a type argument that no field stores, as Rust refuses a type parameter that no field uses (E0392) or that only the type itself uses, and a type that names one through `PhantomData<T>` keeps the borrow of its argument, so `let q = keep(|| v.len()); v.push(1);` is E0502 while `q` is used after it (checked with rustc 1.82 on 2026-10-28), against counting it only where a field can store a value of it, under which `qs.Add(Keep(() => qs.Count))` passes for a `data Q<T>(int X, List<Q<T>> N)`, and against refusing such a declaration, as Rust does; the cost is that such an `Add` is refused though no value of the type can keep the lambda). A field's type is read with the type's arguments in place of its parameters, so a field of type `T` holds what the argument for `T` holds *(assumed: as C# and Rust read the fields of a generic type, which no answer named)*. A map holds one where its key or its value type can, a nullable type where the type it makes nullable can, and a class where one of its type arguments or a field of it or of a class derived from it can *(assumed: each holds the values of its types, and a class takes the words of a `data` type in full, its type arguments among them, as "Counts, as Rust" gives them to a `data` type; no answer named either)*. So is an assignment of the variable or of a path into it, and a `mut` method called on either, in a statement of an `else` arm's block inside the expression, nested statements among them, where the expression holds the read's value while the arm runs: `G(xs, H() else { E => { xs = List<int>(); 5 } })` (the owner, 2026-10-25, "Compile error, as Rust", as Rust refuses the assignment of a borrowed variable, E0506, against a count the call would hold of each argument it borrowed, at a count and a release per such call). That block is the one an arm has inside an expression, since an arm of a `match` used as a value is the value it yields (section 5). A read that makes a new value first, `xs.Count` or `xs[0] + 1`, and a read that runs after the call has ended, `G(F(mut xs), xs)` or `F(mut xs) + xs.Count`, hold no such value (the owner, 2026-10-24, "Rust's line", and "Allowed, as Rust", against refusing every other read of the variable in the expression). Neither does a read of a copied type, which the expression holds as a copy: a number, a `bool`, a `char`, a pointer, a time, the type of a C function, and a `data` value or a union made only of such types that holds no value of its own type, so `G(n, F(mut n))` for an `int n` passes `n` as it was before the call (the owner, 2026-10-24, "Rust's Copy line", as Rust copies an `i32` before it takes `&mut n` and refuses the call for a `Vec`, against every type, under which that call is the error where Rust and C# accept it; the cost is that the rule asks the type of each such read, and a `data` type that gains a list field turns such calls into errors). A list, a `string`, a type parameter and a `data` type that holds a value of its own type are not copied, as Rust's `Vec`, `String`, a generic `T` without `Copy` and a `Box` are not `Copy` (the owner, 2026-10-25, "Rust's Copy set", against every type that holds no list, under which a `string` and a `data` type of numbers that holds itself pass while nothing frees them, and need a count once something does; the cost is that `G(s, F(mut s))` for a `string s` is the error). A function value is copied, as Rust copies a function pointer and a closure whose captures are `Copy`, so `G(f, Swap(mut f))` passes (the owner, 2026-10-25, "Copied", which replaces the function value among the types "Rust's Copy set" held, an answer given on the claim that Rust's closures are not `Copy`, which does not hold; against holding it, since its type does not tell a lambda that captures a list from one that captures nothing; the cost is a count and a release at run time for each such read of a function value that holds counted captures). So is a `data` value or a union made only of function values and copied types, `data Rule((int) => int Step, int Limit)`, as the rule of "Rust's Copy set" copies such a value made only of copied types and Rust copies a struct of function pointers and `i32` (the owner, 2026-10-25, "Copied too", against holding such a value while the bare function value is copied, at the same cost per read). A `data` type is copied only where every type derived from it is too, since a value of it can be one of them *(assumed: Rust has no derived types, and a value of the base can hold a derived type's list)*. Such a read is the compile error `changed-while-held`, at the line of what changes the value, an argument after `mut`, a `mut` method's call or an assignment (the owner, 2026-10-25, "New id changed-while-held", as Rust tells E0502 and E0506 apart from E0596 and E0499, against `mut-at-call`, which keeps a missing or misplaced marker and a path passed as `mut` twice), once for each such change however many reads it meets *(assumed: E1, one defect, one error)*. A hole of an interpolation of a type other than `string` is made text where it stands, so its read makes a new value *(assumed: C#'s interpolation appends each hole in the order written)*. A hole of a `string` holds the string it read until the interpolation is made, so a hole after it that changes what it read is the error (the owner, 2026-10-29, "Refuse, as Rust", as Rust's `format!` borrows its arguments until it has made its `String` (checked 2026-10-29 with Rust 1.82: `format!("{}{}", s, f(&mut s))` is E0502), against a count of the string taken at the hole, as `Add` counts its item, under which such a program prints the string as the hole read it, at the cost of a test of the bit and a change of the count at every hole of a string that a hole after it can change; the cost is the error where a program changes a string that a hole before has read, met by a copy of the string into a variable on a line before). An interpolation is a new value, one of a single hole of a `string` among them, which holds a count of its own, so a call around it holds nothing of what the hole read (the owner, 2026-10-29, "New value, as format!", as Rust's `format!` makes a new `String` (checked 2026-10-29 with Rust 1.82: `g(format!("{}", s), f(&mut s))` compiles, and `g` is given the string from before the call), against the string itself, under which a call around it holds the read as it holds the variable and is the error; the cost is a change of the count for each interpolation of one hole of a string made while the program runs).
  - Classes stay references with identity and change only through their own `mut` fields.
  - A handle of section 12, a `File`, a `TcpListener` or a `TcpStream`, is the exception to the copy of a value: a copy of it is a second holder of the one handle, and a `Close` through one holder closes it for every other (section 12, decided there).

```
mut order = GetOrder(id)
order.Customer.Address.City = "Wien"     // short for three nested `with`, done in place
users[id].Name = name                    // the same inside a map held by an actor

void Fill(List<int> xs) { xs.Add(1) }          // compile error: xs is not mut
void Fill(mut List<int> xs) { xs.Add(1) }
Fill(mut numbers)                              // the caller sees and allows the change
```

- `data`: immutable records, equal by content. The fields compare as the fields of a C# record do: a value with a NaN field equals itself and is found again as a key of a map, while `==` on the field alone says false, as in C# (the owner, 2026-10-09, against the field's `==`, under which such a value is unequal to itself and cannot be found in a map). Each field compares by the equality Kurz gives its type, a class field as the `class` item below says, by identity, by content or by its named fields, and NaN equal to NaN is the one thing taken from C#'s `Equals` (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 13); a class declares no method named `Equals` or `GetHashCode` (the `class` item below, the owner, 2026-10-30), and whether a `data` type may declare one stands open (section 14). A field of a union compares equal when the two values hold the same case and the two values of that case are equal, as Rust's derived `PartialEq` compares a field of an enum and a C# record a field of type `object` (checked 2026-10-23 on .NET 10: a record `Box(object V)` gives `Box(5) == Box(5)` true and `Box(5) == Box(5L)` false) (the owner, 2026-10-23, "Same case, equal value", against no `==` on a `data` type that holds a union, under which comparing two such values takes a `match` on the field; the cost is a compare of the cases before the compare of the values). The two values of that case compare as a field of the case's type does, so a `double` or `float` case that holds NaN equals itself, as a C# record calls `Equals` on a field of type `object` (the owner, 2026-10-24, "Field equality", against the case's `==`, under which such a value is unequal to itself). Rust's derived `PartialEq` is the model for the cases alone: it compares the two values of a case with their `==`, under which NaN is unequal to itself, and Kurz departs from it there (checked 2026-10-24: the standard library's documentation of `PartialEq`, under which a derived one on an enum is equal for the same variant with all fields equal, and floating point has `NaN != NaN`). `==` takes two values of one union type wherever they stand *(assumed: one type, its first case the same and its other cases the same set, as the bullet on unions below makes two unions one type (the owner, 2026-10-21), and as Rust compares two values of one enum, and `!=` as the negation of `==` here, in the refusal below too)*, alone, as a list's items, in a list a field holds and as a map's keys, and compares them as a field of a union compares, the same case, then the two values of that case, as Rust's derived `PartialEq` compares two values of an enum (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Case and value, as Rust", against `==` on a union in a field alone, under which a union alone, a list of union items and a union key are `type-mismatch`, at the cost that a program wraps a union in a `data` type to compare it or to key a map with it; the cost is the emitter's compare of a union alone and of a list of union items, and no program that built before the answer does otherwise): a `double` or a `float` case by IEEE's `==` where the union stands alone or in a list that stands alone, as Rust's `==` compares the case's values, and as C#'s `Equals` inside a `data` value or a key, a union that is a key itself among them (the collections, below) *(assumed: the case of a union key read as a key under the answer "Equals inside data/keys" (below), which named the items of a list that a `data` value or a key holds; a default the product pass of "Case and value, as Rust" took)*, where Kurz departs from Rust as for a field. A union with no `null` case beside a value of one of its cases takes no `==` and no `!=`, on either side, `type-mismatch`, as Rust refuses an enum beside the payload of one of its variants, `Some(5) == 5` and `Ok(5) == 5` on a `Result<i32, String>` alike, a nullable side being lifted first (the bullet on `null` below), so that `(int | string)? a == 1` is `type-mismatch` too, the union the lift leaves standing beside a value of one of its cases, and the shape of `Some(5) == 5` in Kurz, `int? a == 5`, compares as C# lifts it, and a program compares through a `match` or gives the value the union's type first (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Refuse, as Rust", against a value whose type is exactly one of the cases, taken with no widening, at the cost of a rule neither Rust nor C# has and of `int | long a == 1` reading otherwise than `a == 1L`, and against the widening of an assignment, as built before the answer, at the cost of T8's widening, a literal that two number cases could take among it, applied to `==`, and of programs a tightening afterwards breaks; the cost is a `match` or a typed variable where a program compares a union with a value). A `data` type may inherit from another `data` type. Unions (`Circle | Square`) are available as well.
- `class`: may have `mut` fields, single inheritance plus interfaces as in C#. Classes are the tool for big inheritance trees. A class with `mut` fields compares by identity; an immutable class compares by content. A class can replace that by naming the fields that count: two instances are then equal when those fields are equal, and the hash is derived from the same fields (the owner, 2026-10-02). A `double` or `float` field compares as a field of a `data` value does, NaN equal to NaN, among the named fields (the owner, 2026-10-10) and under the content equality of a class without `mut` fields alike, which compares the same fields. This replaces "equality is overridable, as in C#": C# overrides `Equals` of `object`, and Kurz has no type that every value belongs to. The cost: a class cannot bring comparison logic of its own, such as a name compared without regard to case. There are no operator declarations: `+` on two instances is a method such as `a.Add(b)` (the owner, 2026-10-09, against C#'s operator declarations with the comparisons excluded; the cost is that a vector type reads as `a.Add(b).Scale(2)`). `a + b` written on two instances is the error for a value of one type where another is required, the error of an arithmetic operator on operands it has no meaning for (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 13). A method named `Equals` or `GetHashCode` on a class is a compile error whose message points to `equal by`, and `==` stays as this item gives it (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Error on Equals", against Rust's way, `==` only where content equality is declared, as `derive(PartialEq)` declares it, with identity in a form of its own, as Rust's `ptr::eq` and C#'s `ReferenceEquals`, at the cost of a new form and the equality of classes reopened, and against such a method as an ordinary member that changes nothing about `==`, as the record held it, at the cost that a class ported from C# gives two answers without a word; the cost is one check, and two names no method of a class can take); the error's id is `equals-method`, and it stands at the method (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Accept both", which took the id and the line proposed with this record, against names of the owner's own). The fields are named in a clause after the head of the class: `class User(int Id, mut string Name) equal by Id` (the owner, 2026-10-02, against a marker word in front of each field). `equal` and `by` become core words with it. The clause names one or more fields with `, ` between them, stands after the primary constructor and the base part and before a body, may name a field of the primary constructor, of the body or of the base, and a name that is no field of the class is the compile error `equal-by-unknown`. *(assumed: the reference's reading K15, accepted by its id on 2026-10-09 without its text shown)* A `mut` field can be named in the clause (the owner, 2026-10-02, against the lean to refuse it); a class without the clause is not touched by that and compares as before; the answer of 2026-10-30 below, which set aside refusing a `mut` field in the clause, confirms the first half of this reading of the owner's answer, "yes if the equal thingy is overridden otherwise no", that a `mut` field can be named; that a class without the clause is not touched by it is the reading of "otherwise no" *(assumed: the answer of 2026-10-30 does not speak of a class without the clause)*. The cost of a `mut` field that counts: when it is assigned, the hash of the instance changes, so an instance that is a key of a map at that moment would no longer be found under it; such a class as the key type of a `Map` is therefore a compile error, and the developer keys the map by a copy of the field (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Error at the Map key", against no `mut` field in `equal by`, at the cost that such a class compares by its fixed fields alone, against a check in test builds, where a map stores the hash of each key and checks it at a lookup, at the cost of a word for each entry and of a bug that no test reaches staying silent, and against the clause as it stood, at the cost of an entry lost with no report; the cost is one check at the key type); the error's id is `mutable-key`, and it stands at the line that writes the key type (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Accept both", which took the id and the line proposed with this record, against names of the owner's own). Across inheritance: instances of different classes are never equal, whatever their fields hold; the fields of the base count as fields of the class when `mut` decides between identity and content; and the clause of the base is inherited, a clause of the derived class adding its fields to the base's (the owner, 2026-10-03, by choosing the option whose contrast was a derived clause "replacing instead of extending" the base's; against C#'s record equality, which compares the run-time types and every field with a replacing clause, and against equality by the fields of the static type, under which `a == b` and `b == a` can differ). The cost: a base-typed collection cannot find an instance by a base-typed key.
- `actor`: the concurrent unit (section 6). Actors implement interfaces but do not inherit from each other.
- Small `data` values are compiled as plain values without reference counts, automatically; there is no separate `struct` keyword. *(assumed)*
- Numbers use C# names and sizes. The integer types are C#'s set, signed and unsigned: `sbyte`, `byte`, `short`, `ushort`, `int` (32-bit), `uint`, `long` (64-bit), `ulong` (the owner, 2026-10-02; the first list had `byte`, `short`, `int` and `long` only). The others are `float`, `double` and `decimal`. C#'s `nint` and `nuint` are not in the set: a size or an index is an `int` or a `long` (the owner, 2026-10-09, against pointer-sized types whose width follows the target, 32 bits on a device and 64 on a server, under which a constant's overflow would depend on the target and a value of that type could not be sent between machines without naming a width; the cost is that ported interop code writes `long`).
- Integer overflow wraps silently in release builds. Around that:
  - Compile errors for constant expressions that overflow, implicit narrowing (`long` into `int`) and mixing `uint` or `ulong` with a signed type (below). A constant expression under `+%`, `-%` or `*%` folds to the wrapped value instead, as the operator promises for every value, `2147483647 +% 1` to `-2147483648`; a conversion to an integer type written on a constant that does not fit its target, `byte(300)`, is the overflow error, as C# refuses a constant cast that does not fit (the owner, 2026-10-09, against both being the error, under which a checksum written on literals does not compile, and against both computing, under which `byte(300)` is `44` and a value is lost without a word; the cost is a constant folder that knows two kinds of operator). An overflowing constant expression is the error for the integer types and `decimal` alone: one of `float` or `double` folds to the value IEEE 754 gives it, an infinity or NaN included, as C# folds it. A literal whose value lies outside the range of the type it takes is the error whatever that type, a duration (below) included: `1e400` or `3.5e38f` is the error, as C# refuses such a literal, and one that rounds to zero or to a subnormal is that value, as there. A constant fits the target of a written integer conversion when its value, the fraction dropped, lies in the target's range: `int(2.5)` is `2`, and `int(1e10)`, `int(1.0 / 0.0)` and a NaN are the overflow error (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 13). A written conversion to a floating-point type folds as IEEE 754 has it, to an infinity beyond the range and with rounding, never the error (the owner, 2026-10-10). A constant expression of a time type, which counts nanoseconds in an integer, is the error as an integer one is; a written conversion to `decimal` fits when the value, rounded to `decimal`'s precision, lies in its range, and `decimal(1e30)` is the error (the owner, 2026-10-11, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 14).
  - Durations and timestamps are their own 64-bit types, never raw integers. This removes the bug class where an uptime counter overflows after weeks of running. Their names are `duration` and `timestamp`, in lower case like the other built-in types, and one step of their 64 bits is one nanosecond, which holds about 292 years of duration and, counted from 1970-01-01 *(assumed: the zero point)*, timestamps from 1677 to 2262 (the owner, 2026-10-03, against capitalized names like the types of the library, a step of one millisecond and .NET's tick of 100 nanoseconds). The wider variant the owner asked for with that choice is a second pair of 128 bits with the same step, into which the narrow pair widens without a word and from which a conversion is checked as every conversion is (the owner, 2026-10-04, against a 64-bit pair with a millisecond step and against a type of the library), a conversion that loses the value raising in every build since 2026-10-28 (below). Their names, `longduration` and `longtimestamp`, stand to the narrow pair as `long` stands to `int`; an operation between the two pairs widens the narrow operand and has the wide type; and a duration literal is a `duration`, or a `longduration` when its value does not fit one, takes a written or expected type, and is the overflow error when its value does not fit that type or lies beyond both ranges, as an integer literal is (below). *(assumed: the reference's readings under T29, the names, the mixed operations and the type of a literal, accepted by the rule's id on 2026-10-09 without their text shown)* The cost: 128-bit arithmetic, and a second name for every operation on time in the library. `+` and `-` on the durations and timestamps of either pair, and the unary `-` on the durations of either pair, raise the exception `overflow` where the result leaves its type's range, in every build, and so does a written conversion from the wide pair into the narrow one where the value does not fit it (the owner, 2026-10-28, "Raise in every build", asked for the operations and for the conversion, as Rust's `Duration` and `SystemTime` panic and C#'s `TimeSpan` throws in a build without checks, which rustc 1.82 and .NET 10 showed that day, against the integers' rule for the operations, under which a release build wraps and a span or an instant from the other end of the range comes back without a word, and against the wrap of a lost value in a release build for the conversion, the one place a time would change without a word; the cost is a check and a branch on every such operation in a release build). A `longduration` and a `longtimestamp` take 16 bytes aligned to 16, as Rust's `i128` from rustc 1.77 on, C's `__int128` on x86-64 and .NET's `Int128` on Linux, which keeps the 8 of a `long` on Windows (checked 2026-10-29: the Rust blog's post on the change in 1.77 and the .NET runtime's pull request 69204) (the owner, 2026-10-28, "16 bytes, 16-aligned", against the 8 of a `decimal`, under which a data value that holds one is laid out otherwise than C lays out a structure with an `__int128`, which C code reading it through a pointer meets; the cost is up to 8 bytes of padding in a data value that holds one).
  - Test builds throw on overflow instead of wrapping. An explicit wrapping operator exists for intended cases such as hashes: `+%`, `-%` and `*%`, which wrap in every build (the owner, 2026-10-02).
  - A project keeps those checks in its release build by a setting in its `project.kz`, as Cargo's `overflow-checks = true` keeps them in a release profile; without the setting a release build wraps (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Project setting", against no setting, at the cost that a bug no test reached wraps in production, and against checks in every build with `+%` for wrapping, at the cost of a compare and a branch for each integer operation, against the speed goal of section 13; the cost is one setting and one switch in the emitter). *(assumed: that the setting is named `overflow-checks`, Cargo's key, with the form of the other settings of `project.kz`, and that it holds for the whole program, the code of its packages among it, as Cargo reads the profiles of the workspace's root alone and applies them to every package it builds; the name and the reach were taken at their defaults and not put to the owner)*
  - Tests can fast-forward virtual time (section 11).
- Around numbers, from the rounds of 2026-10-02:
  - Number literals take every form C# gives them: decimal digits, `0x` and `0b` digits, the prefix letter in either case and `_` allowed directly after the prefix as well, `_` between digits, a fraction, with or without digits before the `.`, and an exponent (`1e3`) for a `double`, and the C# suffixes in upper or lower case, except that a suffix holding a lower-case `l` is a compile error, because `1l` reads as `11`, where C# only warns (the owner, 2026-10-09, against the forms listed before alone, under which `1e-9` is written out in full; the cost is that the lexer is C#'s).
  - An integer literal is an `int`, or a `long` when its value does not fit, or a `ulong` when a `long` does not, without C#'s step through `uint` (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reference's reading L10 and its ladder); where an integer type is written or expected it takes that type *(assumed)*, and is the overflow error below when the value does not fit it; where the written type is no number type, `string s = 300`, the literal is the error for a value of one type where another is required, as any value is *(assumed: it follows from the type error of this section and was not put to the owner)*. A written or expected type reaches every literal of a constant expression, so `long big = 2147483647 + 1` is a `long`, and a literal that is one operand of an operator whose other operand has a type takes that type, so the `1` of `u + 1` with a `uint` is a `uint` and the `100` of `sbyte low = -100` is an `sbyte` (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reference's reading L17, which round 13 had not asked about; C# converts the constant `int` instead, which Kurz cannot do across signedness). The promotion of narrow operands below is for the operands of a run-time operation. A constant expression is folded in the type its literals take, one operation at a time, each operation by the rule of its own operator: under `+ - * / %` an operation whose result leaves an integer or `decimal` type is the constant-overflow error, where a floating-point one folds to its IEEE 754 value (above), and under `+%`, `-%` and `*%` it wraps in that type. So `sbyte low = -100` and `byte c = 200 + 50` compile, `byte c = 200 + 100` is the error, and so is `byte c = 200 + 100 - 100`, whose result would fit but whose first sum does not; `byte b = 255 +% 1` is `0`, and `2147483647 + 1 +% 0`, with no written type and so in `int`, is the error at the `+`. A literal that does not fit the type it takes is the constant-overflow error wherever it stands: `byte c = 300`, never an `int` narrowed, and `b + 300` with a `byte` `b`, whose `300` takes `byte` by the sentence above, as much as `sum +% 300` (below); the cost is that `b * 1000` on a `byte` is written `int(b) * 1000`. Kurz departs from C# here, where a constant expression has the type of its operands, `int` for `2147483647 + 1` whatever the written type, and overflows there (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 13). The target of a written conversion reaches the integer literals inside it, so `long(2147483647 + 1)` is `2147483648`, while a fraction or exponent literal inside it keeps its own type and the conversion applies to the folded value, so `float(1e300)` is an infinity (above) and `int(2.5)` is `2` (the owner, 2026-10-11, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 14); a `-` written directly before a literal, with no operand before the `-`, belongs to the literal, so `sbyte x = -128` and `int x = -2147483648` compile and `n-1` stays a subtraction; a written floating-point or `decimal` type reaches integer literals as well, so `double d = 1 / 2` is `0.5`, where C# divides the integers first; a fraction or exponent literal beside an integer operand keeps its type, and the integer operand widens to it, so `b + 1.5` with a `byte` is a `double`; and literals of different types in one constant expression fold in the type `+` would find for them at run time (the owner, 2026-10-10). Under a written or expected type every literal takes it first, so `float f = 1 + 2.5` is `3.5` as a `float` and `float f = 1e300` the overflow error of a literal outside its type's range, and a fraction literal under a written integer type, `int i = 1 + 2.5`, is the error for a value of one type where another is required, as `string s = 300` is (the owner, 2026-10-11, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 14).
  - A narrower integer becomes a wider one without a word when the wider type holds every value of the narrower, as in C#: within one signedness, and across it where C# widens (`byte` into `short`, `int` and `long`, `ushort` into `int` and `long`, `uint` into `long`); every integer type becomes `float`, `double` or `decimal` without a word, rounded to the nearest value the type holds where it does not hold every value, as in C#, and that rounding is not a loss in the sense of the conversion rule below; a signed type never becomes unsigned without a word (the owner, 2026-10-03, against widening within one signedness only, under which `int x = b` with a `byte` needs `int(b)`). The cost: `b + i` with a `byte` and an `int` is an `int`, and `float f = i` rounds without a word where `float(i)` is checked. Where the two rules meet: an operation finds its common type as C# does, except where C# joins a signed with an unsigned operand through `long` or refuses the pair, which is `uint` or `ulong` with a signed type; there Kurz reports the error for mixing signed with unsigned. So `u + i` with a `uint` and an `int` is the error where C# computes a `long`, while `sb + b` with an `sbyte` and a `byte` is an `int` and `u + b` with a `uint` and a `byte` is a `uint`, as there. The error looks at the two operand types as written, before any promotion: a `byte` or a `ushort` beside a `uint` becomes a `uint` and beside a `ulong` a `ulong`, as C#'s binary numeric promotion makes it, not an `int` first (the promotion bullet below). (the owner, 2026-10-04, against C#'s `long` for `uint` with `int`) Where a union is required, a value that is no case of it stands there when it widens into exactly one of its cases, so a `byte` stands where `int | string` is required; one that would widen into two of them, a `byte` where `short | int` is required, is the error for a value of one type where another is required, and a conversion such as `short(b)` picks the case, by the same rule that makes a call that fits two functions through widening an error (section 8) (the owner, 2026-10-21, against the narrowest case, under which the `byte` becomes a `short` by C#'s better-conversion rule, and against no widening into unions, under which the value must be one of the cases exactly, as Rust widens nothing implicitly; the cost is a conversion at such places). A value that is one of the cases, a `data` value among them, is that case, and no other fit by widening or derivation is looked for, so a `short` where `short | int` is required is a `short`, and a `User` where `User | Person` is required, with `User : Person`, is a `User` (the owner, 2026-10-21, against a value that would also go to another case by widening or derivation being the error for a value of one type where another is required, as two fits are, under which that `User` is that error too; C#'s better-conversion rule prefers the identity conversion, and Rust has only the exact fit; the cost is that adding a case to a union can change without a word which case an existing value goes to, as a `User` goes to `Person` where `int | Person` is required and to `User` where `int | Person | User` is, while under the option turned down that addition is a compile error; the owner kept this answer with that cost shown on 2026-10-21). A value that would go to two cases by deriving from them, an `Admin` where `User | Person` is required with `Admin : User : Person`, is that error too, and a case that leaves a function through a call and would go to two of the cases after the first of its return type is the error for a case the return type does not list (section 5); a conversion picks the case for a number, a variable of the case's type for a `data` value, as `User u = admin` makes an `Admin` a `User`, and a `match` on the call for a propagated case (the owner, 2026-10-21, against the nearest case, the most derived base or the narrowest type, as C#'s better-conversion rule picks it, under which derivation and propagation would answer differently from widening; the cost is that such code needs that conversion, variable or `match`). *(assumed: the variable; the answers named a conversion or a `match`, and a `data` value has no conversion to its base)* A literal where a union is required whose cases it would widen into twice has its own type, `int` for `5`, so `long | double x = 5` is that error, and `5L` or `5.0` picks the case, as a literal argument has its own type for the choice between functions (section 8), so that the case does not depend on the order the cases are written in (the owner, 2026-10-21, against the first case in the written order that can hold it, as a written or expected type reaches a literal (above), under which the case depends on the order and on the value; the cost is a suffix at such places). A union with exactly one number case, a case of a number type (an integer type, `float`, `double` or `decimal`), reaches a number literal, an integer literal or one with a fraction or an exponent, as a written type does, so `byte | string x = 5` makes the `5` a `byte`; where it has two or more number cases the literal has its own type, so `byte | short x = 5`, whose `int` widens into neither case, is that error too, and `byte(5)` picks the case (the owner, 2026-10-21, against every number case the literal fits reaching it, with two fits the error, under which `byte | short x = 300` is a `short` and the case depends on the literal's value, and against the literal's own type even beside one number case, under which `byte | string x = 5` needs a conversion; the cost is a conversion at such places, as for a literal whose own type would widen into two cases). The union's one time case, a case of a time type (`duration`, `timestamp`, `longduration` or `longtimestamp`), reaches a duration literal as a written type does, whatever number cases the union has, so `int | duration d = 5days` makes the literal a `duration` and `duration | string d = 106752days` is the overflow error; with two or more time cases the literal keeps its own type, a `duration`, or a `longduration` when its value does not fit one (the owner, 2026-10-21, against the literal's own type always, under which that second example is the error for a value of one type where another is required; the cost is the same conversion at such places as for a number literal).
  - A conversion on purpose is the type's name used as a function: `int(value)`. A conversion that loses the value behaves as overflow does: it wraps in a release build and throws in a test build (the owner, 2026-10-02), but for a time value narrowed into the 64-bit pair, which throws in every build (above), and for a conversion into `decimal`, which throws in every build as well (below); this is for a value the program computes, since a conversion to an integer type written on a constant that does not fit is the compile error above, and one to a floating-point type folds as section 4 says. From a `double`, `float` or `decimal` source into an integer type, a dropped fraction is no loss: `int(2.5)` is `2`, toward zero, as in C#. A value outside the target's range, an infinity and NaN lose the value: a test build throws, and a release build gives the target's largest or smallest value for an out-of-range value and an infinity, and `0` for NaN (the owner, 2026-10-03, against wrapping the integer part as an integer source wraps, and against counting a dropped fraction as a loss). The saturation is Kurz's own definition: C# leaves the result unspecified, .NET saturates on every platform since .NET 9, and for a `decimal` source C# throws in every build. So "wraps" describes an integer source and "saturates" a floating-point or `decimal` one. Between the floating-point types and `decimal` the IEEE 754 rules of C# hold: a `double` into a `float` rounds and becomes an infinity beyond the range, NaN stays NaN and an infinity stays an infinity *(assumed: the `float` and `double` targets, which the question of 2026-10-03 did not name)*. Into `decimal`, which has neither, NaN, an infinity and a value beyond its range raise the overflow exception in every build, a release build too, as C# throws for them (the owner, 2026-10-28, "Raise in every build", against saturating in a release build and throwing in a test build as an integer target does, which this bullet assumed before; the cost is a release program that ends with an error line instead of a clamped amount). A `double` becomes its exact binary value rounded half to even at the largest scale up to 28 whose digits fit `decimal`'s 96 bits, its zeros at the end removed down to the scale 0: `decimal(0.1)` is `0.1000000000000000055511151231` (the owner, 2026-10-28, "Exact binary value", against the shortest digits that read back as the same `double`, `0.1`, as the Rust crate rust_decimal's `from_f64` gives them, and against the 15 significant digits C# keeps; the cost is surprising digits for almost every `double`; rust_decimal's `from_f64_retain` comes near it and differs in the last digits, `123.456` giving `123.45600000000000306954461843` where the exact value rounds to `123.45600000000000306954461848`, checked on 2026-10-28 with rust_decimal 1.36) *(assumed: the rounding half to even, the largest scale whose digits fit and the zeros removed at the end, readings of the option's "rounded to 28 digits")*; that rounding is no loss. A `float` becomes the `double` that holds it exactly, and then that *(assumed: the answer named the exact binary value, which the `double` holds; C# keeps 7 significant digits of a `float`)*. A zero that comes of the conversion has no sign, `decimal(-0.0)` included *(assumed: the reading of the answer "None, as rust_decimal", under which only the unary `-` gives a zero its sign)*. A `decimal` becomes a `double` as C# computes it: the low 64 bits of its 96-bit integer as a `double`, plus its high 32 bits times 2^64 as a `double`, divided by the `double` nearest to 10 to the power of its scale, as .NET's `VarR8FromDec` computes it, so `double(1m / 3m)` is `0.33333333333333337` (the owner, 2026-10-28, "As C# computes it", against the nearest `double`, `0.3333333333333333`; the cost is a last bit that can differ from the nearest `double`), and a `float` as that `double` rounded, as C# computes it *(assumed)*.
  - `+ - * / %`, the comparisons and `&& || !` have the meaning and the precedence they have in C#. Integer division drops the fraction, toward zero. *(assumed)* `+%` and `-%` take the precedence of `+` and `-`, and `*%` that of `*` *(assumed: proposed on 2026-10-09 and not objected to)*, and the associativity of those operators as well (the owner, 2026-10-10). They take integer operands only: a floating-point or `decimal` operand, `d +% 1.0` or `sum +% 1.5` on a `byte`, whose `1.5` makes the sum a `double`, is the error for a value of one type where another is required (the owner, 2026-10-11, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 14). `%` by zero raises as `/` by zero does; a floating-point division by zero yields an infinity, or NaN for `0.0 / 0.0`, as IEEE 754 and C# have it; and a division or remainder of literals by the literal `0` (`10 / 0`) is a compile error of its own, because that division can never succeed (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reference's reading T25, which round 13 had not asked about). That error reaches further than the confirmed sentence: it is for a divisor that is a constant zero whatever the dividend, `x / 0` with a run-time `x` and `1 / (1 - 1)` with a folded divisor included, as C# reports a constant divisor of zero; it is for the integer types and `decimal`, as there; and a floating-point division by a constant zero is no error and yields the infinity or NaN above, as there (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 13). An operand narrower than `int` is promoted to `int` before `+ - * / %`, unary `-`, `~`, `& | ^`, `<<`, `>>` and the comparisons, as in C#: the sum of two `byte`s is an `int`, a shift on one has the width 32, and a negative shift count is masked as there; where the other operand is a `uint`, a `long` or a `ulong`, the narrow operand takes that type instead of `int`, as C#'s binary numeric promotion does, so that `u + b` is a `uint` and `l + b` a `long` (the owner, 2026-10-03, against computing in the operands' type). The cost: `byte c = a + b` needs `byte(a + b)`; an overflow never happens at 8 or 16 bits, but `ushort * ushort` is computed in a signed `int` and can overflow it. The wrapping operators `+%`, `-%` and `*%` do not promote: they compute in the wider of their operand types and wrap there, so that an 8-bit checksum is `a +% b` on two `byte`s. (the owner, 2026-10-04, against promoting them, which would put a mask on every narrow checksum; C# has no such operators) Under the wrapping operators, which do not promote, operands of different signedness are the error for mixing signed with unsigned, whatever their widths, and a literal operand takes the type of the other operand, so that `sum +% 1` on a `byte` computes in `byte` (the owner, 2026-10-09, confirming the reference's reading T28). A literal that does not fit that type, `sum +% 300` on a `byte`, is the constant-overflow error, as a literal that does not fit a written type is (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 13).
  - A `decimal` is C#'s System.Decimal: an integer of 96 bits, a scale from 0 to 28 that counts its digits after the point, and a sign, in 16 bytes. `+ - * / %` compute, round half to even and choose the scale of their result as System.Decimal does, but for the remainders below, so `1.50m * 2` is `3.00`, `1m / 3m` is `0.3333333333333333333333333333` and `5m / 2m` is `2.5`; `%` takes the sign of the dividend, but for a zero, which has none (below); `==` and the comparisons compare the values, so `1.5m == 1.50m` *(assumed: T2 names C#'s type; Rust has no `decimal`, and its crate rust_decimal chooses other scales, `2.50m * 0` is `0` there where System.Decimal keeps `0.00`, so the two do not agree, and Kurz takes C#'s)*. A result beyond ±79228162514264337593543950335 raises the overflow exception in every build, a release build too, as C# throws for it (the owner, 2026-10-28, "Raise in every build", against raising in a test build and saturating in a release build; the cost is nothing at run time beyond what the rounding checks anyway, and a release program ends with an error line instead of a wrong amount). A zero that `+`, the binary `-`, `*`, `/` and `%` compute has no sign: `1.50m - 1.5m` shows `0.00` and `0m * -1m` shows `0`; only the unary `-`, which turns the sign, gives a zero its sign, as `-(0.0m)` shows `-0.0` (section 8), and the digits and the scale stay System.Decimal's, `2.50m * 0` showing `0.00` (the owner, 2026-10-28, "None, as rust_decimal", against System.Decimal's sign bit, which it sets on `1.50m - 1.5m`, so that the text of section 8 would show `-0.00`, and against no sign on any zero, as C# shows none; the cost is a test for zero after each operation, and a sign bit that differs from C#'s where no `==` and no text of C# shows it). `%` gives the exact remainder at the larger of the two scales, also where System.Decimal throws `OverflowException` for a remainder that fits: `4294967295m % 0.9999999999999999999999999999m` is `0.0000000000000000004294967295` (the owner, 2026-10-28, "Exact remainder", against raising the overflow exception where C# throws, under which the runtime would mirror the widths System.Decimal computes in and a remainder that fits would raise; the cost is nothing at run time, and the result differs from C#'s only where C# throws; rust_decimal 1.36 gives `0.9232812099469197934124335104` there).
  - `&`, `|`, `^`, `~`, `<<` and `>>` work on the bits of integers, with the meaning and the precedence they have in C#. Between two types `|` still makes a union; between two values it combines bits. (the owner, 2026-10-02)
  - A duration or a size is a number with a unit from a fixed list: `5min`, `30s`, `256kb`. The list is `ns`, `us`, `ms`, `s`, `min`, `h` and `days` for a duration, and `kb`, `mb` and `gb` for a number of bytes, in steps of 1024. (the owner, 2026-10-02; `ns` and `us` the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "Add us and ns", as Rust's `Duration` has both and C#'s `TimeSpan` has its microseconds (checked 2026-10-21: Rust's `Duration::from_micros` and `Duration::from_nanos`, and .NET's `TimeSpan.FromMicroseconds`, since .NET 7), against the units as they were, under which a duration's text cannot always be read back as a literal, in the question's words, since the units it writes below a millisecond, `us` and `ns` (section 8), were no units of a literal; the cost is two more unit words) The number before the unit is a decimal integer or a decimal fraction, in the forms the number literals above have, `.5h` included: `1.5s` is 1500 ms and `0.5h` is 30 min. A fraction that does not fall on a whole nanosecond, or on a whole byte before `kb`, `mb` and `gb`, is the compile error `inexact-literal`; a hexadecimal, a binary or a suffixed literal before a unit is the compile error `syntax`, the one for text no rule gives a meaning (the owner, 2026-10-09, against a decimal integer alone, under which one and a half seconds is written `1500ms`; the cost is a row in the error table and a decimal-to-nanosecond conversion in the compiler). A number with an exponent before a unit is that error as well, since the number before a unit is a decimal integer or a decimal fraction and nothing else; and the unit is read before any suffix, the longest unit that matches, so that `1ms` is a millisecond and not `1m` followed by `s`, and only a suffix the writer spells before a unit, `1Ums`, is the error (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 13); by that reading `1us` is a microsecond and not `1u` followed by `s`. A duration literal beyond 64 bits of nanoseconds is a `longduration`, and one beyond both ranges the compile error `constant-overflow`, by the reading of a duration literal under the wide pair above, which stands assumed there.
- Strings are UTF-8 and immutable; there is no flag to change the encoding. Indexing goes through `.Bytes` or `.Chars`. `.Chars` yields Unicode code points, one element per code point; graphemes are left to a library (the owner, 2026-10-02). - Generics as in C# (`Map<int, User>`), lambdas as in C# (`x => x * 2`), primary constructors (`class User(string Name, mut int Age)`). A type argument a call does not write is inferred from the arguments: a type parameter takes the one type every argument in its positions has; different types there, or no argument in its positions, are the compile error `cannot-infer`, and the type arguments are written (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reference's reading T27, which round 13 had not asked about; C# would widen a `byte` to an `int` to make `Max(b, i)` an `int`, which the conversion rules above do not allow, and C#'s inference has changed between its versions, so no version of it is named). A literal argument takes the type the other arguments in the parameter's positions fix, so `Max(b, 1)` with a `byte` infers `byte`; two instances of different classes with one base are `cannot-infer`, since no common base is searched for (the owner, 2026-10-10). When literals alone stand in a parameter's positions they take the type a constant expression of them has, so `Pick(1, 2)` infers `int` and `Pick(1, 2.5)` `double`; a literal that does not fit the fixed type, `Pick(small, 300)` with a `byte`, is the overflow error, as such a literal is wherever it stands; and where one argument's class is the base of the other's, `Pick(Dog(), Animal())`, the parameter takes the base, which every argument has (the owner, 2026-10-11, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 14).

- Class bodies follow C# (the owner, 2026-10-02): a class names its base class and its interfaces after `:`, an interface is declared with `interface`, and further constructors, `static` members and `override` are written as there: a method that a derived class overrides is marked `virtual` in the base and `override` in the derived class, `override` on a method the base does not mark `virtual` is a compile error, and so is a method of a derived class with the name and the parameters of a base method but without `override` (the owner, 2026-10-04, against every method being overridable without a marker, which would differ from C#). As in C#, a method marked `override` can be overridden again in a class further down, the nearest declaration in the whole chain of bases is the one that has to be `virtual` or `override`, and a method of an interface the class implements is no base method and takes no `override`. A method that implements a method of an interface the class names is written `pub`, as C# demands `public` of an implicit implementation: a class that names an interface and lacks one of its methods is the compile error `missing-member`, at the class, naming the method, and a method that is there without `pub` is `interface-method-private`, at the method (the owner, 2026-10-09, against `pub` implied by the interface, under which a method that looks private is public, and against one id for both mistakes). A method of the class implements an interface method when its name, its parameter types in their order and its return type are the interface's; a `pub` method inherited from the base class counts, as in C# (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 13). One inherited without `pub` does not count: the class is `missing-member` (the owner, 2026-10-10), and implements the interface with a `pub` method of its own, which hides nothing, since a method the class cannot see is hidden by nothing (below). Where Kurz departs from C#: hiding is an error, not a warning, and C#'s `new` has no counterpart; the cost is that adding a method to a base class breaks every derived class, in every package, that already has a method of that name and those parameters. There is no `sealed`, so every override can be overridden again, and an override has the return type of the method it overrides (the owner, 2026-10-09, confirming the reference's reading, and on 2026-10-10 the exact return type against the covariant return of C# 9; C#'s `sealed override` was not offered as an option); an override whose return type differs is the compile error `override-return-type`, at the method (the owner, 2026-10-09, against the general type error at the method's line, whose message would name no override). A constructor, and only a constructor, assigns each field without `mut` once before its body ends, as C# lets a constructor set a `readonly` field; a further constructor of a class that has a primary constructor calls the primary one first, as C# 12 requires, and a class without one has C#'s constructors (the owner, 2026-10-03, against a class with a primary constructor having no further constructor). The cost: the flow analysis that proves "once, before the end". The once is per instance, stricter than C#, which allows any number of assignments: a field that the primary constructor or an `=` in the body sets is assigned by no constructor, and a field without `mut` and without `=` is assigned exactly once on every path of every constructor that does not chain; such a field cannot exist in a class with a primary constructor, which a call of that constructor would leave unset. A further constructor without the call of the primary one, and a path that leaves such a field unassigned or reads it first, are compile errors. A `mut` field without `=` is assigned on every path of such a constructor as well, any number of times, because Kurz has no zero value that it could hold otherwise (the owner, 2026-10-09, confirming the reference's reading; what it rules out is a `mut` field that starts at a zero value as a C# field does, which Kurz has not). Reading such a field before its first assignment is that error too (the owner, 2026-10-10). In a class with a primary constructor, which assigns no such field, a `mut` field written between the braces without `=` is the compile error `field-unassigned`, as a field without `mut` is (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 13). A constructor written in the body is private without `pub`, as a member is; the primary constructor, and the empty constructor of a class without one, are visible wherever the class is. (the owner, 2026-10-04, against C#'s rules, under which a constructor assigns any number of times and an unassigned field gets the type's zero value, `default(T)`, which Kurz has nowhere else) A `static` field exists once for the program, so it holds an immutable value only: `static mut`, and a static field whose type is or holds a class, are compile errors, because a `mut` field would be state that every actor reaches, against section 6, and a class instance, mutable or not, lives in one actor's heap, which no other actor reaches into (the owner, 2026-10-03, against one copy per actor). *(assumed: that the heap is the reason for an immutable class and for a value such as a `List<Button>`, and that a static field of a generic class exists once per type argument, as in C#)* A field is written `Type name`, a method like a function. There is no property syntax. A method that changes its own value carries `mut` in front of its return type: `mut void Add(T item)`. That marker is for values: a method of a class assigns the `mut` fields of its instance without one and can be called through every reference (the owner, 2026-10-02). The cost: a call on a class instance does not show whether it changes the instance. A `data` type takes its methods in a body of the same form. *(assumed: the last sentence)*
