## 5. Outcomes and errors

The language enforces the C# `OneOf<>` pattern: every method declares every outcome that can happen under normal use.

- The return type is a union. The **first case is the success case**. A value of a union stands where another union is required when its first case goes to the first case of that union and each of its other cases to a case of it after the first, each by being that type, by widening into it (section 4) or by deriving from it, so an `A | B` stands where `A | B | C` is required, and where `B | A` is required it is the error for a value of one type where another is required (section 4), as in Rust `Result<A, B>` and `Result<B, A>` are two types with no conversion between them (the owner, 2026-10-21, against accepting it wherever the other union lists all of its cases, in whatever order, under which the success case of a value changes with the type it is put into; the cost is that swapping the order takes a `match`). So `long | NotFound r = Find(1)` compiles where `Find` returns an `int | NotFound`, while a `User | Admin` where `User | NotFound` is required is that error, since its `Admin` would become a success (honest signatures, below) (the owner, 2026-10-21, against the first case being the same type while the other cases may widen, as Rust's `Result<i32, E>` is no `Result<i64, E>`, under which `long | NotFound r = Find(1)` is refused while `long r = Find(1)` compiles, and against exact types everywhere, with no widening or derivation between unions; the cost is that a conversion between unions checks each case). A case after the first that would go to two of the cases after the first is that error too, as a value that would go to two cases is (section 4). *(assumed: the answer on the cases of a union sends each case after the first to one case after the first, and the answer on two fits named a value and a propagated case, not a case of a union)* A case after the first that is one of the cases after the first goes there all the same, and no other fit by widening or derivation is looked for, so an `int | short` stands where `int | short | long` is required (the owner, 2026-10-21, as for a value (section 4), against two fits being that error even beside an exact case, under which such a union needs a `match`; the cost is that adding a case to a union can change without a word which case an existing union's case goes to, as the `short` of an `int | short` goes to `long` where `int | long` is required and to `short` where `int | long | short` is, while under the option turned down that addition is a compile error; the owner kept this answer with that cost shown on 2026-10-21). Two unions with the same first case and the same other cases are one type, whatever the order of the cases after the first, so `List<A | B | C>` and `List<A | C | B>` are one type (the owner, 2026-10-21, against two types, the order being part of the type as a generic type is invariant in C#, under which a list of one cannot be passed where the other is required; the cost is that the compiler orders the cases after the first itself, by the numbers of their tags (below), and that a reader sees one type written two ways). A case's tag is the number its type has in the program, one number for each type of the program, so that the order the compiler gives the cases after the first is that of those numbers, and a case that goes to its own type in another union keeps its tag (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 19, "Program-wide type number", against the cases after the first sorted by their full type names, at the cost of a table lookup in each conversion between two unions whose cases sit at other places and of numbers that move when a type is renamed, and against the order of the types' declarations, at the cost of the same lookup and of numbers that move when a declaration moves to another file; the cost is that the numbers may differ between two builds of a program, so that a message between nodes of different builds names its case another way, which the format of such a message decides and this answer does not). How the compiler gives out the numbers is its own, and no form of the language outside `raw` shows them, while code that reads a tag through a pointer, the runtime's among it, reads the numbers of its own build; the numbers are given out when the whole program is compiled, as every program is (section 2); a tag is as wide as the largest number among its union's cases needs, a byte while a program has at most 256 types, so that a union may take a wider tag than a count of its own cases would need, a cost the question did not name; a case that widens or derives into another case of the union it goes to takes that case's number, with its value converted (section 4) *(assumed: proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*.
- **Unwrap rule:** calling such a method yields the success value directly. Every other case leaves the calling method and goes to its caller.
- **Honest signatures:** a propagated case must be listed in the calling method's own signature. Otherwise it is a compile error. It is listed as a case after the first one, which is the success case: a return type that is no union lists none, so `NotFound Lost()` whose body calls `int | NotFound Find()` and lets its `NotFound` propagate is that error, as Rust's `?` fills only the `Err` side (the owner, 2026-10-21, against a propagated case leaving as the success of the calling function, which a return type that is the case itself, or that names it first, would let it do; the cost is that such a function needs a `match` on the call to return the case). A case after the first lists a propagated case that is its type and one that widens into it, as section 4 widens a value (the owner, 2026-10-21, with the answer on the success type below, whose propagated `short` widens into `int`, against the options and at the cost that answer names). It also lists one that derives from it. *(assumed: the derivation, which the answers did not name)* A propagated case that would go to two of the cases after the first, a `byte` under the return type `string | short | int`, is that compile error, and a `match` on the call picks the case (the owner, 2026-10-21, against the nearest case, for the reasons and at the cost section 4 names). A propagated case that is one of the cases after the first goes there, and no other fit is looked for, so a propagated `short` under the return type `string | short | int` is listed by its `short` (the owner, 2026-10-21, as for a value (section 4), against two fits being that compile error even beside an exact case, under which such a function needs a `match` on the call; the cost is that adding a case to a return type can change without a word which case an existing propagated case leaves as, as a propagated `short` leaves as the `int` under `string | int` and as the `short` under `string | int | short`, while under the option turned down that addition is a compile error; the owner kept this answer with that cost shown on 2026-10-21). A propagated case is looked for among the cases after the first only, so in `short | int F()` calling `bool | short G()` the `short` widens into `int` and leaves as `F`'s `int` case (the owner, 2026-10-21, against that compile error, with a `match` on the call picking the case; Rust's `?` converts the error into the error type of the calling function and never looks at its success type; the cost is that a propagated case that is exactly the success type leaves as another case, by widening, without a word).
- A union of cases can be given a name, and the name stands in a signature for its cases, so that a function deep in an application names the cases that pass through it in one word (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Named unions", against every case listed, at the cost of long signatures that change with each new case at a leaf, against an erased case that every case widens into, as Rust's `anyhow::Error` or C#'s `Exception`, at the cost that a `match` cannot list what is inside and of an allocation for each erased case, and against cases inferred for a function without `pub`, at the cost of a signature that changes from a distance; the cost is one form of declaration and nothing at run time, and `match` stays exhaustive).
- In a test build a propagated case carries the line of each hop, the call through which it leaves a function, kept beside the case from the line tables that the trace of section 6 reads; a release build keeps none (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Hop lines in test builds", against no place, as Rust's `?` carries none, at the cost that a developer adds fields where a place matters, and against a convention of a `Cause` in the standard library, as C#'s `InnerException` and Rust's `Error::source`, at the cost of a field on every case that wraps another, which nothing checks; the cost is, in a test build, a store at each hop into a list of lines that grows by one with each hop, a pass in the compiler, and nothing in a release build).
- To keep all cases instead of propagating, either `match` the call directly or give the variable an explicit union type. A written union type holds every case the call can return: one that lists only some of them is the compile error for a value of one type where another is required, as an assignment in C# must fit its type; the other cases are kept by naming them, or handled by a `match` (the owner, 2026-10-09, against a written type as a filter that lets the missing cases leave the function, under which a declaration ends the function on some paths without a word; the cost is that a function with five cases forces a five-case type on every variable that keeps its result). A written type that is the success case itself, `User?` for a call that returns `User? | DbError`, names no other case and unwraps as the rule above says; one that names any case beyond the success case names them all (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 13). A written type the success case converts to, `long` for an `int` success or `User?` for a `User` success, counts as the success case itself: the call unwraps and the value widens as in an assignment (the owner, 2026-10-11, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 14).
- A postfix `else` block handles selected cases; unlisted ones still propagate. An arm can recover with a value, transform and return another case, or `throw`.
- **Exceptions** are only for situations the called method truly cannot recover from. There is no `catch`. An exception kills the actor it happens in (section 6).
- **`match` lists every case** of the union; a missing case is a compile error. A default arm is written `else`. An arm can also test a literal, and `match` can be used as an expression that yields the value of the arm that ran. There are no patterns over fields. (the owner, 2026-10-02) When a value fits several arms, the first arm in source order whose type the value is runs, as C#'s `switch` does; an arm may name a type derived from a case (`Admin` where the case is `User`), and an arm that can never run, because an earlier arm covers its type, is the compile error `unreachable-arm` (the owner, 2026-10-09, against arms that name cases only, under which a `match` cannot tell an `Admin` from a `User`; the cost is a row in the error table and a subtype check per arm). An `else` after arms that cover every case, and a literal arm after an arm for its whole type, are `unreachable-arm` too (the owner, 2026-10-10). A literal arm after an arm with the same literal is that error as well, as C# reports a pattern an earlier one handles; an arm that names a type that is no case of the union and derived from none, `string s` in a `match` over `Yes | No`, is the error for a value of one type where another is required, at the arm, as C# refuses a pattern of a type the value cannot have (the owner, 2026-10-11, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 14).
- **Top-level code has no caller**, so a call there has to handle every case that is not success; letting one propagate is a compile error. (the owner, 2026-10-02)
- **`throw` takes a value or a text**: `throw ConfigMissing(path)` or, as the short form, `throw "no config"` (the owner, 2026-10-02). It is a statement and can stand wherever one can. The bare `throw` of an `else` arm stays. What a supervisor reads as the `Reason` of a crashed child (section 6) is that value. The type of `Reason` is the union of everything the child's code can throw, found over the whole program (the owner, 2026-10-03, against a fixed `Reason` type that carries the thrown value's text and the place, and against limiting `throw` to `data` values and text). The cost: the type of `c.Reason` changes when a function the child calls changes what it throws, and a `match` over it has to follow. The runtime's own errors (overflow, a conversion that lost its value, division by zero, an index out of range, a reversed range, an exhausted stack, the last by the owner's choice of 2026-10-09 in section 6: the run-time errors of the reference's chapter 12) are values of one `data` type of the runtime that names the error, and that type is a member of every `Reason` in every build, although a test build raises more of them than a release build. The place and the chain id are fields of the `Crashed` case beside `Reason`, not part of the thrown value. A thrown class instance is moved out of the dying child's heap into the supervisor's, with everything it reaches, before the heap is freed; the cost is that walk, once per crash. (the owner, 2026-10-04, against a compile error for a thrown class instance)
- The compiler lists, for every `pub` function, the exceptions it can raise, in the editor and in the package's documentation, from the `Reason` it computes over the whole program (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Compiler lists them", against nothing shown, at the cost that a package's `throw` reaches its caller unseen, against a checked `throws` clause in the signature, at the cost of signatures that change with every new `throw`, against this section's rule that exceptions are for what a method cannot recover from, and against the list with a project setting that refuses a package whose `pub` functions throw more than the runtime's own errors, at the cost of a rule like a grant to keep; the cost is compiler output and no change to the language). *(assumed: that a package's documentation lists what its own code and its dependencies can raise, and, for a call through an interface or a function value, that it raises what the implementer or the function it is given raises, since the program that supplies them is not known where the package is built)*
- **The success case may be `void`**: `void | NotFound Remove(int id)`; the call is then a statement. (the owner, 2026-10-02)

```
data User(int Id, string Name, string? Email)
data NotFound

actor UserStore {
    mut users = Map<int, User>()

    pub User | NotFound Get(int id) => users[id] ?? NotFound
}

pub User | NotFound | Invalid Rename(UserStore store, int id, string name) {
    if name == "" { return Invalid }
    user = store.Get(id)                 // user is a User; NotFound went to the caller
    return user with { Name = name }
}

// keep all cases
User | NotFound result = store.Get(id)

match store.Get(id) {
    User u   => print(u.Name)
    NotFound => print("none")
}

// handle selected cases
user = store.Get(id) else { NotFound => User.Guest }                       // recover
user = store.Get(id) else { NotFound => return Invalid }                   // transform
user = store.Get(id) else { NotFound => throw }                            // cannot happen here
```

## 6. Concurrency

### Actors

An actor is an object with private state, its own heap and an inbox. In C# terms: a class with a private `Channel<T>` and one task looping over it, all written by the compiler.

- Nobody outside can touch its fields.
- Calls on an actor look like ordinary method calls. The compiler turns each into a message.
- An actor handles one message at a time, so there are no locks and no data races inside it.
- An idle actor costs no CPU and a few hundred bytes. Millions run on a handful of OS threads.
- There is no `async`/`await`. Every call looks synchronous; the runtime parks the actor while it waits.
- An actor parked in the middle of a call is a compiler-made state machine, built only for functions that can wait; the whole-program view knows which ones. All other code runs on the ordinary stack. *(assumed)*
- Messages from one actor to another arrive in the order they were sent. *(assumed)*
- A call waits for its result by default. `send` makes a call fire-and-forget: `send store.Add(user)`. *(Waiting by default is the reading of "the Elixir way" that was stated back to the owner.)*
- What may cross between actors: values (`data`, collections, strings, numbers), immutable class instances and actor references. A mutable class instance crosses only with an explicit word at the call: `copy` (the receiver gets a deep copy) or `move` (the sender's variable is dead afterwards, nothing is copied). Two actors can never reach the same mutable object.
- A generic function sends a value of a type parameter only where the parameter carries a limit of the language, inferred for every type as Rust infers `Send`, so that the error stands at the signature (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Inferred limit, as Send", against a check of each instance where its type arguments are known, at the cost of an error inside a generic function that names an instance its author never wrote, and against a mutable class sent as a `T` and copied without a word, at the cost of a hidden deep copy against the explicit `copy` and `move` above; the cost is one more limit in generic code that sends) *(assumed: that the types that meet the limit are those that cross without `copy` or `move`, the reading of the option's types that may cross)*.
- How a value crosses on one machine is the runtime's decision and invisible in code: a small value is copied into the receiver's heap, a big one is shared by pointer. Shared data lives outside the actor heaps and is counted with counters that are safe across cores. Erlang does the same for binaries above 64 bytes.
- Identity does not survive crossing: what arrives is a different object. Immutable classes compare by content, so this is invisible for them.

```
store.Add(copy order)
store.Add(move order)
```

### Scheduling

Switching between actors is cooperative by default: an actor gives up its core at actor calls and I/O. A runaway loop then costs one core, not the system, because other cores take over the waiting actors.

- An actor opts into stronger fairness after its name, where `per` sits as well. `yield checks`: the compiler inserts switch points at loop ends and function entry. `yield timer`: a timer interrupt switches the actor out.
- The compiler warns about a loop inside an actor that has no switch point and no provable end.

```
actor ReportBuilder yield checks { ... }
actor ReportBuilder yield timer { ... }
```

### Crashes and supervision

- An exception kills only the actor it happens in.
- The supervisor is whoever spawned the actor; no extra code. Explicit supervisor trees exist only for other policies.
- An actor spawned by hand that has not crashed or stopped ends when its supervisor, the actor that spawned it, ends, as an Elixir supervisor shuts its children down (the owner, 2026-10-30, "With its supervisor", against an end when no reference to it remains and its inbox is empty, at the cost of counted references to actors across actors and machines, and against no end by itself, with a report of actors that wait on nothing, as Go's profile of leaked goroutines, at the cost of leaks found in production; the cost is a list of children for each actor at run time, and a child cannot outlive its spawner). Two limits the record holds narrow those last words, and two items it holds open can add to them: a child that its spawner's crash ends outlives it by the message it runs (below), and a child on another machine than its spawner outlives it by the window of section 7's detector, where the child's side holds the majority, while how long a child on a side with no majority outlives its spawner (below) and whether a child whose supervisor ends without a crash runs its current message to its end (section 14) stand open. On another machine than its spawner, a child ends when that end reaches it as a message, or, where the spawner's machine is cut off and the child's side holds the majority, when section 7's detector takes that member out of the log of the members, about 20 s, in which the child outlives its spawner, so that, where its side holds the majority, a child outlives its spawner by up to that window *(assumed: as Erlang's links deliver `noconnection` to a linked process, since the answer names no mechanism across machines)*. Where the child's side holds no majority, as the smaller side of a split or either side of an exact tie, no side takes that member out, and the child's side stops its members (section 7, "Failures"); whether a stopped member ends the actors it runs stands open (section 14), and with it how long such a child outlives its spawner. A crash of an actor ends the actors it spawned by hand as well, and the instance that its supervisor starts in its place begins with none, as Akka's `preRestart` stops an actor's children and Erlang's links end them (the owner, 2026-10-30, "They end, as Akka", against children that keep running, at the cost of children that act on a crashed parent's state, and of orphans; the question gave this option no cost of its own). Such a child stops after the message it runs, as an actor that Akka's `context.stop` stops finishes its current message (the owner, 2026-10-30, "After current message", against an end at once, as an Erlang link's exit signal ends a process, at the cost of a call cut partway, the half-written state that the bullet on a call whose caller crashed sets aside, and of an interrupt at a switch point; the cost is up to one message's work that nobody waits for). When a child stops where its supervisor ends without a crash, after its current message or at once, is open (section 14).
- Default on a child crash: restart it with fresh state.
- Restart limit as in Erlang, where one is written: too many crashes in a short window crash the parent. Where none is written, no limit holds (below).
- By default a restart waits a pause that grows with each crash up to a cap, and no limit on restarts holds where none is written, so that a lasting fault, a database that is down, restarts the actor about every 30 s without end while the process stays up and answers with errors (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Growing pause", as Kubernetes backs off a crashing container and Akka a crashing actor, against Erlang's model with Elixir's limit of 3 restarts in 5 seconds, at the cost that an outage of a database ends the process, and against a kind of exception for an unreachable dependency that restarts with a growing pause and never escalates, at the cost of a second kind of crash to learn; the cost is a slower recovery after a short blip). A limit holds where `max N in <duration>` is written (below) (the owner, 2026-10-30, "No limit, as Akka", as Akka's backoff restarts without a limit where none is set (checked 2026-10-30: the documentation of `BackoffSupervisorStrategy.withMaxRestarts`, "Zero or negative disables the upper limit on restarts (and is the default)") and Kubernetes backs off a crashing container without end, against crashes counted in a window wider than the cap, 10 in 5 minutes, at the cost of a second default to learn and of a long outage that still ends the process, and against the limit as this bullet held it, counting a crash that comes sooner than its pause, under which a database that stays down crashes each restart straight away and ends the process in about a second; the cost is a lasting fault that restarts without end). - Calling a dead or unreachable actor raises an exception in the caller.
- Every call has a timeout (5 seconds by default, as in Elixir), then an exception in the caller.
- The chain ID carries a deadline, the one of the call at the chain's root: a call made inside the chain gets the time left, a call or a wait past the deadline raises the timeout exception in the caller with no parameter, and an inbox drops a message whose deadline passed before its callee starts it (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Deadline on chain ID", against a fixed timeout for each call with no cancel, the callee running on as Elixir's `GenServer.call` lets it, at the cost of work done for callers that are gone, and against an explicit token as a parameter, as C#'s `CancellationToken`, at the cost of a parameter on every function of the path, against "Every call looks synchronous"; the cost is a deadline in each message and a compare at each call and where a message is taken from the inbox). *(assumed: that a message to another machine carries the time left, read on the monotonic clock of each side, as gRPC sends a call's `grpc-timeout`, and no time of day, so that no two clocks need to agree, since the answer names no form across machines; the cost is a deadline that runs late by each hop's time in transit, as gRPC's does, so that a callee can start a message whose caller has already raised the timeout)*
