```
rows = attempt { Import(file) } else { Crashed c => return ImportFailed(c.Reason) }

w = spawn Worker(queue)                                   // default: restart fresh; too many crashes crash the spawner
w = spawn Worker(queue) on crash stop
w = spawn Worker(queue) on crash restart after 1s max 10 in 1min
w = spawn Worker(queue) on crash (c) {
    log("died: {c.Reason}")
    restart
}
```

### Deadlocks

Three layers, so that a hang cannot happen silently.

1. **Callbacks work.** Every call carries a chain ID. An actor waiting inside a chain lets in calls that belong to the same chain. `A.Save` waits on `B.Check`, which calls `A.Load`: this runs like a nested method call. Unrelated messages still wait.
2. **Compile error for loops of waiting calls between actor types**, analysed per method. `vouch` on the call overrides it and means "different instance, I vouch": `vouch c.Size()`. Exempt without override: a callback through a reference the caller passed in that same call.
3. **Instant runtime detection.** On every wait the runtime follows the wait line. If it leads back, one actor gets an exception immediately, naming both chains, and its supervisor restarts it.

Across machines, layer 3 sees chain loops but not crossing chains; those fall to the timeout.

A waiting actor finds its reply and the calls of its chain with no scan of its inbox: a call's reply goes to a one-shot slot made for that call, and a message whose chain ID names a chain its target waits in goes to a side slot as it is queued (the owner, 2026-10-30, "One-shot reply slot", as Tokio answers a request through a `oneshot` channel made for it, against a scan of the inbox at each wait, Erlang's selective receive, at the cost of a wait that grows with the queue; the cost is a lookup for each message queued and a small map for each waiting actor).

Side effect: the chain ID doubles as a trace ID, so log lines can carry it automatically.

## 7. Distribution

- Built into the language and on by default. A compiler flag turns it off for single-machine targets such as ESP32.
- A call on an actor works unchanged whether the actor lives in this process or on another machine. Clustering, remote spawn and serialization are part of the runtime.
- **Network split**, applied to everything cluster-wide (singleton actors as well as jobs):
  1. The side holding the majority of machines runs it.
  2. On an exact tie no side holds a majority, and neither runs it: both stop their members ("Failures", below).
  3. If no side qualifies, the policy is per item: `skip` (default), or run on all sides with a developer-written `merge` block that is called with both results when the cluster heals. Whether such a side runs at all, where a side without a majority stops its members (item 2 and "Failures", below), stands open (section 14).
  - Kurz can compare and replay its own state only. Effects on the outside world (an email, a payment) cannot be merged.
- **Members.** Members connect on demand, and the cluster-wide directory of keyed actors (section 13) is split across them by consistent hashing over the list of members, as Orleans splits its directory of grains (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Consistent hash, Orleans", against a full mesh and a directory copied to every machine, as Erlang's `global`, at the cost of n² links and an agreement of the whole cluster for every new key, and against a directory service outside the program, as etcd, at the cost of that dependency and its availability; the cost is one more hop on a miss in the directory, a protocol of membership and a move of entries at each join). Every join and every leave goes through a log of the members that a majority of the current members agree on, as Raft keeps one, and the majority of the network split counts that set (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Raft membership", against a fixed list of members in `project.kz`, at the cost of a redeploy to grow the cluster, and against the last view each side saw, at the cost of two majorities after a join during a split; the cost is a consensus protocol in the runtime, and a join needs a majority up).
- **Failures.** Members send each other a heartbeat every second, and a phi-accrual failure detector reads them, as Akka's cluster reads them; a member unreachable for a set time, 20 s as in Akka's split brain resolver, is taken out of the log of the members by the side that holds the majority, and the side without it stops its members (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Phi accrual + majority, as Akka", against a fixed tick, as Erlang's, a tick every 15 s and `net_ticktime` 60 s, a `nodedown` and the program deciding, at the cost of 60 s to notice and a split brain left to the program; the cost is about 20 s for each split, and a side without a majority that stops) *(assumed: the detector's threshold and its other settings at Akka's defaults, the threshold 8)*. On an exact tie of a network split neither side holds a majority, and both stop their members while the split lasts, unless an operator takes members out of the log of the members, as a Raft group commits nothing on an even split (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Raft: neither side wins", against the side that holds the oldest machine running while the log of the members takes no join and no leave, as Akka's resolver keeps one side of a tie, at the cost of an operator's action and of a rule beside Raft's, and against an odd number of voting members, a member that only votes added where the count is even, at the cost of that member; the cost is an outage at every even split, which an odd number of members avoids). The operator's removal is no commit of the log of the members, which no side of an even split can make, and how it is made stands open (section 14).
- **Trust.** Members prove membership to each other with mutual TLS by default, each machine with a certificate from an authority of the cluster, and the traffic between them is encrypted (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Mutual TLS default", as Rust's ecosystem makes rustls the norm and ASP.NET Core's templates start with HTTPS on, against a shared secret as Erlang's cookie with TLS on request, at the cost of the exposure Erlang's own documentation warns of, and against no authentication on a network that must stay private, at the cost that one exposed port gives full control; the cost is a handshake for each connection, encryption of each message, certificates to issue and rotate, and the cipher primitives of section 2). A switch turns mutual TLS off, and then a member's port binds the loopback addresses alone, 127.0.0.1 and ::1, for tests and a cluster on one machine, while a cluster across machines needs TLS (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Loopback only", against a private range of addresses that the switch names, at the cost that an address is no proof of membership, and against a port open to anyone, as Erlang's without TLS, at the cost that whoever reaches the port reaches the cluster; the cost is no cluster in plain text across a trusted network). A member sends only through a reference it was given, an id of 128 bits that nobody can guess, or through a name registered as public, as a capability model grants (the owner, 2026-10-30, "References it holds", against anything, as an Erlang node calls any process, at the cost that one compromised member owns the cluster, and against public names alone, as gRPC serves named services, at the cost of a name in code for every actor called; the cost is that a reference has to reach a member before it sends, and the code that registers public names).
- **Delivery.** A `send` to a durable actor (section 13) is delivered one time or more, from an outbox that the sender logs on its disk (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Durable: at least once", against zero deliveries or one for every `send`, as Erlang delivers it, at the cost of a silent loss on a dropped link, and against one delivery or more for every `send`, with a buffer at the sender and duplicates removed by a message ID at the receiver, at the cost of memory at every sender and an ID for each message; the cost is writes to the sender's disk for those messages alone). A `send` to another actor on another machine is delivered one time or not at all, as Erlang delivers it *(assumed: what the answer leaves to the sends that do not go to a durable actor)*. Each `send` to a durable actor carries an id, and the actor's log records the ids for a window and drops a repeat, as Kafka's idempotent producer and Akka's delivery with ids do (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Drop by message id", against duplicates left to the program, at the cost that every durable handler has to be idempotent and a mistake shows only under failure; the cost is an id for each message and a window of ids in the log).
- **Inspection.** The runtime has a read-only inspector of a running cluster: its actors on each machine, their inbox lengths and crash counts, and a snapshot of one actor's state with its `secret` fields stripped (section 13), behind the cluster's authentication (above) (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Read-only inspector", as .NET's diagnostic tools show a live process and BEAM's observer a node, against logs with chain IDs and crash traces alone, at the cost of a new build with more logging for a fault seen only in production, and against the inspector with record and replay switched on for one actor in production, at the cost of disk and CPU while it is on and of recordings that hold users' data; the cost is code in the runtime and one more port to secure, and nothing while nobody connects).
- No hot code reload; it would cost runtime performance.
- **Upgrades:** code versions never mix inside one cluster. A new version forms a new cluster and traffic switches over. Clients outside the cluster are the exception (section 13). State moves only where an actor says so with `upgrade`:
  - No `upgrade` line: the actor starts fresh in the new cluster, the same as after a crash.
  - `upgrade keep field`: the field is carried over, matched by name and type. A new field gets its default, a removed field is dropped.
  - Block form for when the shape really changed.
  - A call parked in a durable actor (section 13) goes on in the new code: `kurz release` matches each wait point of the old code to one of the new code by method and position, and a wait point with no match is a build error that names it (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Release matches waits", against a parked call dropped and the actor started with its kept fields, at the cost that the trial of section 13's sample never ends, and against the old cluster serving its parked calls as long as one exists, at the cost of two clusters for up to the longest wait; the cost is compiler work of the kind `kurz release` does for the `pub` surface).
  - The log of a durable actor reaches the new cluster by copying: the new cluster's members join as copies of the log and catch up from a stream, and when they are current, the lead and the traffic move to them; the old cluster ends after the messages in flight (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Replicate, then switch", against a stop, a copy and a start, at the cost that the actor is unavailable while it is copied, and against a shared store with a lease, at the cost of a store outside the program under every durable actor; the cost is twice the log's storage during an upgrade).

```
actor UserStore {
    mut users = Map<int, User>()
    mut cache = Map<int, Page>()

    upgrade keep users                                          // cache starts empty
    upgrade (old) { users = old.users.Where(u => u.Active) }    // or transform by hand
}
```

```
every 5min on split run {
    RecountStock()
} merge (mine, theirs) => mine.Newer(theirs)
```

## 8. Syntax

- Variables: `x = 5` declares an immutable variable, `mut x = 5` a mutable one. The type may be written: `int x = 5`. An unused variable is a warning in a test build, the default one, and a compile error in a release build; either catches a typo that would otherwise declare a new variable (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Warn in test, error release", against the error in every build, as the record held it, at the cost of a broken build for every line commented out, and against a warning in every build, which a CI run can turn into an error, as Rust's lint is under `-D warnings`, at the cost that the check of typos depends on that flag; the cost is a diagnostic whose level the build sets). A parameter that its function never reads, and the variable of a `for` loop whose body never reads it, are no error (the owner, 2026-10-03, against making them errors like a variable, with the words that the compiler just optimizes it).
- No semicolons. A newline ends a statement. A `;` that ends, starts or follows a statement is the semicolon error, one for a run of them, and the statement after it parses on its own; a `;` anywhere else, inside parentheses, brackets or an interpolation, or where an operand, a name, a member, a parameter or an arm is expected, is the error for text no rule gives a meaning (the owner, 2026-10-10).
- No parentheses are needed around a condition, and braces are always required: `if x > 5 { ... }`. A condition in parentheses, `if (x > 5) {`, compiles all the same, since an expression in parentheses is an expression like any other; a `{` on the next line is the compile error for a missing block, because the line ends the statement and nothing continues it there (the owner, 2026-10-09, against continuing the statement onto the `{`, a further exception to the line rule, and against an error of its own for the parentheses, a special case in the parser for one spelling). The same error is raised wherever a block that a construct takes opens on the next line or is replaced by a statement, at the line where the block should have opened: the last line of the header of a `while` or a `for`, the line of the `match` or of the call an `else` follows, the line of the word `else` or `raw`, the line of the name or the word for the body of a `class`, `data`, `interface`, `enum` or `flags` or the braces of `with`, and the first line of the signature for the body of a function outside a type body or of a constructor; the list names the constructs the reference has cases or rules for, and the rule holds for every other block of this record, an `attempt` or `retry` block, an actor body and the block of `every ... run` among them, at the line where the words before the block end; a method signature inside a type body that ends its line is the bodiless form of an interface member, so its `{` on the next line is the error for text no rule gives a meaning (the owner, 2026-10-10).
- Signatures in C# order, type first: `User | NotFound Get(int id) => ...`.
- Everything is private by default: members are private to their type, top-level types and functions are private to their folder. `pub` exposes; `prot` exposes to inheriting types. The fields of a primary constructor are the exception: they can be read wherever their type is visible, as the positional members of a C# record can (the owner, 2026-10-02). Without that, every such field would carry `pub`. The cost: a field that has to stay private is declared in the body instead.
- A folder is a namespace; there is no `namespace` line. Files in one folder see each other without imports. `use` brings in other folders and packages.
- The project file is written in Kurz itself (`project.kz`), not in a separate format. It holds one `deploy` block per deployment with that deployment's switches: the inbox mode (section 13) and, by the same idea, distribution on or off and the target platform. *(assumed)*
- String interpolation is always on: `"hello {name}"`.
- A string over several lines is written as a raw string literal is in C# 11: it opens with `"""` at the end of a line and closes with `"""` on a line of its own. The text starts on the line after the opening and ends before the closing line, and the indentation of the closing line is removed from every line; a line that is indented less is a compile error, as there (the owner, 2026-10-02). A `"` inside the block is an ordinary character, as there. Unlike there, `{expression}` and `\` keep the meaning they have in every other literal, so a brace of JSON or CSS is written `\{` (the owner, 2026-10-02, against a block without interpolation, which was the form offered). `"""` opens a block only at the end of a line: `"""abc"""` on one line, text after an opening `"""` and text before a closing one are compile errors, and a line of the block that holds only whitespace is exempt from the indentation rule and is an empty line of the text, as in C# 11 (the owner, 2026-10-09, against a one-line form that holds `abc` with a `"` as an ordinary character, as C# 11 has it, and against an id of its own for the malformed shapes; the escape gives a `"` without the one-line form). Only characters other than whitespace count as text beside a `"""`, as C# 11 has it, so trailing spaces after the opening and the indentation before the closing are no error; and code may follow the closing `"""` on its line, `""")` closing a call, as C# 11 allows, since "on a line of its own" is about what stands before it (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 13). There is no block that opens with four or more quotes, which C# 11 allows: `""""` at the end of a line is a compile error (the owner, 2026-10-10). A lone carriage return, one that no line feed follows, is an ordinary character inside a literal, except at the end of a line of the block, where every trailing one is dropped with the line's end, and whitespace outside a literal, so a file whose lines end in a carriage return alone is one line, which does not compile as its writer meant; the cost is nothing, since no current editor writes those line ends (the owner, 2026-10-10).
- Primary constructors, C# lambdas, C# generics.
- Top-level statements instead of `Main`.
- A statement continues on the next line while a `(` or a `[` is open, when its line ends in a binary operator, a comma or `=>`, and when the next line starts with `.`. Nothing else continues it. There is no `;`: not at the end of a line and not between two statements. (the owner, 2026-10-02) The `=` of an assignment counts as a binary operator here. *(assumed)* In detail, each a reading of one of the three exceptions (the owner, 2026-10-10): a line that starts with `?.` continues the statement as one that starts with `.` does, and empty lines and comment lines between the statement and that line do not end it; a line break inside the braces of an `enum`, `flags` or `with` body and inside the `< >` of type arguments or type parameters does not end the statement, as one inside parentheses does not; a line break after the `:` of a base list continues the declaration; a line that ends in the `|` of a type or in `..` or `..<` continues on the next; and a trailing comma is accepted in an `enum` or `flags` body and between the brackets of a list (section 4), and refused in the braces of `with`, where it is the error for text no rule gives a meaning (section 8). A line that starts with `.` directly followed by a digit starts a statement with a fraction literal (above) and continues nothing (the owner, 2026-10-11, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 14).
- `name = expression` assigns when a variable of that name is visible and declares one when none is, so a variable cannot hide another one. A variable counts as used when it is read at least once; being assigned again does not count. A `mut` variable that nothing changes is a warning, not an error. (the owner, 2026-10-02)
- A written type always declares; when the name is already visible that is a compile error. A variable is visible from its declaration to the end of its block, and every declaration has a value. *(assumed)*
- `a..b` is a range that includes both ends, `a..<b` leaves the end out. A loop over numbers is `for i in 0..<n`; the C form with three parts does not exist. (the owner, 2026-10-02) A range never counts down, and a range whose end lies below its start is an error, not an empty range: a compile error when both ends are constant expressions, an exception when the program runs otherwise (the owner, 2026-10-02, against the proposal that such a range holds no number). `a..<a` holds no number and is valid. The cost: `for i in 1..n` throws when `n` is 0, so a loop that may run zero times is written with `..<`. The range and its loop variable have the common type of the two ends, found as `+` finds it: `0..<n` with a `long` `n` is a range of `long`, and integer ends for which `+` raises the error for mixing signed with unsigned are that error (the owner, 2026-10-09, against a range that is always `int`, under which a loop over a `long` range is written by hand; the cost is that the loop variable's type follows the end, which a reader finds at the declaration of `n`). An end whose type is no integer type, `0..<1.5` or `0..2.0` whatever its value, is the error for a value of one type where another is required (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 13).
- `else` and `else if` follow the closing brace on its line; a condition is a `bool`; `while condition { }`, `for name in collection { }`, `break` and `continue` mean what they mean in C#. *(assumed)*
- A function body is an expression after `=>` or a block with `return`; a function without a result is declared `void`; a function or a type can be used above its declaration. *(assumed)*
- Functions may share a name when their parameters differ, a parameter or a field may have a default value (`int Count = 1`), and an argument may be passed by name (`Item(ProductId: 7)`). The owner chose overloads on 2026-10-02 against the proposal to leave them out. When a call fits several functions, the one whose parameters have exactly the types of the arguments, with no default value used, wins; any other call that fits more than one is a compile error (the owner, 2026-10-02). The cost of that rule: where two functions fit through widening, the call needs a conversion such as `long(x)`. For this rule a literal argument has the type it has on its own, `int` for `4`, so `Show(4)` picks `Show(int)` over `Show(long)`, as in C# (the owner, 2026-10-02). A call of a name that has more than one function and fits none of them is the compile error `argument-mismatch`, at the call, with a message that names each function and why it does not fit (the owner, 2026-10-21, against the error of the first function, in the order of declaration, that the arguments bind to, under which the error changes when two declarations swap places; the cost is a longer message, and an id that no longer names the argument's own fault, such as a narrowing conversion). *(assumed: that the line is the first line of the call, as an error of the whole construct reports it (below); the answer said at the call)* The cost of overloads as such: a name no longer stands for one function, for the reader and for `mock` (section 11), and the name of a parameter becomes something its callers depend on. Arguments are evaluated from left to right, as in C# *(assumed: proposed on 2026-10-09 and not objected to)*, in the order written at the call, named arguments included (the owner, 2026-10-10). A parameter's default value is a constant expression, as C# requires of it: a literal, `null`, a value of an `enum` or `flags`, a `data` value without fields, or an expression of literals that section 4 folds (the owner, 2026-10-11, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 14); one that is not is the compile error `default-not-constant`, at the parameter (the owner, 2026-10-10, against the general type error and the id for text no rule gives a meaning; the spelling of the id and its line are *(assumed: proposed on 2026-10-10, after the owner's choice)*). A field's default may be any expression (the owner, 2026-10-10, against constants alone, under which a field of a collection type could have no default): it may read the parameters of the primary constructor, the fields declared above it and functions outside the class, and is evaluated in declaration order before the body of a constructor runs, as C# evaluates an initializer, which refuses an instance member there; a field declared below it, a method of the class or `this` is the error for a name that is not visible; the cost is that such a default runs at every construction, which a constant one does not (the owner, 2026-10-11, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 14).
- A source file is read as UTF-8, and a string literal holds the code points written in it, as they are written, without normalization (the owner, 2026-10-09, confirming the reference's reading L1). A file that is not valid UTF-8, an encoded surrogate included, is one compile error, `invalid-source`, at the line of the first bad byte; a byte-order mark at the start of the file is skipped, because some Windows editors and tools write one (the owner, 2026-10-09, against refusing the mark too, under which a file saved with the mark does not compile; the cost is one accepted leading code point that is not part of the text). No conformance case shows the error: the lint and every gate read a corpus file as UTF-8, so a case that is not would be refused by the tools as they stand, and the reference names the id in its rule and not in its table of the ids cases expect. The two-byte line break that Windows editors write is read as one line break wherever it stands in a source file, inside a `"""` block too, so the same program compiles and prints the same text whether its lines end in a line feed or in the two-byte break (the owner, 2026-10-10, against reading the two bytes as written, under which the text of a block depends on the editor; the cost is that no string literal can hold the two-byte line break as written: a carriage return directly before a line feed is written through its escape, where a carriage return that no line feed follows stands as written). No conformance case can show it either: the repository stores every file with line feeds alone, and checks them out so on every platform (`.gitattributes`, `* text=auto eol=lf`). A carriage return that no line feed follows is read as the rule on strings over several lines says, above.
- `//` starts a comment that runs to the end of its line. In a string literal `\n`, `\t`, `\"` and `\\` mean what they mean in C#, and `\{` is a brace that starts no interpolation; the other escapes of C# 12 (`\'`, `\0`, `\a`, `\b`, `\f`, `\r`, `\v`, `\u` with four hex digits, `\U` with eight and `\x` with one to four) mean what they mean there, and a backslash before any other character, or before a `u`, `U` or `x` without the digits it takes, is a compile error, as there (the owner, 2026-10-03, against the five alone; the version, under which the `\e` of C# 13 is such an error, is *(assumed)*). A `\u`, `\U` or `\x` whose digits name a surrogate, U+D800 to U+DFFF, or a value beyond U+10FFFF is that error too, since a string holds code points and not UTF-16 units (the owner, 2026-10-10); a pair of such escapes that C# would join, `\uD83D\uDE00`, is the error at the first, and `\U0001F600` writes the code point (the owner, 2026-10-11, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 14). `true` and `false` are the values of `bool`. A name starts with a letter or `_` and goes on with letters, digits and `_`; upper and lower case differ. A letter is one of ASCII's: a name with any other character, an umlaut included, is a compile error, and C#'s `@` names do not exist (the owner, 2026-10-09, against any Unicode letter as C# allows, under which two names can look the same, and against `@keyword` names, a second spelling C# has for interop; the cost is that a C# program with an umlaut in a name is edited). *(assumed: the comment, the five escapes, `true` and `false`, and the rest of the form of a name)*
- Only the core words are reserved. A user-defined keyword (section 9) is reserved in the files that import it. (the owner, 2026-10-02) The core words are the words the language uses as syntax, listed in the reference as a closed list (the owner, 2026-10-03, against reserving every keyword of C#, which would take `goto`, `unsafe` and `checked` from programs for nothing). The names of the built-in types (`int`, `string`, `bool`, ..., and `duration` and `timestamp` with their wide pair) are core words as well, as most of them are keywords in C# (the owner, 2026-10-04, against ordinary names that a declaration hides in its block). The cost: nothing can be called `string`, and a ported program that calls a local `duration` or `timestamp`, which C# does not reserve, has to rename it. A core word used as a name reports the one error at its declaration, and a use of it afterwards none; a core word where an expression stands, `print(if)`, and an untyped lambda parameter or the name of a named argument that is a core word, `by => by.Id` or `f(by: 1)`, are the error for text no rule gives a meaning, where the typed parameter `(int by)` is the reserved-word error; and `match` starts a match when a token that can start a value follows it, and is read as a name otherwise, so `match.Add(1)` and `print(match)` parse as a call and a name, with the reserved-word error at the declaration of that name, or the error for a name that is not visible where none declared it (the owner, 2026-10-10).
- A compile error is identified by a word, such as `unused-variable`. Tests of the compiler pin the id and the line of an error, never its message (the owner, 2026-10-02). The exceptions a program can raise have ids as well, and a test pins the id and the line that raised (the owner, 2026-10-03, against tests that say only that something is thrown). An error is reported on the line that holds the offending construct: the declaration for an unused variable, the first class declaration on the path for a possible cycle. *(assumed: the last sentence)* A construct over several lines reports the line of the smallest part that is wrong, an argument its own line and a binary operator its operator's line; only an error of the whole reports the first line. *(assumed: proposed on 2026-10-09 and not objected to)* A block that is missing, or that opens on the next line, is an error of the whole construct, reported where the block should have opened: for an `if` the line of its condition (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 13), its last line when the condition runs over several lines (the owner, 2026-10-11, confirming the reading proposed after the review of round 14), and for the other constructs the line named with them in section 8. A `)`, `]` or `}` missing at the end of the file is reported at the last line that holds code, a block of `"""` that is never closed at its opening line, and an interpolation that no `}` closes on its line at the line of its `{` (the owner, 2026-10-10). A text that no rule gives a meaning is one compile error, `syntax`, reported on the line where the text stops making sense, the line of the first token no rule accepts, except for the three unclosed shapes above, whose lines are named with them; where the construct's rule names an id of its own, that id is reported and not `syntax` (the owner, 2026-10-10, confirming the reference's reading E4, which round 13 had not asked about, and with it the token and the precedence of the rule's own id). Such an error that only the type checker can see, a `match` on a set of flags or a call with a `void` success used as a value, is reported at the line of the construct (the owner, 2026-10-10).
- `print(value)` writes the text of a value and a line break, which is one line feed on every platform (the owner, 2026-10-10, against the platform's own line ending, under which the output of a program differs by the machine that runs it; the cost is that a tool on Windows that expects the two-byte line break in a program's output reads bare line feeds). The text of an integer is its decimal digits, of a string the string, of a `bool` `true` or `false`. *(assumed; the name belongs to the standard library, whose naming is open)* A `data` value has a text derived from its type, in the manner of C# records: `User { Id = 1, Name = Ann }`. A value of a generic `data` type shows the name its declaration gives, without the type arguments, `Pair { First = 1, Second = one }`, as a C# record and Rust's derived `Debug` write it (checked 2026-10-23 on .NET 10) (the owner, 2026-10-23, "Declared name", against the name with the type arguments, `Pair<int, string> { First = 1, Second = one }`, which tells two instances of one generic type apart at the cost of a longer text). A value of a union shows the text of the value of its case, `5` for an `int | string` that holds 5 and `Box { V = 5 }` for a `data` value with a field of that union, as a C# record shows a field of type `object` (checked 2026-10-23 on .NET 10) (the owner, 2026-10-23, "The case's own text", against the case with its type, `5 (int)`, a form no other text has, and against a compile error, under which a `data` type with a field of a union has no text; the cost is that the `int` 5 and the string `"5"` show alike). A union with a case that has no text, an instance of a class without one (below) or the success without a value of section 5, has none itself, and printing a value of it is the compile error for a class without text, as for a list or a `data` value that holds such an instance (the owner, 2026-10-24, "Compile error no-text", against a text for the values of the other cases alone, failing when the program runs, and for the success without a value against a text of its own, as Rust's `Debug` shows `()`, a form no other text has). The other values have a derived text as well: a list shows its items between brackets, `[1, 2, 3]`; an `enum` value and a `data` value without fields show their name; null shows `null`; a `double` shows the shortest digits that read back as the same number. A class instance is the exception: it has a text only when its class or a base class declares one, and printing an instance of a class that neither declares nor inherits one is a compile error. An instance can reach itself through a `weak` reference, so a derived text would need a rule for where to stop. The cost: one declaration in every class hierarchy that gets printed. (the owner, 2026-10-02; "or a base class" and "hierarchy" follow from the next choice) The method is inherited, and the class of the instance picks it when the program runs, as `ToString()` is in C#, so a `User` variable that holds an `Admin` prints the `Admin`'s text; a `Text()` without `pub` cannot be used by `print`, which is a use outside the class, and that is the error for a member that is not visible (the owner, 2026-10-03, against counting only a `pub Text()` the class itself declares). `print` of an instance inside its own class, and an interpolation of one there, reach `Text()` as a use from outside does, so a `Text()` without `pub` is that error there too (the owner, 2026-10-09, against a reading under which a private `Text()` has two meanings). Through an interface the text exists when the interface declares `string Text()`, and through a type parameter when the parameter is limited to an interface that declares it; otherwise printing is the same compile error as for a class without text (the owner, 2026-10-03, against a check for each instantiation of a generic function). A type meets such a limit when it has a text: the values above, a class that declares or inherits a `pub Text()`, and a value whose interface or type parameter has a text by the sentence before, without naming the interface. (the owner, 2026-10-04, against an interface a class has to name) The match holds only for an interface whose only member is `string Text()`; an interface that declares more is met by naming it, because a type that has a text does not have its other members. *(assumed: the reference's reading A10, accepted by its id on 2026-10-09 without its text shown)* The cost: a generic function that prints its argument needs the limit, and a base-typed value may print more than its static type says. A class declares its text with a method of a fixed name that takes no parameters, `pub string Text() => "counter {Count}"`, as `ToString()` does in C# (the owner, 2026-10-02, against a list of the fields that show). The owner asked with it that such a text is built with as little work as the compiler can manage. The mechanism for that *(assumed: it was proposed in the reply to that wish)*: an interpolated string is compiled into writes into one buffer, without a string of its own for each part; and where a text goes straight into `print` or into another interpolated string, a `Text()` whose body is a literal or an interpolated string writes into the buffer of its caller, so that for such a body the instance's text is never a string of its own. A `Text()` with a block body, or one that returns what another function returns, builds that string first and pays its allocation. What stays: a number is turned into digits when the program runs, and a text that is kept, in a variable or a field, is one allocation. No number is promised for it, and none of the gates of section 13 measures it. A map shows its entries between brackets, each as `key: value`: `[Ann: 31, Bea: 28]` (the owner, 2026-10-02); a map without entries shows `[]`, as a list does. *(assumed: the last part)* The remaining texts *(assumed: they were listed with that question and not objected to)*: a `float` shows what a `double` shows, in the sense of the detail below, with digits and an exponent bound of its own, a `decimal` the digits it holds, `1.50m` as `1.50`, with a `-` before a zero whose sign is set, `-(0.0m)` as `-0.0`, as a `double` shows `-0` (the owner, 2026-10-28, "Sign, as Rust", as rust_decimal shows such a zero, against no sign, as C# shows none; the cost is that an amount can show as `-0.00`; which zeros have a sign is section 4), a `char` its character, a duration its value split into units from days down (`1h 30min`), the detail below naming them, a timestamp its instant in UTC as RFC 3339 writes it (`2026-10-28T09:30:00Z`), the detail below as well, and a set of flags its names with ` | ` between them; a `double` without a fraction, one that needs an exponent and one that is no number show what C# prints for them (`1`, `1E+21`, `NaN`), where that text reads back as the value (below). In detail *(assumed: the reference's readings A11 to A14, accepted by their ids on 2026-10-09 without their text shown)* *(assumed: that the exponent form starts below `1E-04`, at `1E-05` for the powers of ten, and that it is the remainder below a second that shows in milliseconds, proposed on 2026-10-10 after the first review of that day and not yet put to the owner by their text)*: a `float` shows the shortest digits that read back as the same `float`, `0.1f` as `0.1`, and takes the exponent form from `1E+09` up, `123456790f` showing `123456790` and `1e9f` `1E+09`, as .NET prints a `float` (the owner, 2026-10-28, "As C# prints a float", against the bounds of a `double`, under which `1e9f` shows `1000000000`); a duration shows its value split into its units from the largest down, each unit at most once and a zero count left out, the remainder below a second in milliseconds and, when no formatting is chosen, the remainder below a millisecond in microseconds and nanoseconds, `1s 250us` (the owner, 2026-10-10, against rounding or cutting to milliseconds), zero as `0ms` and a negative one with `-` before the whole; a timestamp shows its instant in UTC as RFC 3339 writes it, `2026-10-28T09:30:00Z`, the fraction of a second in groups of three digits, as many groups as hold it and none for a whole second, `09:30:01.500Z`, `09:30:01.000250Z`, `09:30:00.000000001Z`, and a year outside 0000 to 9999, which only a `longtimestamp` reaches, with its sign and at least four digits, `+10000-01-01T00:00:00Z` and `-0001-12-31T23:59:59Z`, as Rust's `chrono` writes them, the expanded years of ISO 8601, which RFC 3339 does not have (the owner, 2026-10-28, "RFC 3339 UTC, digits as needed", then "Groups of three, as chrono" and "Sign + 4 or more digits, as chrono", which chrono 0.4.38 showed that day, against leaving the text open, nine digits always and the count of nanoseconds since 1970, then against the fewest digits and against six digits of a year as JavaScript writes them; the cost is a calendar in the runtime) *(assumed: the calendar, the Gregorian one before its introduction as well, with no leap second and a year 0 before the year 1, as chrono and Unix time count, which the answers named through chrono and did not say)*; a set of flags shows its names in the order of their declaration and the empty set as `None`; and what C# prints for a `double` is what .NET Core 3.0 and later print with the invariant culture, an exponent form for a nonzero magnitude of `1E+17` or more or below `1E-04`, `1E+16` showing `10000000000000000` and `1E-05` the first power of ten below that bound it shows (the owner, 2026-10-28, "As .NET prints: 1E+17", after .NET 10 wrote `1e15` as `1000000000000000`, as the source of .NET Core 3.0 has it, against keeping `1E+15`, the bound of .NET Framework's shorter format, under which Kurz writes the values from `1E+15` to `1E+17` otherwise than C#), `0` for zero, `-0` for a negative zero, `Infinity`, `-Infinity` and `NaN`; where .NET writes a text that does not read back as the value, as .NET 10 writes the power of two `2.9802322387695312E-08` as `2.980232238769531E-08`, the text is the shortest digits that do, the nearest to the value among them, as Ryu takes them *(assumed: the shortest digits that read back are the rule above, which .NET promises for these values too, and the nearest among them)*, and of two such texts equally near the value the one whose last digit is even, as Ryu and Rust's `ryu` crate take it: 2^-25, `2.98023223876953125E-08`, shows `2.9802322387695312E-08` and not `2.9802322387695313E-08` *(assumed: the tie, which no answer named)*. The order of a map's entries is the one of its storage (section 4). A list or a `data` value that holds an instance of a class without a text has no text either: printing it is the same compile error, because the type of what it holds is known where it is printed (the owner, 2026-10-03, against showing the name of the class). The cost: a `data` type with such a field cannot be printed.

## 9. Keywords that transform code

- Core words live in the compiler: `actor`, `data`, `match`, `mut`, `spawn`, `weak`, `pub` and the like.
- Chore keywords are written in Kurz, with the same mechanism users get, and ship in the standard library.
- Chores that should become keywords:
  - HTTP endpoints and routing
  - retry, timeout, circuit breaker around a call
  - caching
  - mapping between types
  - validation
  - database queries
  - scheduled jobs
  - state machines
  - dependency wiring
  - logging

### User-defined keywords

Users can define full block keywords. The rules that keep editor tooling easy:

1. **Free shape, declared as a pattern.** The head is built from slots: fixed words, expressions, types, names, blocks, declarations, with optional and repeated parts. The pattern is data the editor reads without running code. A keyword starts with its own word, and two keywords used in one file may not overlap.
2. **The inside is plain Kurz.** A keyword never adds new syntax inside its slots.
3. **Declared and imported.** A file names the keywords it uses.
4. **The output is plain Kurz and viewable.** Errors point at the developer's source, not at the expansion.
5. **Sealed at compile time.** Keyword code cannot read files, network or clock.
6. **No capture.** A keyword only touches what is passed to it. Names it introduces inside its body are declared in its pattern.
7. **Read-only type information.** A keyword may inspect the fields and methods of types it is given.
8. **No redefining** built-in keywords or each other.

```
keyword every <duration interval> [per <scope where>] <block body>
keyword route <verb> <string path> <method handler>
keyword retry <int times> <block body> [else <block fallback>]
```

- A package that defines keywords needs a grant of its own in `project.kz`, as one that touches files or the network does (section 13), so that a team sees and chooses the forms it admits (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Grant for keywords", against the eight rules alone, at the cost that each package adds its own form for a chore and two code bases read differently, and against a rule of the design that a new core form names the form it supersedes and the older one draws a warning, at the cost of a judgment in every design round; the cost is one more grant). The grant goes to the package that defines keywords alone, written `allow keywords "pkg"` beside `allow raw`, and a package that uses its keywords needs nothing (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Definer, `allow keywords`", against a grant for each package that uses them, at the cost of a line for each such package and of forms that read differently from package to package; the cost is that a project cannot admit a keyword for one part of it alone) *(assumed: that `"pkg"` stands for the name of the package that defines the keywords)*.
- Rule 4 has its tools: the command `kurz expand` and a view in the editor show the expansion of a use, and an error points at the use and carries the line of the expansion as well (the owner, 2026-10-30, "kurz expand + editor", as Rust's `cargo expand` and the view of generated source that C#'s IDEs give for source generators, against "viewable" with no tool named, at the cost of a promise with nothing behind it, and against the same with a compile error where one use expands past a size the project sets, at the cost of one more setting; the cost is work in the compiler and its tools).

## 10. Scheduled jobs

- Position gives the default scope; `per` overrides it.
- Defaults: ticks follow the clock, a tick is skipped if the previous run is still going, ticks missed during downtime are dropped. A job never runs in parallel with itself.
- `at 03:00 daily` names an hour in UTC unless the code writes a zone (section 12, decided there).

```
every 5min { CleanupSessions() }          // top level: once in the cluster
every 5min per node { FlushMetrics() }    // on each machine

actor Session {
    every 30s { Ping() }                  // inside an actor: belongs to each instance
}

every 30s after done { }                  // 30s after the last run finished
every 30s overlap queue { }               // queue a tick instead of skipping it
every 30s catchup { }                     // run ticks missed during downtime
at 03:00 daily { }                        // calendar schedule
```

## 11. Dependency injection and tests

- There is no container. A singleton is an actor declared `per cluster` or `per node` and addressed by its type name (section 13). For actors spawned by hand, wiring is constructor parameters.
- A service with no state is plain functions, or a class with constructor parameters, run on its caller's actor; `per cluster` and `per node` are for state that must be single; and state that many actors read and few change is a snapshot that one actor publishes and any actor on the machine that holds it reads without a message, as an `Arc` that its owner swaps gives it in Rust and an ETS table in Elixir (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Functions + snapshots", against the record as it stood, under which every service is one actor and one queue for all its callers, the shape Elixir's documentation calls an anti-pattern, and against an actor that runs its methods that change no state on many cores at a time, at the cost of section 6's one message at a time and of a marking of reads and writes; the cost is a rule of usage, a `mock` that reaches such a class as it reaches an actor, and for a snapshot one atomic load for each read, a copy for each publish and a new kind of shared value). *(assumed: the words "on the machine that holds it", as an `Arc` and an ETS table are local to one process and one node)*; how an actor on another machine reads it is open (section 14).
- Tests swap implementations with `mock`. The swap happens at compile time, inside the `test` block that writes it, and reaches only what that test runs: a program's own run swaps nothing, in the test build, which the command builds unless `--release` is given (section 2, decided there), as in the release one, since `build`, `run` and `exec` run no `test` block; the `test` blocks of a program run under `kurz test file.kz`, as `dotnet test` runs a project's tests (checked 2026-10-20: the documentation of `dotnet test`), and the swap is in effect in the build that command makes, inside the test that writes it, and nowhere else; each test under `kurz test` gets instances of its own of the singletons it reaches, of those the program declares (above, and section 13, decided there), each spawned for that test at the test's first message to it, in the build its swaps make, so a test reaches the instances spawned for it and no other test's, and a singleton no test reaches is not spawned, and the instances a program's own run would spawn do not run under a test; the name of the test build of section 2 (decided there) names the overflow checks of section 4 and not the running of tests *(assumed: the command `kurz test` and its name, proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*; so no interface has to exist just for faking; the cost is that the code a test reaches is compiled one time per distinct set of swaps, a compile-time and binary-size cost that grows with the tests that mock different things, and one spawn of each singleton per test that reaches it *(assumed: that the swap reaches the test alone, the singletons a test reaches spawned one per test, at its first message, and its cost, the spawn per test among it, proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*.

```
test "rename fails for unknown user" {
    mock UserStore with FakeStore
    ...
}
```

- The runtime owns clock and scheduler, so a test can fast-forward virtual time. Timers, jobs and timeouts all fire; the test takes milliseconds.

```
test "survives 60 days" {
    server = spawn TcpServer
    advance 60days
    server.Ping()
}
```

## 12. Standard library

Everything is self-written. Tiers:

- **Core** (runs on microcontrollers): numbers, strings, collections, math, time.
- **Runtime:** actors, supervisors, scheduler, timers, clustering, serialization, `Stream<T>`.
- **System:** files, processes, environment, TCP/UDP, DNS.
- **Backend:** HTTP server and client, TLS, `Sealed<T>`, logging, the chore keywords.
- **Data:** database driver and the query keyword. MySQL first; the wire driver is built last.
- **Tooling:** test runner, formatter, editor language server. Package manager later.
- **Not in the box:** graphics, audio, windowing. These are packages over the C-ABI.

Database:

- Queries compile to SQL at build time, so SQL mistakes are compile errors and nothing is translated at run time.
- LINQ-style chains for the common case, checked raw SQL for complex queries. Interpolated values become parameters.
- A chain is lazy, and its read consumes it: it runs where it is read, and a second read of the same chain is a compile error, as a Rust iterator moved into a `for` loop cannot be used again, so that a program that reads twice writes `ToList()` (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Consumed by its read", against a chain that runs where it is assigned and gives a `List<T>`, at the cost of the whole result in memory and no streaming of a large one, and against a chain that runs again at each read, as C#'s `IEnumerable<T>`, at the cost of C#'s second query without a word; the cost is a check of single use in the compiler) *(assumed: that a `Stream<T>` (section 13) is consumed by its read in the same way, the question having named it beside the chain)*.
- Both schema directions are supported. Code first: the database is generated from `data` types. Database first: a tool command (`kurz db pull`, in the manner of `dotnet ef`) connects through the wire driver package and writes the schema into the repository as generated Kurz. Builds read only that file, so compilation stays sealed (section 9, rule 5).
- Failures: an unreachable database is an exception (supervisor and `retry` deal with it). A constraint violation or a missing row is a union case.

Migrations, code first:

- A migration is generated by diff, kept in the repository and editable: `kurz db migrate "add size"`, in the manner of `dotnet ef migrations add`. Three differences from EF:
  - A rename is stated in code (`string FullName was Name`) and never guessed as drop plus add.
  - There is no model snapshot file. The compiler replays the migrations to know the schema before, so nothing conflicts on merge.
  - SQL inside a migration is checked against the schema as of that step.
- The new cluster applies pending migrations itself, once (one machine, a cluster-wide lock), before traffic switches. A tool command (`kurz db update`) exists for pipelines that want the step explicit.
- Old and new version share the database during the switch, so the compiler splits every migration in two. Steps that are safe for the old code (a new table, a new column with a default, a new index) run before the switch. Steps that break the old code (drop, tighten) run after the old cluster is gone.
  - The compiler knows every column the old release touches, because all queries are compiled and `kurz release` snapshots them.
  - A step that cannot be made safe is a build error that names the old query it breaks.
  - A renamed column keeps its old physical name until the second phase.
  - There are no down scripts. Before the second phase nothing is destroyed, so rolling back is switching traffic back.

Database first: migrations belong to whoever owns the database. After a change, `kurz db pull`, and the compiler flags every query that no longer fits.

```
users = db.Users.Where(u => u.Age > 18).OrderBy(u => u.Name).Take(10)
users = sql "SELECT * FROM users WHERE age > {minAge}"
```

Files and sockets:

- A call of the standard library on a file or a socket returns a failure of the system, or of the path or the address it was given, as a case of its result, `IoError`, and raises no exception for it: `File.Open(path)` returns `File | IoError`, and `data IoError(IoKind Kind, int Code, string Path)` holds the kind of the failure, the system's number for it and the path or the address the call was about (the owner, 2026-10-29, "One union, as Rust", as Rust's `io::Result` carries an `io::Error` with an `ErrorKind` and the system's number (checked 2026-10-29 with Rust 1.82: a file that does not exist gives the kind `NotFound` and the number 2), against the database's rule above, under which an expected outcome, a file not found, one that exists, a permission or an address in use, is a case and a failure of the device, an I/O error, a full disk or a reset, an exception that ends the actor, at the cost of two mechanisms and of an actor that a full disk restarts, and against a case type for each kind, as C#'s subclasses of `IOException`, at the cost of long signatures and of every exhaustive `match` that a new kind breaks; the cost is a tag, and an `else` at a script's first read (section 5)). The error holds the path, as C#'s `FileNotFoundException` names its file, and its text is the words of its kind and the path, `not found: data/x.bin`, in place of the text A3 of the reference gives a value of a `data` type (the owner, 2026-10-29, "Path in the error, as C#", against the kind and the number alone, as Rust's error has no path (checked 2026-10-29 with Rust 1.82: "No such file or directory (os error 2)"), at the cost that every caller that reports the error adds the path itself; the cost is a count on the path string, taken where a call fails). The text of the kind `Other` adds the system's number after its words, `other (os error 24): probe.txt`, as Rust's text writes the number (the owner, 2026-10-29, "Number for Other", against the words alone, at the cost that two failures of the kind, too many open files and a descriptor that is not open, print the same text, and against the number after every kind, as Rust's `Display` writes it, at the cost of a longer text where the kind names the failure; the cost is the text of the number, made where the error is printed). `IoKind` is an `enum` whose values are the kinds Rust's `ErrorKind` gives the error numbers of Linux, `NotFound`, `PermissionDenied`, `AlreadyExists`, `AddressInUse`, `BrokenPipe`, `ConnectionReset` and the rest, and `Other` for a number to which Rust gives none of them, the kind Rust 1.82 calls `Uncategorized` and no program can name (the same answer) *(assumed: that what raises anywhere raises in such a call too, a `max` below 0 or a list past its largest count (below), the answer naming the failures of the system, of the path and of the address alone; the list, which the reference gives, of Rust's kinds with each abbreviation written out, `AddrInUse` as `AddressInUse`, as the answer writes it (checked 2026-10-29 with Rust 1.82: the kind of every error number from 1 to 133); the kinds `InvalidInput` for a path that holds a NUL byte and `WriteZero` for a write of which the system takes no byte, which Rust's standard library gives itself; the number 0 where the error is not the system's; the words of a kind as its name's words in lower case; a text without the colon where the path is empty; and the first path of a call that names two, proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*.

Only the top-level statements see `args`: a function reads `Environment.Args()` or takes the list as a parameter, and may name a parameter or a variable of its own `args`, while a top-level `args` of the program's own, `List<string> args = ["a"]`, is `redeclared` (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Top-level code only", against `args` seen everywhere as a top-level variable, at the cost that no function could name a parameter or a variable `args`; the cost is that a function that wants the arguments gets them passed). - No text or interchange format ships in the box, not even JSON.

Hashing:

- SHA-256, HMAC-SHA256, MD5 and CRC32 are written in Kurz in the standard library and checked against the vectors of their standards, FIPS 180-4, RFC 4231, RFC 1321 and the check value of CRC-32/ISO-HDLC, as RustCrypto's crates are written in Rust (the owner, 2026-10-30, "In Kurz, as RustCrypto", against OpenSSL's `libcrypto` through the C-ABI, as .NET's `SHA256` calls it on Linux, at the cost that a program that hashes needs `libcrypto.so.3` where it runs, and against both, at the cost of two implementations to keep equal; the cost, measured with the question, is SHA-256 of 16 MiB in 79 ms against OpenSSL's 6.4 ms, the speed of the same code in Rust, 71 to 78 ms). They need no grant, since they read no file, socket or variable (section 13), and they build for every target, Windows among them, since they make no call of the system *(assumed: proposed with the product pass and not put to the owner)*. A block the system refuses for a digest or a hasher's state ends the process with `allocation-failed`, as a list's does (section 4) *(assumed: not put to the owner)*. The cipher primitives of TLS stay as section 2 has them.
- A program writes a type and a verb, `Sha256.Hash(b)`, `Md5.Hash(b)`, `Crc32.Hash(b)` and `Hmac.Sha256(key, message)`, as C#'s `SHA256.HashData`, in Kurz's casing (the owner, 2026-10-31, "`Sha256.Hash(b)`", against Rust's verb, `Sha256.Digest(b)`, at the cost that the verb reads oddly for a CRC, and against one name for the group, `Hash.Sha256(b)`, at the cost of no type to hold a hasher's state). A digest is `Bytes` in the standard's order, 32 bytes for SHA-256 and HMAC-SHA256 and 16 for MD5, and the CRC is a `uint`, as zlib's and C#'s `Crc32.HashToUInt32` give it (the owner, 2026-10-31, "`Bytes`, CRC32 a `uint`", against a type of fixed length per algorithm, which the language does not have, and against `Bytes` for the CRC too, at the cost of a conversion for a checksum compared as a number; the cost is that the length is not in the type). `Hex.Encode(b)` gives the lower-case hex of the bytes, as Rust's `hex::encode`, and `print` of a `Bytes` stays its list of numbers (the owner, 2026-10-31, "`Hex.Encode(b)`, lower case", against a member `b.Hex()`, at the cost of a library's concern on a built-in type, and against the upper case of C#'s `Convert.ToHexString`, at the cost that a program lowers it for the protocols that sign in lower case). A key of HMAC takes any length, as RFC 2104 reads it: a key longer than the block of 64 bytes is hashed first; a `string` is hashed through `s.Bytes`, with no second form that takes it *(assumed: proposed with the product pass and not put to the owner)*. The parameters are named `bytes`, as `File.WriteAll(path, bytes)` names its own, `key`, `message` and `tag`, and `a` and `b` *(assumed: not put to the owner)*. Every file sees the six names `Sha256`, `Md5`, `Crc32`, `Hmac`, `Hex` and `FixedTime`, as it sees `File` and `Environment`, so that a file's own declaration of one, a type, a function, a variable or a parameter at any level, is `redeclared`, while a field takes the name *(assumed: not put to the owner; the cost is six names a program cannot take)*.
