## 13. Further features

Agreed in a later brainstorm round. Each one is compiled into a program only when the program uses it.

### Actors addressed by key or by name

`per` on an actor says how many of it exist and where:

```
actor Metrics per node { }          // one per machine:    Metrics.Count("hit")
actor Billing per cluster { }       // one in the cluster: Billing.Charge(order)
actor Session per int userId { }    // one per key:        Session[42].Add(item)
actor Cart per caller { }           // one per signed-in client (see "Clients and devices")
actor Circuit per connection { }    // one per client connection
actor Worker { }                    // spawned by hand, the reference is passed around
```

No spawn, no lookup, no registry. The runtime finds a keyed actor or creates it on some machine, places each key, puts idle actors to sleep and rebalances when machines join. For the developer this is horizontal scaling with zero code; the cost is compiler and runtime work. It needs a cluster-wide directory, and during a network split the three-step rule of section 7 applies per key.

- A `per cluster` or `per node` actor is the singleton: no registration and no wiring, and `mock` works on it unchanged. The dependency is no longer visible in a constructor; the compiler can list who uses what.
- A keyed actor that is not `durable` is dropped after an idle time: 10 minutes by default, overridable (`idle 10min` as a sketch). Its state is gone and the next call starts it fresh.

### Durable actors

```
durable actor Subscription per int userId {
    mut plan = Plan.Free
    pub void Trial() {
        plan = Plan.Pro
        wait 30days
        plan = Plan.Free
    }
}
```

Every change reaches an actor as a message, so logging messages to disk makes its state survive crashes and restarts. A log on the local disk does not survive the loss of the machine, which the first version of this section claimed. `durable` therefore has three modes:

- Local disk only: one disk write per message; the state is lost with the machine.
- A copy on N machines before a message counts as stored (`durable 2`): survives the loss of a machine and costs a network round trip per message.
- A copy sent in the background: fast, and the last moments are lost when the machine dies.

The default is two copies when distribution is on and local disk on a single machine. The spelling of the first and third mode is not fixed. Enabled only where the `durable` keyword is used. Kurz gets its own storage engine for this, written in Kurz.

During a network split (section 7), a message counts as stored only where a majority of the actor's copies took it on the side that holds the cluster's majority *(assumed: "a majority of the actor's copies", of the default two copies both; whether an actor of three copies or more needs all of them, as the mode above says, or a majority, as the comparison below reads Raft and Kafka, stands open, section 14)*, and the side without it refuses a durable `send` with an error while the split lasts, as Raft commits an entry and as Kafka's `min.insync.replicas` refuses a write (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Majority side only", against both sides taking messages and merging them after the split, as Dynamo and Riak, at the cost of histories in conflict or lost messages; the cost is that the side without the majority takes no durable message during a split). With the default of two copies (above) a message needs both, so a split that puts the two copies on two sides refuses the durable `send`s of that actor, the side without the cluster's majority refusing them with an error for the detector's window and then stopping its members (section 7) and the side with it, where it holds one of the copies, refusing them for the detector's window and the copy that the answer on a lost copy gives (below), since the detector does not tell a member across a split from a lost one: the comparison with Raft and Kafka holds for three copies, of which two are a majority, and of two copies the majority is both (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Keep 2, name the cost", against a default of three copies, two of which must take a message, as Kafka's replication factor 3 with `min.insync.replicas` 2 and a Raft group of three, at the cost of a third write to a disk and over the network for each message of every durable actor; the cost is no durable write for an actor whose copies a split separates), a cost the answer on a lost copy bounds on the side with the majority where it holds a copy. While the machine of one of an actor's two copies is lost, and the side that holds the cluster's majority holds the copy left, the actor's durable `send`s are refused until a new copy is made on another member of that side, after the detector of section 7 takes the lost member out of the log of the members, about 20 s, as Elasticsearch places a replica again after its `delayed_timeout` (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Until new copy", against a refusal while that machine is down, as Kafka with two replicas and `min.insync.replicas` 2, at the cost of writes refused until an operator acts where the machine is lost for good, and against writes to the one copy left, at the cost of "a message needs both" and of messages lost at a second failure; the cost is a copy of the actor's log over the network and onto a disk, and a rule for the member that takes it). The `send`s stay refused for the detector's 20 s and for the copy, whose time grows with the size of the actor's log. A cluster of two machines that loses one is a tie of one against one, under which the machine left stops its members (section 7) and makes no copy, while the operator's removal on a tie stands open (section 14); and what the side with the majority does with a durable actor whose copies all stand outside it is open (section 14).

### Inbox overflow

Waiting calls throttle themselves: the caller waits, so load cannot pile up. For everything else the owner's direction is that a full inbox spills into a storage engine local to the node or process, the same engine durable actors use, to free memory and queue work without cluttering everything else.

When that storage is full as well, or the target has none, the sender gets an exception. The inbox works in different modes depending on the deployment:

- `spill`: memory first, then local storage, then an exception in the sender.
- `fail`: a fixed number of slots; when they are full, an exception in the sender.
- `drop oldest`: when full, the oldest waiting message is discarded.
- `drop newest`: when full, the arriving message is discarded. *(assumed: only the name was shown to the owner)*

The default is set per deployment in `project.kz`, and an actor can override it after its name. *(assumed: the owner confirmed the modes; the placement was part of the same question)*

A waiting call that meets a full inbox raises in its caller immediately, in every mode, and `spill` takes a `send` alone; the `deploy` block sets a limit on the memory part of `spill` (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Raise + spill cap", as .NET's `BoundedChannelFullMode` and Rust's `try_send` show the full case at the write, against the modes as they stood, a waiting call dropped and left to its timeout and no limit on the memory of `spill`, at the cost of overload turned into writes to disk and stalls of 5 seconds; the cost is one more rule and one more setting). *(assumed: that the limit is the bytes the memory part of `spill` holds, at which `spill` goes on to local storage, as the mode above says, so that a limit on memory sends overload to the disk sooner and does not spare the disk, which the cost the question named for the modes as they stood does not show; and that a limit the `deploy` block sets holds for each inbox, as the default below does)*. Where the `deploy` block sets no limit, each inbox in `spill` holds up to 64 MB in memory before it goes on to local storage, as the NATS client holds up to 64 MB pending for each subscription by default (the owner, 2026-10-30, "A default limit" and "64 MB per inbox", against no limit, the unbounded memory set aside with "Raise + spill cap", and against a limit that every `deploy` block writes, at the cost of a line in each deployment and a new error id, and, for the size, against 32 MB for each inbox, at the cost of overload that reaches the disk sooner, and a quarter of the process's memory shared by all inboxes, at the cost that one actor takes the share of the others; the cost is a sum that grows with the number of actors that spill at once). The process's memory alone bounds that sum: past it an allocation that fails ends the process (section 6), every message held in memory with it.

```
// project.kz
deploy server { inbox spill }
deploy esp32  { inbox 32 fail }
```

```
actor Telemetry inbox 1000 drop oldest { }
```

### Cluster simulation tests

Required.

```
test cluster 5 seed 1337 {
    split 2 | 3
    advance 10min
    heal
    check Stock[7].Count() == 40
}
```

Clock, scheduler and network are all Kurz, so several machines run inside one process, deterministically. Any failure reproduces from its seed. C-ABI calls break the determinism.

### Record and replay

An actor is deterministic given its messages. Recording inbox and I/O results turns a crash into a file that can be stepped through. This is built for local development and testing only, not for production environments.

### Packages

```
// project.kz
use "github.com/kurz-lang/json" 1.2
use "github.com/symo/mysql" 0.9 allow network
```

```
use json                       // may touch: nothing
use mysql allow network        // may touch: network only
```

- **Permissions.** Whole-program compilation knows which package opens sockets, reads files, calls C or contains `raw` blocks (section 3). A package that exceeds what the project granted fails to compile.
- **Files and sockets.** A package that calls the file functions of section 12 needs `allow files`, and one that calls its socket functions `allow network`; a call without the grant is the compile error `files-not-allowed` or `network-not-allowed` at the call, while the program itself needs neither, so that a script reads a file with no project file (the owner, 2026-10-29, "allow files + allow network", against one grant `allow io` for both, at the cost that a package that reads one file may open sockets too, and against the grants for the program as well, as `raw` asks a project file of it (section 3), at the cost of a project file for a script of one line; the cost is that the checker knows which functions of the standard library touch files and which the network) *(assumed: that the `Directory` functions are file functions, proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*. Every package on the path of calls from the program's code to such a function needs the grant, read over the whole program, so that no package borrows a grant through another that has it (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Every package on path", against the package whose code makes the call of the standard library alone, which this bullet assumed before, at the cost that a grant is borrowed through any package that has it, and against that with a listing, for each grant, of the packages that reach it, at the cost of a list to read and no refusal; the cost is that a package that uses a package of an HTTP client needs `allow network` itself). For a package on that path that calls no such function itself, the error stands at its call that leads on toward the function, a line of its own code, and names the path, as `Report -> Store.Save -> File.Write`, one error for each such line (the owner, 2026-10-30, "At its call, naming the path", against the error at the dependency in `project.kz`, at the cost of no line of code, and against the error at the call of the file function, at the cost of a line in code the user did not write; the cost is several errors where a package calls on along the path in several places). A call through an interface on the path counts as a call of every implementer of `std/` and of the packages, and a call of a function value as a call of every such function whose value is taken, so that no grant is borrowed through them, while an implementer or a function of the program's own code is no path that needs the grant, a path from a package's call ending there and what that code calls being a path from the program's code, so that `FileLog.Write -> Store.Save -> File.Write`, with `FileLog` the program's own logger, needs the grant of `Store` and not of the package that called the logger, as the cost of the option set aside reads it (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Program's own don't count", against every implementer counting, the program's own among them, as Java's `AccessController` checks every frame of the stack, at the cost that a package that takes a logger needs `allow files` whenever the program hands it one that writes files; the cost is a rule to explain, and a walk that tells the program's code from a package's) *(assumed: that the walk counts such a call where it never reaches the function, at the cost of a refusal there; whether a `send` to an actor puts the sender's package on the path stands open, section 14)*. Where several paths lead through one line, its one error names the shortest, two of one length in the order of the source (the owner, 2026-10-30, "The shortest", against every path in one error, at the cost of a long text, and against the first path the walk finds, at the cost of a text that changes with code the line does not reach; the cost is that the other paths show only after this one is fixed) *(assumed: that of two of one length the error names the one that stands first where the two part, the call written first in that function, or, at one call through an interface or a function value, the implementer whose file comes first in the order of the paths of section 4 and, in one file, whose line comes first; not put to the owner)*.
- **The environment.** A package that reads a variable of the environment, `Environment.Variable` (section 12), needs `allow env`, as Deno's `--allow-env` guards `Deno.env`, on every package on the path of calls as the grants above, and a read without it is the compile error `env-not-allowed`; the command line and the clock need no grant (the owner, 2026-10-30, "`allow env`, as Deno", against no grant, as Rust's and C#'s reads, at the cost that a dependency reads every secret in the environment and sends it where it has `allow network`; the cost is one more grant line for a package that reads its configuration) *(assumed: the id, in the form of `files-not-allowed`)*.
- **Raw and native.** `allow raw` and `allow native` stay with the package whose code holds the `raw` block or the C declaration, and a package that calls it needs neither, as a safe Rust wrapper over `unsafe` code asks nothing of its callers (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Stay with the package", against the path of calls that `allow files` and `allow network` follow (above), at the cost that the standard library wraps `raw` code everywhere and nearly every package would need the grant; the cost is that a project that grants `allow raw` trusts every wrapper the package exports).
- **Source only.** The whole-program checks (cycle rule, deadlock rule, permissions, `mock`) need the source, so closed-source binary packages of Kurz code cannot exist, and Kurz code has no binary interface of its own: no stable ABI, no plugin and no closed package, the one binary boundary being `extern "C"` (the owner, 2026-10-30, "None", against plugins as processes of their own that speak the binary format of section 12 through actor calls, at the cost of a message for each call, a second process and an exception to section 7's rule that versions never mix in a cluster, and against a stable ABI for a subset of Kurz's types, as Rust's crABI experiment, at the cost of a frozen layout of strings, lists and unions; the cost is no plugin and no closed package).
- **Home.** A git URL plus a version, pinned by content hash in a lock file. A build needs no central registry; a registry, where one exists, is an index over git (the owner, 2026-10-01, round 4). The language project runs such an index, which shows for each package its grants, the checks `kurz release` made of its `pub` surface and the packages that use it (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Index with grants", as Elixir's Hex shows the documentation and the use of every package, against no index, git addresses alone, at the cost of discovery by word of mouth, and against a wider set of first-party packages, at the cost of the upkeep of each; the cost is a service to run, while the facts exist in every build).
- **Versions.** The resolver picks the lowest version that satisfies everyone, as Go does and as NuGet does for transitive packages: no surprise upgrades, and a security fix needs an explicit bump.
  - One version of a package per program; two versions would mean two copies of each type and of each `per cluster` actor inside. Where no one version satisfies two packages, the build fails and names both packages that ask, and `project.kz` can force one version, as Cargo's `[patch]` and NuGet's central pinning do (the owner, 2026-10-30, "One + override", against the build error alone, at the cost that a developer waits for a release upstream or forks, and against different major versions as different packages, as Go's `/v2` paths and Cargo's ranges allow, at the cost of two copies of the code in one binary, two types for one idea and two instances of a `per cluster` actor; the cost is one setting, whose risk the developer owns).
  - The compiler checks version numbers: `kurz release` compares the `pub` surface with the last release, with the same machinery as for `open`, and a breaking change without a major bump is an error. *(assumed)*
- **C code.** A package declares the C-ABI functions of libraries that already exist on the target (operating system, driver, SDK), or ships prebuilt binaries; both need `allow native`, and so does a function of the package's own that takes the C calling convention (section 2, decided there). A package declares them in a block of declarations with no bodies, `extern "C" { long write(int fd, byte* buf, long n) }`, and a call of such a function stands inside a `raw` block, as Rust declares them in an `unsafe extern "C" { ... }` block, whose functions only an `unsafe` context calls unless one is marked `safe` (checked 2026-10-21: the Rust Reference's chapter on external blocks, under which the 2024 edition requires the `unsafe` before `extern`) (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 19, "Block, call in raw", against a declaration with no body for each function, `extern "C" long write(int fd, byte* buf, long n)`, mirroring the form of a function of the package's own, at the cost of `extern "C"` repeated on every line and no group to name a library on, and against the block with its functions called anywhere in a package granted `allow native`, at the cost that an unchecked call into C reads as a safe call and a review cannot find the calls by their `raw`; the cost is that every call of a C function is wrapped in a `raw` block, and that the runtime's source is wordier). Round 21's answer "Safe call, native-not-allowed" (below) narrowed that cost to the calls of a function a block declares, a call through a value of a C function type standing in a `raw` block too (below). A block names the library its functions come from after `in`, `extern "C" in "dbghelp" { ... }`, as Rust names it in a `link` attribute on the block (checked 2026-10-21: the same chapter, whose `link` attribute's `name` key is the name of the native library to link), and a block without it takes what the target links by default, the C library among it (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 19, "extern "C" in "lib"", against the grant naming the libraries in `project.kz`, `allow native "dbghelp"`, at the cost that the source does not say where a function comes from and two libraries with one function's name cannot be told apart, and against a word of its own, `extern "C" library "dbghelp" { ... }`, at the cost of a new word for one use; the cost is that `in` takes a third meaning, after the `in` of a `for` and the direction of an operand of an `asm`). A declaration takes the types that the form of a function of the package's own, `extern "C" int OnSample(int frames) { ... }`, takes (section 2), its last parameter may be `...`, as Rust's may, for a C function that takes a variable count of arguments (checked 2026-10-21: the same chapter, under which a function of an external block is made variadic by `...` as its last parameter), an argument in the place of `...` takes those types but a number smaller than an `int`, a `bool` and a `float`, which C's default promotions would widen and which the developer converts, as Rust refuses such a value and asks for a cast (checked 2026-10-21: the explanation of Rust's error E0617), a block in a package without the grant is the compile error `native-not-allowed`, a call of a declared function outside a `raw` block the compile error `raw-required` (section 3), so that a package that calls one needs `allow raw` beside `allow native`, and a declaration with a type that cannot cross to C, or such an argument in the place of `...`, the compile error `native-type` *(assumed: the types, the `...`, its arguments and the three ids, proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*. A declaration takes default values and its call named arguments, which the compiler fills in at the call as for every function (section 8), so that C receives its arguments by position alone, while a `mut` parameter in a declaration is `native-type`, C taking a pointer, written `int*`, to which the call passes `&x` (section 3) (the owner, 2026-10-22, round 21, "Defaults + named yes, mut no", against all three with a `mut` parameter passed to C as a pointer to the variable, at the cost that the declaration hides that C receives a pointer, and against none of the three, at the cost that the developer writes every argument; the cost is nothing at run time). In a declaration whose last parameter is `...`, the arguments without a name fill the declared parameters in order, those with a default among them, and only those beyond them stand in the place of `...`, as C# binds a positional argument to the parameter at its position and gives a `params` array only those at or after its own (checked 2026-10-22: the C# standard's section on corresponding parameters) *(assumed: the order, proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*. A function of the package's own that takes the C calling convention is called by its name as every function of the package is, with no `raw` block, its body being Kurz that the checker checks and its convention alone differing, as Rust asks an `unsafe` block of no call but that of a function marked `unsafe`, or of one marked with `target_feature` from code without its features, which a function it defines with `extern "C"` alone is not (checked 2026-10-22: the Rust Reference's chapter on unsafety, whose list of what safe Rust cannot do names those two calls and no other, its page on the `unsafe` keyword, under which an unsafe function is prefixed with `unsafe`, and its chapter on functions, under which the `extern` qualifier gives a definition its ABI), while a call through a value of a C function type stands inside a `raw` block (below), and in a package without the grant such a function is the compile error `native-not-allowed` at its declaration, as a block is (the owner, 2026-10-22, round 21, "Safe call, native-not-allowed", against every call by name of a function of the C convention inside a `raw` block, the package's own and a block's alike, at the cost of a `raw` block around calls of code the checker checked in full, and against the safe call with an id of its own for the missing grant, at the cost of one more row in the reference's table of errors; a cost the question did not name is that whether a call by name of a function of the C convention needs `raw` depends on where the function comes from). A block of C functions stands at the top level of a file alone, and anywhere else, inside a function, the body of a type or a block of code, it is the error for text no rule gives a meaning, so that a package declares a C library at one level and the compiler collects the symbols it links in one place (the owner, 2026-10-22, round 21, "Top level only", against a block anywhere a function stands, its names visible in the block that holds it alone, at the cost of a second place to collect the symbols to link and a rule of scope for the checker; a cost the question did not name is that a function that calls a C function finds its declaration at the top level of a file). A C declaration, a declaration in a block of C functions, has one name, a C function being one symbol: a declaration whose name another function of the package, a type or another C declaration has is the compile error `redeclared` at the second of the two, so that no call picks between a C function and a function of Kurz (the owner, 2026-10-22, round 21, "One name, one function", against a C declaration as one more overload of the functions of its name (section 8), which a call picks by its arguments, at the cost that a reader cannot tell without the types which code a call reaches; a cost the question did not name is that a function of Kurz that wraps a C function takes another name than it, that two libraries with one function's name, which round 19 chose the `in` of a block to tell apart (above), cannot both be declared in one package, and that one C function declared in two files of a package is declared twice), the second of two declarations in two files being the one in the file that comes after the other in the order of the paths in which section 4 reads a program's files for the base that closes a cycle, and in one file the one written after the other *(assumed: that order across files, proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*. A function's address passes to C as a value of a C function type, written as a function type of section 4 after `extern "C"`, `extern "C" (int) => void`, whose value is the name of a function that takes the C calling convention, the package's own or one that a block declares, as Rust writes the type `extern "C" fn(i32)` and a function item coerces to it (checked 2026-10-21: the Rust Reference's chapter on function pointer types, under which function pointers can be created through a coercion from function items) (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 19, "extern "C" (int) => void", against `&F` giving an untyped `void*`, at the cost of no check that the function fits the call C makes, and against C#'s spelling of the value, `&F`, typed the same way, at the cost that taking the pointer is a raw operation and each one is wrapped; the cost is a second kind of function type, which captures nothing and is only for C, and which the checker keeps apart from the first). Such a value is never `null`, as a function type of section 4 is not, its nullable form is `(extern "C" (int) => void)?`, as section 4 writes a nullable function type and as Rust holds a function pointer that can be absent in an `Option`, a call through it stands inside a `raw` block, as a call of a declared C function does, it stands where a raw pointer stands (section 3), in a field of C-layout data, `extern "C" data Point(int X, int Y)` (section 2), among them, and a lambda or a function that does not take the C calling convention where such a type is required is the error for a value of one type where another is required, and a `null` that C gives where such a type is not nullable, in a field it writes or as the result of a function a block declares, is a misuse of `raw` code, as a `fn` pointer value must be non-null in Rust (checked 2026-10-22: the Rust Reference's chapter on behavior considered undefined), so that a declaration where C may give `null` takes the nullable form *(assumed: the `null`, the call in `raw`, where it stands, the error and the `null` that C gives, proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*. Kurz never compiles a package's C, and its runtime has no C, being Kurz (section 2, decided there); a build for the ESP32 models compiles Espressif's framework in C from its source with Espressif's toolchain and links its radio's precompiled binary libraries, which no `allow native` marks, being the framework's, and a build for AVR links the chip's C library, avr-libc, each under the runtime of its target (section 2, decided there). A package's prebuilt binary is opaque; `allow native` is the visible flag.

### Constraints in types

```
data User(int Age where 0..150, string Email where IsEmail else "not an email")
data Range(int From, int To) where From <= To          // a rule across fields

bool IsEmail(string s) => s.Contains("@")

route POST "/users" (User user) => store.Add(user)
```

An invalid value cannot exist. Input from outside yields `User | Invalid` automatically, so validation needs no code in the handler. Custom validators must be possible, as C# has them; a validator is ordinary Kurz code.

- `Invalid` carries every failure (field, rule, message), not only the first, so a form can show all of them at once.
- A validator is a pure function returning `bool`: no I/O and no actor calls. A check that needs the outside world ("email already taken") is not a type rule; it is a union case of the method.

### Clients and devices

Browsers (through WebAssembly), apps and microcontrollers take part, but not as cluster machines: server to client is harder than server to server. The owner's requirement: the developer states in code, easily and securely, which actor runs where, what it may access and how connections are made. Decided on 2026-10-01:

- **Placement.** One project. `on <deployment>` after an actor's name says where it runs; an actor without it runs on the server. The compiler builds one binary per `deploy` block in `project.kz`. A deployment with `connect` is outside, a client; one without is a cluster member. Whatever both sides use, `data` types and pure functions, is compiled into both; there is no shared project. A client holds only call stubs for server actors, so server code cannot ship. The build lists what ships to each client.
- **Two zones.** Inside is the cluster. Outside is everything that connects in: browser, app, device. The outside reaches only methods marked `open`. The server checks every incoming call against a table the compiler made, so a modified client cannot call anything else. Arguments are validated on arrival by their type constraints; the same constraints run in the client, so a rule is written once.
- **Identity.** Inside an `open` method, `caller` says who is on the other end. Sign-in code sets it (`caller.SignIn(user.Id)`); it is kept on the server and cannot be forged. A device gets its key when it is flashed.
  - `per caller`: one actor per signed-in identity. From outside it is addressed by its type name, and a client only ever reaches its own; inside code addresses it by key (`Cart[userId]`). Reading another user's data by changing an id is impossible by construction.
  - `per connection`: the same, but the actor lives and dies with one connection, like a Blazor circuit.
  - Other keyed actors stay reachable when they have `open` methods, and check `caller` themselves.
- **Direction.** Nothing shared ever waits on the outside, because a slow or hostile client would hold a server actor until the timeout.
  - A shared actor (`per cluster`, `per node`, keyed, spawned by hand) reaches a client only with `send`; a waiting call is a compile error.
  - A client's own actor (`per connection`, `per caller`) may wait on that client, since a stall then hurts only that client. Shared actors reach such an actor with `send` only, so nothing shared stalls behind it. The compiler tracks which actors can stall.
  - Device state reaches the backend through a server-side twin actor that the device reports to. A `per caller` actor may also ask its device and wait.
- **Streams.** `Stream<T>` is in the box: a sequence pulled by its consumer. It crosses actors and the boundary, and the runtime batches and paces it. It is the same idea as `IAsyncEnumerable<T>` in SignalR streaming. Big payloads travel as streams, because one message is one value in memory: a 2 GB argument cannot exist, and a hostile client could claim one.
- **Offline.** A waiting call from the outside to the inside has the union case `Offline`, because a lost connection is normal there. It is handled with `else` or listed in the signature. Inside the cluster the exception rule of section 6 stays.
- **Versions.** Clients in the field run old code for months, so "versions never mix" (section 7) cannot hold at this boundary. Breaking changes always happen, and keeping even one version compatible is hard for some teams, so compatibility is opt-in per deployment:
  - `supports current` is the default: nothing is kept compatible and every breaking change is free. A client of an older release gets `Outdated` when it connects; a browser tab reloads itself. The cost is that each release interrupts every connected client.
  - `supports 2..` keeps releases from 2 on compatible, checked by the compiler. `kurz release web 1.4` writes a snapshot of the `open` surface into the repository. The build fails when a change breaks a supported release: a method removed, a type changed, a field removed, a union case the old client does not know. An added field needs a default. The compile error is not a ban on breaking; it is the list of who breaks.
  - A breaking change stays possible while an old release is supported, through a shim that translates the old shape (`was`). The error names the missing shim, and once the old release is dropped the compiler flags the shim as dead.
  - `caller.Version` is there for changes of meaning with the same shape. The compiler sees shape only.
  - Only the handshake is frozen forever: connecting, `Outdated`, and where the update is.
  - A device that gets `Outdated` updates itself over the air through code of its runtime (section 2), which every build for a device carries where the device's target has an update, which a build for AVR has not (below, decided there) (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "The runtime's", against a library, which only the devices that need it carry, at the cost that a device that gets `Outdated` must link that library to update itself; the cost is the update code in every build for a device). On the ESP32 models, whose framework gives the two slots and the going back, the runtime fetches the release from where the handshake says the update is, writes it into a second slot beside the release it runs, which goes on running, checks its signature, switches to it at the restart and goes back to the release it ran by itself where the new one does not start, a release counting as started where it marks itself valid after its start has reached the handshake, and a restart before that mark, a crash or a loss of power in its first run among them, starting the release it ran, as ESP-IDF's rollback does (checked 2026-10-21: ESP-IDF's documentation of over-the-air updates, under which a reset that comes before the application marked itself valid boots the application before it) (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "At the server handshake", against the end of its own start, before any connection, at the cost that a release that cannot connect stays installed and cannot fetch its fix; the cost is that a server out of reach at a release's first start sends a good release back), so that a device that loses power during the write keeps the release it ran (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "Two slots", against one slot and a small loader that writes in place, at the cost that power lost during the write leaves a device only the loader can save; the cost is the flash for two program images); the update is the runtime's on the targets whose base gives two slots, the ESP32 models, and a build for AVR, whose flash has no second slot the chip switches to, has no update (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "No update on AVR", against a loader in AVR's boot section that copies the new release over the running one at the restart, at the cost of that loader and of no copy to go back to, so that a device on AVR could not go back by itself; the cost is no update over the network on AVR); a release is signed by a release key of the project, whose public half is compiled into every build for a device that has an update (above), which checks each release with it (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "Project release key", against no key of its own and trust in the TLS connection to the server the handshake names, at the cost that whoever takes over that server can push any release to every device; the cost is that the project keeps the private half secret, and that losing it strands every device).
  - The wire format at the boundary carries field numbers, which is slower than inside the cluster.
- **`secret`.** A secret never crosses the boundary in the clear, in either direction, and is never logged.
  - A `secret` field in a normal type is stripped when the value crosses and does not exist on the other side: code there that touches it is a compile error. No separate type per side is needed. A secret lives on the side whose code touches it; both sides touching it is a compile error.
  - A whole `secret data` type cannot cross at all; trying is a compile error. A private key marked this way provably never leaves the client.
  - The only way across is `Sealed<T>`, in the box: the value is encrypted on the sending client for named recipients, and the server stores and routes bytes it cannot open. This is what end-to-end encrypted apps build on.
  - Key exchange, several devices per user and groups are a first-party package. The cipher primitives come through the C-ABI, as with TLS; where they come from in the long run is open (section 14).
  - Limits: the server cannot validate, search or index sealed content, so constraints run on the receiving client after opening. In a browser the server delivers the client code, so a compromised server can ship code that leaks keys; a native app and a flashed device do not have that hole. Who talks to whom, when and how much stays visible.
- Limits per client connection (message size, messages in flight, rate) are on by default and set in the `deploy` block; a client that exceeds them is disconnected. *(assumed)*
- Transport is the runtime's job: WebSocket for browsers, a TLS socket for devices, the Kurz binary format on both. The UI toolkit in the browser is a package, like graphics. *(assumed)*

```
// project.kz
deploy server { }
deploy web {
    target wasm
    connect "wss://shop.example.com"
}
deploy sensor {
    target esp32
    connect "tls://iot.example.com"
    inbox 32 drop oldest
}
```

```
data Item(int ProductId, int Count where 1..99)
data User(int Id, string Name, secret string PasswordHash)

actor Catalog per cluster {
    open List<Product> Search(string text) => products.Where(p => p.Name.Contains(text))
    pub void Reindex() { ... }                   // inside only
}

actor Cart per caller {                          // server side, one per signed-in caller
    mut items = List<Item>()
    open void Add(Item item) { items.Add(item) }
}

actor CartView on web {                          // runs in the browser
    void AddClicked(int productId) {
        Cart.Add(Item(productId, 1)) else { Offline => ShowBanner("offline") }
    }
}

actor Thermometer per caller {                   // server-side twin of one device
    mut last = Reading(0)
    open void Report(Reading r) { last = r }
    pub Reading Last() => last
}

actor Probe on sensor {                          // runs on the ESP32
    every 10s { send Thermometer.Report(ReadAdc()) }
}

temp = Thermometer[12].Last()                    // the backend asks the twin, never the device
```

```
actor Transfer per connection {                  // the client's own actor: it may wait on that client
    open FileId Upload(string name, Stream<Bytes> content) {
        file = Store.Create(name)
        for chunk in content { file.Append(chunk) }
        return file.Close()
    }
    open Stream<Bytes> Download(FileId id) => Store.Open(id).Chunks(256kb)
}

id = Transfer.Upload(picked.Name, picked.Chunks(256kb))            // client
for chunk in Transfer.Download(id) { save.Append(chunk) }
```

```
// project.kz
deploy web { supports current }     // an old tab gets Outdated and reloads itself
deploy app { supports 2.. }         // kept compatible, checked by the compiler
```

```
data User(int Id, string FirstName, string LastName)

was 1.4 User(int Id, string Name) {
    up   => User(Id, Name.Before(" "), Name.After(" "))     // arriving from a 1.4 client
    down => User(Id, "{FirstName} {LastName}")              // leaving to a 1.4 client
}
```

```
secret data PrivateKey(Bytes Value)                        // client only: sending it does not compile
secret data Message(string Text)

box = Sealed<Message>.For(bob.PublicKey, Message("hi"))    // client A
send Chat[room].Post(box)                                  // the server cannot open it
msg = box.Open(myKey) else { Forged => return }            // client B
```

### Speed

Runtime speed has priority everywhere; the compiler may be heavy. An optimisation the model allows: an actor handles one message at a time, so what was allocated while handling it and is not reachable from the actor's state at the end can be freed in one sweep at the end: what the message let out, in a `send`, a reply or a `spawn`, by `copy`, by `move` or with no word, as the values section 6 lets cross so (section 6, decided there), was copied into the receiver's heap or moved outside every actor heap and shared by pointer when it crossed (section 6, decided there as to the copy and the share; the move out of the heap at the share is decided by the answer below for a value built during the message and an assumption of this sentence for a value the state held, which the marker below names, and a value built in the heap during the message that leaves it at a `move` is allocated outside the heap from the start where the compiler sees at the allocation that the value crosses, an escape analysis, and is copied with its graph at the crossing where the compiler cannot see that (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "Escape analysis", against a copy of the graph at every `move`, at the cost of the copy `move` is meant to spare; the cost is that analysis in the compiler and a copy where it cannot see)), so nothing the sweep frees is needed by the receiver, the original of a `copy` among what it frees; the allocator tells what the state reaches from what it does not by a walk from the state at the end of the message (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "Walk from the state", sound and simple, against a mark at each store, at the cost that every heap store has to know whether its target is reachable from the state and that garbage the state let go of within the same message stays; the cost is a walk of the whole state on every message, so that an actor holding a large tree pays for it) *(assumed: the sweep, a reading of the actor model's one message at a time, proposed on 2026-10-01, the values it spares and the move out of the heap at a share of a value the state held, proposed with this record, none of them put to the owner as a question of its own, while round 17 decided how the sweep walks (above))*. The gate of a value tree below measures a tree built and changed within one message, a value of that message's heap, and a tree held in an actor's state pays that walk and is not measured by that gate, since the gate is a benchmark of computation and the answer "Walk from the state" names the cost of a tree in the state (the owner, 2026-10-21, round 17, "Gate a tree in one message", against a walk of only the values the message wrote, at the cost of a mark at each store, the option that answer set aside, and against a gate that measures a tree in the state as well, with a bound of its own set at its first measurement, at the cost of a second, looser number; the cost is that an actor holding a large tree pays for it, unmeasured). Beating hand-written C in some scenarios is welcome.

Goal (the owner, 2026-10-01): Kurz should not be meaningfully slower than C++ in computation and memory work, or than ASP.NET Core in web serving. This is a goal with gates, not a guarantee for every program. Safe code pays for the checks the compiler cannot remove: the index check, the counter check before a write, counter updates on shared values and the liveness check on a `weak` edge. C++ pays none of them and proves nothing. Where a measured hot path needs it, a `raw` block removes them (section 3, decided there). A call pays nothing for the line of a crash, which the line tables of the build carry, in the executable on Linux and in the PDB beside it on Windows; the runtime walks for the raising frame's line and reads the tables one time at a fault's or a primitive's crash, a raise by code carries its line as a constant, reads nothing and stores its context, a few registers, on the crash path, and the trace's walk and reads are done when it is written, never per call (section 6, decided there, with the assumptions its markers name), as a Rust or C program pays nothing per call for its debug information *(assumed: the comparison with Rust and C, proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*; each `print` pays one system call (section 6, where the call is an assumption), each call into a runtime primitive but the switch, and each call into the allocator's entry or the scheduler, from an actor's code or from a primitive, one compare against the actor's stack limit, or against the empty limit of a thread with no actor, in the runtime's record of the thread, with the loads that find it on Windows, so that a call into a primitive that calls the allocator's entry pays two in all, one at each entry, and three where it gives a block back after its change (section 6, an assumption there), each entry into the package of the allocator and the scheduler and each exit from it an update of the count per thread (section 6, decided there by the answer "End the process", whose cost named the count, updated on each entry and saved at each switch), and each switch to an actor's stack and each switch back the scheduler's store of the limit, two per round trip, the switch's saving, clearing and restoring of that count, two loads and three stores per round trip (section 6, decided there as to the saving at each switch, by the same answer), and on Windows the four stores into the thread environment block besides, eight of those per round trip, so that a round trip stores five times on Linux and thirteen times on Windows (section 2, decided there as to the fourth store, an assumption there in the rest), while on WebAssembly, where every actor runs on the one stack, a round trip has no switch and no limit, and on the ESP32 models the switch sets the watchpoint besides (section 6), a store per switch; C++ pays no such call per line where its stream is fully buffered, as it is on a file or a pipe written with a newline and not with `std::endl` (the C standard's rule for `stdout`: fully buffered where it is not an interactive device; on a terminal the C library of the POSIX systems line-buffers it, and Windows's C runtime writes on every call and buffers no console line; `std::endl` flushes, and `std::cout` passes through `stdout`'s buffer while it is synchronised with it, as it is unless the program turns that off; checked 2026-10-20: the C standard's paragraph on the buffering of the standard streams as opened, the GNU C library manual's chapter on stream buffering, Microsoft's documentation of `setvbuf`, under which a stream on a character device is flushed after each library call, and the C++ standard's `std::endl` and `std::ios_base::sync_with_stdio`); on a terminal it pays the same call per line, or more. The gates measure the release build (section 2, where `--release` is decided): the overflow checks of a test build (section 4, decided there) are a cost of that build and not of safe code *(assumed: which build the gates measure, the Windows loads and the system call per `print` as costs of section 6's assumptions, and the comparison with C++'s buffered stream, proposed with this record and not put to the owner)*. The numbers that turn a gate red (the owner, 2026-10-02, accepted for now): a value tree within 1.1x of C++ `std::map`, for a tree built and changed within one message (above, decided there), HTTP serving within 1.1x of ASP.NET Core, and an idle actor at most 512 bytes. Each number is a ratchet from its first measurement: it may only get tighter, once the evaluation that section 14 keeps open has confirmed it; until then a measurement may still move it. The owner wants the numbers looked at again against measurements (section 14).

The sweep runs at the end of a message after which what the actor allocated passes a threshold, so that its walk is paid for an amount allocated and not for each message, as BEAM collects a process's heap when it fills (the owner, 2026-10-30, "After a threshold", against the sweep after every message, at the cost of a walk that follows the size of the state on every message, so that an actor that holds a gigabyte as a cache walks it for every lookup, and against an actor that opts out of the sweep and relies on the counts of sections 3 and 4, at the cost of garbage that no count covers; the cost is garbage that stays a little longer, and a counter for each message). The answer decides when the sweep runs; the sweep itself stays the assumption the marker above names. The threshold follows the live size, as Go's collector paces itself with `GOGC=100`: the sweep runs at the end of a message after which what the actor allocated since the last sweep is at least what was live after that sweep, and at least 64 KiB *(assumed: the floor, proposed with this record and named by no question)*, the factor 2 between the two, and a setting of the deployment changes the factor (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Live size x2, as Go", against a fixed 1 MiB that a deployment sets, at the cost that a cache of 1 GB is walked for every MiB allocated, and against a knob on each actor, at the cost of knobs tuned by guess; the cost is memory up to twice the live size between two sweeps) *(assumed: that the setting stands in the deployment's block of `project.kz`, as the default of the inbox does)*.

The blocks that the escape analysis above proves never reach the actor's state or a crossing live in a region of their message, each taken by a bump of a pointer, all freed by one reset at the end of the message (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Region per message", against no region, at the cost of an allocator call, a free and a change of count for each block, a tree freed node by node, and against a region for each block of code, as Goose frees at the exit of a scope, at the cost of an analysis for each block and a rule for a list that grows inside a region; the cost is memory held to the end of the message, so that a long loop in one message grows its region, and the escape analysis this section accepts for `move`). A block that holds a counted pointer, to a shared value or to a block outside the region, takes the allocator and not the region, so that the reset leaves no count to lower (the owner, 2026-10-30, "No counted pointers", against a list in the region of the counted pointers stored in it, lowered at the reset, as bumpalo's `Box` runs a drop, at the cost of a store for each count taken inside the region, and against a walk of the region's blocks at the reset, as Rust's `typed_arena` runs the drops of its items when it is dropped, at the cost of a walk for each message, which loses the bump's advantage; the cost is fewer blocks in the region, and nothing at run time). The owner's "nothing at run time" means no work beyond what a block pays with no region, which a block kept out of the region pays: an allocator call, a free and a change of count *(assumed: a reading of the answer's words, not put to the owner)*.

The standard library has a packed list: grown at its end, its items back to back, the strings and lists inside an item inline, a union item at the size of its own case, read in order, a read by position walking to its item, as Goose lays out its arrays and its enums, while `List<T>` and its index stay as they are (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Packed list in std", against a fixed size for each item, a block for each string or list inside it and a union item at the room of its largest case, at the cost of a block for each string or list, and against short strings inline in every `string`, as C++'s `std::string` keeps them, at the cost of a branch at every access of a string and a second layout everywhere; the cost is a second, packed layout in the compiler of each type such a list holds, no index in constant time, a change of one item that rewrites the rest, and a second collection for the developer to choose). Whether an item can be removed from a packed list or inserted before its end, each rewriting the items after it, stands open (section 14).

Beside LLVM, the compiler removes index checks with a pass of its own that carries what it knows of lengths across calls, with index types, and with a marker that fails the build of a function where a check remains, as Goose's `checkfree fn` (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Goose's full set", against LLVM's removal alone and a `raw` block for a measured hot path, at the cost of the checks LLVM keeps and of the guarantees `raw` gives up, and against a length assertion in safe code that raises when false and gives its fact to the optimiser, at the cost of one more form of statement; the cost is a large analysis in the compiler, and new types and a marker for the developer).

- What the language reference could not take from this record. - The naming of the standard library, beyond the calls on files and sockets and the hashes, which section 12 names, and a map's members, which section 4 names.
- The form of the line of `project.kz` that silences a lint for its package (section 12): a table of lint ids with a level, as Cargo's `[lints]`, or a list of the ids it silences; while it stands open, nothing silences a lint.
- Whether a `mut` method other than `Add` takes `Add`'s exception for an argument that reads its receiver: the owner's answer of 2026-10-25 on `rows.Add(rows[0])` (section 4) covers `Add`, which stores its item, counted, before the list grows, so that a read of the receiver in its argument holds nothing the change meets, unless a lambda made it that is the argument's value or that a value inside the argument keeps, a value of a type that can hold a function value, which section 4 refuses. The options: the exception for every `mut` method that stores what its argument gave before it changes its receiver, as `Add` does, or for `Add` alone. Rust refuses, for any method, a borrow that the argument still holds while the method runs, and takes a read that made a new value first: `v.push(v.len())`, `v.insert(0, v.len())` and `rows.push(rows[0].clone())` compile, and `v.extend_from_slice(&v)` is E0502 (checked with rustc 1.82 on 2026-10-28). While it is open, the general rule of section 4 (the owner, 2026-10-24) holds for such a method as for any call that takes its receiver as `mut`, with every condition it states. The item asks only whether to widen `Add`'s exception.
- What a check of the runtime's own code ends in a test build on WebAssembly, the ESP32 models and AVR: round 20's answer "End the process" stands in the bullet of section 6 on a stack overflow, which holds on Linux and on Windows alone, by the mechanism that bullet gives them.
- What a check that fires inside the allocator or the scheduler, the standard library's Kurz code above the runtime's primitives (section 6), does in a test build, where it can come half-way through a change of their structures: round 20's answer "End the process" covers a check of the primitives, and the end of the process inside the allocator and the scheduler covers a stack fault.
- How the blocks of more than 256 KiB that a thread keeps after their free (section 2, "Keep them, as glibc") stand to the actors' heaps, from which the runtime's string and list primitives take their memory and which are freed with their actor (sections 2 and 3): whether such a block belongs to the heap of the actor that freed it or to the thread, and what the free of a whole heap does with the blocks it would keep.

- Where the cipher primitives come from in the long run, TLS's (section 2) and `Sealed<T>`'s (section 13) alike.
- A formatting of a text that can be chosen, which the owner's answer on the text of a duration (section 8, 2026-10-10) presumes: how it is written and what it can choose, for a duration; section 12 answers it for a time (the owner, 2026-10-30, "C# patterns + RFCs").
- Whether a block of C declarations (section 13) declares a C library's global variables beside its functions, as an external block of Rust declares statics, and how a read of one stands in a `raw` block.

- How long a durable actor's log keeps the ids of the sends it took, the window of section 7's delivery.
- How the `upgrade` lines of a durable actor (section 7) meet its log, which reaches the new cluster whole: whether the new code replays the old log or starts from the fields its `upgrade` lines keep.
- Whether `kurz exec` keeps the query engine's results on disk, as a build does since 2026-10-30 (section 2), or keeps nothing between two runs, as section 2 assumes for it.
- How the call id of a `retry`'s attempts (section 6) tells apart two calls of one chain to the same callee, which the chain ID alone does not, and how long a callee keeps a finished call's id and its reply.
- Whether the policy of the network split that runs an item on all sides with a `merge` block (section 7, its third rule, and section 13 for each key) still has a side to run on, where a side without a majority stops its members and refuses durable sends (section 7 and section 13, 2026-10-30).
- What a call on a socket gives when its time runs out (section 12, 2026-10-30, "5 s default, settable"), `WouldBlock`, as Rust's standard library gives it on Linux, or `TimedOut`; the member that sets the time, and whether it can set no bound, with the type of the time that the second form of `Connect` takes ("Second form, as Rust"), and what a time of zero and a negative time do in that form, refused with `InvalidInput`, as Rust's `connect_timeout` refuses a zero `Duration`, which holds no negative time ("It is an error to pass a zero `Duration` to this function", checked 2026-10-31 in its documentation), at the cost of an error where a time a program computes comes out at zero or below, or read as another bound, no bound or the 5 seconds of the form without a time, at the cost of a reading Rust does not give, which the documentation has to name; and how the bound holds for an actor's socket, which runs without blocking under `epoll` (section 12, "Readiness I/O"), where `SO_RCVTIMEO` and `SO_SNDTIMEO` bound no call. The options for the member: Rust's `set_read_timeout` and `set_write_timeout`, which take a duration or none, or C#'s `ReceiveTimeout` and `SendTimeout`, in milliseconds, where 0 is none; for an actor's socket: a timer in the poller's wait that wakes the actor at the bound, as Tokio's `timeout` wraps a read, or a deadline the scheduler reads at each switch.
- Whether the compile error for a class whose `equal by` names a `mut` field as the key type of a `Map` (section 4, 2026-10-30) reaches a key type that holds such a class, a `data` type with a field of it among them, and a key type that a type parameter stands for.
- Whether `args = value` among the top-level statements assigns to the program's `args` (section 12), a variable without `mut`, and is `assign-immutable`, as `name = expression` assigns where the name is visible (section 8), or declares one and is `redeclared`, as a top-level `args` of the program's own is under the owner's "Top-level code only" (section 12).
- Whether the other operand of `==` gives a list literal its type (section 4): `xs == [1]` with a `List<long>` `xs` reads `[1]` as a `List<long>`, as a number literal takes the type of the other operand (section 4) and as Rust infers `vec![1]` beside a `Vec<i64>`, or as a `List<int>`, from its items, so that the comparison is `type-mismatch`, as C# 12 gives a collection expression no type there.
- The type of what `Directory.List` gives (section 12, 2026-10-30, "Edge cases only"): the name and the fields of the case that carries the bytes of a name that is not UTF-8, and where that case stands in the result. The options: a union of `string` and a case such as `RawName(Bytes Name)`, or a `data` type for each entry that holds its name as a `string?` and its bytes.
- The `raw` member that copies a string into a block that ends in a NUL byte (section 12, 2026-10-30): its name, what it does with a string that holds a NUL byte, and what frees the block.
- Where the trace's walk runs for a crash that escalates to the root after the frames of the actor that raised were let go of on the fault stack (section 6), since the root actor's own crash walks on the stack that raised: written at the raise for every crash that may escalate, as Rust's panic hook captures a backtrace on the panicking thread, at the cost of a walk at each such raise with the overflow risk near the stack's end; or on the fault stack, as the walk over the abandoned frames runs, at the cost of a switch of stacks and a trace that may name frames already let go of (the owner, 2026-10-30, "Leave it open").
- Whether a `null` arm over a subject of a type parameter builds (section 4): as C# builds `case null` on a `T` without a constraint, the arm matching where a nullable type fills `T` (N11), at the cost of an arm whose reach each filling decides; or `type-mismatch`, as `null` where a `T` is required is, at the cost of generic code that cannot carry the arm.
- What a build does where the code of `runtime/common/` or of `std/` calls a function a target's runtime cannot give, as the code there calls the switch of stacks, which WebAssembly's runtime does not give (section 2): the check of the same functions (section 2, round 20) holds every target's directory to such a function, so that the directory of such a target fails it, and the owner's answer of 2026-10-30, "not supported", covers a call of the program that reaches such a function, not a call of the code of `runtime/common/` or of `std/`. The options: `not supported` at the construct of the program that reaches such a call, an actor for the switch of stacks; or the target's directory gives the function all the same, as the check of the same functions holds it to (section 2, round 20).
- What bounds the hazard a `Close` through one holder of a handle leaves (section 12): a call through another holder that read the descriptor before the store of -1 and enters the system after the close can reach a number the system has given another file since. The options: a close that waits for the calls in flight on the handle, as no clone of Rust's `Arc<File>` can close the file while another clone uses it, the file closing when the last clone is dropped; a lock around each call on the handle; or keeping the hazard, at the cost of a read or a write of a file the program never opened. Beside it: what an actor parked in the poller on a socket another holder closes does, which a close takes out of the `epoll` set where it closes the last descriptor of the socket's open file description (section 12): woken at the close with an error, as Go's poller wakes the calls that wait on a connection at its close ("Any blocked Read or Write operations will be unblocked and return errors", checked 2026-10-31 in the documentation of `net.Conn`'s `Close`), at the cost of a wake-up the close sends to every parked caller; or left parked, the bound of an actor's socket (above) deciding the wait, at the cost of a wait for nothing up to that bound.
- Whether a `data` type may declare a method named `Equals` or `GetHashCode`, which the answer "Error on Equals" (section 4, 2026-10-30) refuses on a class: the options are the same error, `equals-method`, at the method, or such a method as an ordinary member that changes nothing about `==`, as the record held it for a class before that answer.
- What a `static` field's initializer at its first read (section 4, 2026-10-30) does where its function reads, on a path of its calls, the field it initializes or another field whose initializer reads the first: a compile error where the whole program's calls lead from one initializer to the other, an error at run time at the read that enters an initializer still running, or the field read as its type's default while its initializer runs, as C# reads a static field during a cycle of its static constructors.
- Whether a `send` to an actor puts the sender's package on the path of calls that section 13 reads for a grant ("Every package on path"), so that a package does not reach `allow files` or `allow network` through an actor of another package that holds it.
- Whether the file functions of section 12 take a path given as bytes, for an entry of `Directory.List` whose name is not UTF-8, as Rust's `Path` holds an `OsStr`, or such an entry stays one that a program lists and cannot open, rename or delete.
- What a `raw` call of a C function that blocks does to the other actors of its thread, which it holds while it blocks, since the readiness I/O of section 12 covers the calls on files and sockets alone: the options are to leave it so, as a blocking call holds a thread of Tokio's runtime, a pool of threads for C calls a program marks as blocking, as the BEAM runs such native functions on its dirty schedulers, or a hand-off of the thread's other actors while the call blocks, as Go hands off the processor of a thread in a system call.
- Whether a program can save a value as its bytes and load it with one read and a check of its links, as Goose saves a value whose links are offsets (`knowledge/what-goose-does-for-speed.md`, G5).
- Where the exception that a `static` field's initializer threw (section 4, "Same failure, as C#") is kept for the rest of the process, since a class instance lives in one actor's heap and the actor whose read ran the initializer can end before the next read: the options are a copy in memory of the runtime outside every actor's heap, from which each read copies, at the cost of that memory for the rest of the process, or an initializer whose thrown value holds no class instance, as a value that crosses between actors (section 6), at the cost of a compile error for an initializer that can throw one.
- Whether a member that stops because its side of a split holds no majority (section 7, "Failures") ends the actors it runs, a child spawned by hand on that member (section 6) among them, or keeps them stopped until it joins again.
- How an operator takes members out of the log of the members on an exact tie of a network split (section 7, "Failures"), where no side can commit a change of that log: a change forced outside the log, as etcd's `--force-new-cluster` starts a cluster from one member, at the risk of two majorities where both sides force one, or another mechanism.
- Whether a message of a durable actor of three copies or more (section 13) counts as stored where all its copies took it, as the mode `durable 2` says of a copy on N machines, or where a majority of them took it, as a split reads it ("Majority side only") and as a Raft group commits an entry: all of them at the cost of writes refused while the machine of any copy is lost, a majority at the cost of a message held by fewer copies than N.
- Whether an item can be removed from the packed list of section 13 or inserted before its end, each rewriting the items after it as a change of one item does, or the list grows at its end alone.
- Whether a list literal takes the items of another list, C# 12's spread `[..xs, 3]` (section 4, the owner, 2026-10-30, "Leave it Open"): as C# 12, where the room for the items is the sum of the counts, known at run time alone, and `..` at the start of an item has a meaning beside the range of `xs[a..<b]`; or no spread, as Rust's `vec!` has none, a list joined by a member call. While it stands open, `[..xs]` is the error for text no rule gives a meaning.
- When an actor spawned by hand stops where its supervisor ends without a crash (section 6), which the answer "After current message" gave for a crash alone: after its current message, as on a crash and as Akka lets a stopping actor finish its current message, at the cost of up to one message's work after the supervisor ended; without finishing the message, as an Elixir supervisor's `:shutdown` exit ends a child that does not trap exits, at the cost of a call cut partway, the half-written state "After current message" set aside for a crash; or after its current message within a bound, as an Elixir supervisor waits a child that traps exits its `:shutdown` time, 5000 ms for a worker by default, and then kills it (checked 2026-10-31: the documentation of Elixir's `Supervisor`), at the cost of a timer for each stopping child and a call cut at the bound.
- How an actor on another machine reads a snapshot that one actor publishes (section 11, "Functions + snapshots"): by a message the publishing actor answers, at the cost of a round trip for each read, one queue for every remote reader, the shape that answer set aside, and the unreachable exception where that machine is cut off; or from a copy on its own machine, sent to every machine at each publish, at the cost of a send to every machine for each publish and a read of the version before while a publish is in transit.
- What the side that holds the cluster's majority does with a durable actor whose copies all stand outside it (section 13): it refuses the actor's `send`s while no member with a copy is back, each such member keeping its copies while it is out of the log of the members, as a Kafka partition whose replicas all died stays unavailable while they are down, under either setting of `unclean.leader.election.enable`, which picks the replica that leads when one comes back (checked 2026-10-31: the section "Unclean leader election: What if they all die?" of Kafka's design documentation), at the cost of the actor without durable writes for the whole split and of copies kept on a member outside the log; or it starts the actor with no state, the old copies dropped when their members join again, the loss of all the data that the same section names for a majority scheme whose majority failed for good, which Kafka itself does not take, at the cost of the actor's durable state lost at a split.
- What a nullable type written as a case of a union is, `int? | string` (section 4), which the build says `not supported` to: a union whose first case is `int?`, a success that is a nullable `int`, as Rust's `Result<Option<i32>, String>` nests one inside the other, at the cost of a union as a case of a union, a tag inside a tag; flattened to `int | null | string`, at the cost that the `null` stands after the first case, so that in a function's result `int? | NotFound` it reads as an outcome beside `NotFound` (section 5) and leaves the function where the call is not matched; or an error, so that a program writes `int | string | null` itself, at the cost of one more refusal and its text.
- How the time of `TcpStream.Connect(address, timeout)`, and the 5 seconds of the form without a time, meet the lookup of the name they take (section 12), which Rust's `connect_timeout` does not make: the bound covers the lookup too, at the cost of a lookup that does not hold the actor's thread, `getaddrinfo` on a thread of its own or a resolver in the runtime, since `getaddrinfo` takes no bound of its own; or the bound covers the connects alone, at the cost of a call that waits past its time on a slow name server.
- Whether a `null` arm over a subject of a number type, an `int`, builds (section 4): as over a `string`, an arm no value reaches, a departure from C#, which refuses `case null` on an `int` (CS0037), at the cost of a dead arm on a type that never holds `null`; or refused, as C# refuses it, at the cost of a second rule beside the `string`'s.

## 15. Prototype

A throwaway v0 exists outside this repository and was never committed. It was written before this design existed: a C# compiler (`Program.cs`) that emits C and builds through `zig cc`. Its syntax and semantics are not authoritative, and its backend contradicts section 2 (LLVM IR, not C). Only the name carries over.
